<meta>This page serves as the preface to the work. The original manuscript shows significant aging, including heavy foxing (brown spotting) and ink bleeding through from the back of the page, which occasionally obscures the text.</meta>

# Preface.

---

Every human being has their own <term>domain of faith<note>German: *Glaubensgebiet*. This refers to the personal sphere of spiritual or religious conviction held by an individual.</note></term>; these various domains partially overlap, while others diverge. But true knowledge is only one; is true faith not also only one? Many believe, rather, that it is right and proper for everyone to have their own particular faith.

The question arises above all: what is faith in relation to knowledge? For a part of the essence of both lies in their relationship to one another. Is it a relationship of siblings, friends, or enemies? Must knowledge be the servant of faith <note>The author is likely referencing the medieval concept of *philosophia ancilla theologiae*—the idea that philosophy and reason should serve religious doctrine.</note>, or must faith hide from knowledge like an owl before the light? Are they attached to one another like the two heads of <term>Janus<note>The Roman god of beginnings, transitions, and gates, traditionally depicted with two faces looking in opposite directions.</note></term>, so that each looks in a different direction, and each sees only what the other does not? Or can each, according to its different nature, see the same thing only differently than the other?

<summary>The author introduces a philosophical inquiry into the tension between subjective faith and objective knowledge, questioning whether the two are inherently at odds or simply different ways of perceiving the same reality.</summary>
<keywords>preface, faith vs. knowledge, Janus, epistemology, philosophy of religion, truth</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface from page III, moving from the philosophical tension between faith and knowledge to the specific purpose of the current work. It establishes the author's intent to bridge the gap between emotional impulse and rational justification in religion.</meta>

# Preface.

These are old questions, an old dispute that still awaits resolution today. Instead of taking it up again here, however, this little book takes a path away from the dispute—a path where there is no occasion for conflict, because the answer is found almost before the question. What is its other purpose?

As vast as the <term>domain of faith<note>German: *Glaubensgebiet*. This refers to the conceptual landscape or sphere in which religious or spiritual convictions exist.</note></term> and the territory of believers is, it is equally dark; many paths within it run partly together, and partly apart. Few ever think about why they believe what they believe; few consider what entitles them to believe what they believe; few truly strike upon what is right, and many even lose their faith entirely through reflection because they have not found what is right. This little book has grown out of reflection on what is right and what provides the guarantee of what is right in <term>matters of faith<note>German: *Glaubenssachen*. This term covers specific points of doctrine as well as subjective religious experiences.</note></term>. Its intention is to guide reflection back to the path that has appeared to us as the correct one—a path on which much more is gained than lost in terms of faith.

In short, I define the task of this booklet as follows: to show how faith grows out of its <term>motives<note>German: *Motive des Glaubens*. In this context, these are the initial psychological or spiritual impulses or "drivers" that lead toward belief before they are fully reasoned out.</note></term>, to allow the motives of faith to grow into <term>grounds<note>German: *Hauptgründe*. While "motives" are the inner impulses, "grounds" are the firm, rational foundations or justifications for belief.</note></term>, and thereby to allow faith itself to grow.

There are three main subjects of faith, and thus we will also find three main motives and, consequently, three main grounds for faith, which, however, do not

<summary>The author outlines the book's goal: to transform the initial impulses (motives) of faith into firm rational foundations (grounds). This process is intended to prevent the loss of faith that often occurs when believers begin to reflect critically on their convictions.</summary>
<keywords>preface, motives of faith, grounds of faith, religious reflection, methodology, epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface from page III and IV. The author is moving from the abstract necessity of faith toward the specific methodology of this book: creating a bridge between scholarly theology and simple piety.</meta>

...not separate according to objects, but rather to add a bond within the human being to the bond which these things possess in the nature of things.

I unite the commonality of motives and reasons under the name **Principles of Faith**, and thus I can also say that this work is concerned with the principles of faith.

We already have many <term>textbooks<note>German: *Lehrbücher*. These are academic or dogmatic manuals used in formal theological study.</note></term> of faith; however, they are simply too scholarly for most people. In them, we already find proofs for the existence of God, the hereafter, and angels; but they exist only for the scholars, whereas faith exists for everyone. Could the grounds of faith not also be made accessible and intuitive to all? There are many who wish to believe and yet simply cannot; will they be able to learn faith from such textbooks, or will they be able to build up what is missing by using <term>books of devotion<note>German: *Erbauungsbücher*. Popular religious literature intended to inspire piety and "edify" those who already believe, rather than provide logical arguments for the skeptical.</note></term> which already presuppose that faith is fully formed? In this little work, however, an attempt is made to teach faith and to build faith, without requiring either the scholarship of textbooks or the assumptions of devotional books.

Everything within it is so simple, clear, understandable, and—following from self-evident <term>premises<note>German: *Vordersätzen*. In logic, these are the starting propositions from which a conclusion is drawn.</note></term>—almost self-evident, that I admittedly fear people will find it "too much" so rather than too little, since one is usually accustomed to finding things so very different in these matters. And will even the <term>literalist<note>German: *Wortgläubige*. Literally "word-believer"; refers to those who adhere strictly to the literal text of scripture or dogma without deeper reflection.</note></term> be satisfied that I [treat] the most important words to which he...

<summary>The author explains his goal to create a "middle way" for religious writing that avoids the dry complexity of academic textbooks and the assumptions of devotional books. He aims to make the principles of faith accessible and self-evident to the common person.</summary>
<keywords>Principles of Faith, religious education, textbooks, devotional books, accessibility, literalism, logic, premises</keywords>

# Preface.

...[the believer satisfied when one] believes [him], truly taking him simply at his word; the **rational believer** <term><note>German: *Vernunftgläubige*. A term for those who sought to harmonize religious faith with Enlightenment rationalism.</note></term> by taking faith entirely according to reason; and the **unbeliever** by elevating his very reasons for unbelief into grounds for faith. This is necessary because the contradiction between these three—the sphere in which the **life of faith** moves—is rooted, perhaps more than in anything else, in the fact that they themselves fail to do justice to the very thing in which they seek their justification against one another.

Will the **progress** that faith dares to make here, moving beyond its previous standpoint, even be forgiven, since the most devout consider the firm **state of rest** <term><note>German: *Ruhestand*. In this context, it refers to a fixed, unchanging, or static state of dogma that resists new inquiry.</note></term> of faith to be essential to its very nature?

->Take comfort, then, little book of a small **community** <term><note>German: *Gemeine*. An older form of *Gemeinde*, referring to a congregation or a spiritual fellowship.</note></term> and of the tree that sleeps within the **acorn**. May you be a small leaf on the **seedling** of this tree.<-

<summary>The author concludes his preface by stating his goal to reconcile traditionalists, rationalists, and skeptics through a unified logic. He concludes with a poetic hope that his small work will eventually grow into a greater understanding of faith.</summary>

<keywords>rationalism, unbelief, justification, theological progress, metaphor of the acorn, community of faith</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the main text of the manuscript following the table of contents. It introduces the foundational definitions of "faith" and "knowledge" that will be used throughout the work. # I.
## Faith and Knowledge in General.
---

Like all general concepts, faith can be understood in various breadths and ways. Instead of arguing over how it should be understood, however, we will simply explain what we intend to understand and consider by this name here. It is enough if we remain within the limits of linguistic usage, and only fix these limits more firmly for the purposes of the following consideration than they are usually found in the fluctuations of common usage.

Perhaps it would be good to proceed in this manner more often than is commonly done.

<margin>]</margin>
In the broadest sense, then, it is generally understood—and I understand—by faith a <term>holding-to-be-true <note>Original German: *Fürwahrhalten*. This is a technical term in German philosophy (famously used by Immanuel Kant) to describe the subjective acceptance of a proposition as true, regardless of the objective certainty.</note></term> of that which is not certain through experience or logical deduction, to which mathematical deduction belongs.

Should I hereafter also explain what I mean by holding-to-be-true, experience, logical deduction, ma— <note>The text breaks off here at the start of the word "mathematical" (mathematischem), likely continuing on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this opening section, Fechner establishes a working definition of faith as a "holding-to-be-true" based on something other than direct experience or formal logic. He emphasizes the need for clear definitions to avoid the usual linguistic confusion surrounding these abstract concepts.</summary>

<keywords>faith, knowledge, definitions, logic, experience, holding-to-be-true, Gustav Theodor Fechner, epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 1, where Fechner began defining "faith" (Glaube) as a subjective "holding-to-be-true." It moves from linguistic definitions to a rejection of overly dense metaphysical abstractions in favor of a practical, broad understanding of belief that encompasses everything from religion to superstition.</meta>

## Faith and Knowledge in General.

thematic understanding? But one clearly sees that I would then never finish, nor arrive at the beginning of what is actually at stake here. Common usage and the context of the consideration must suffice to allow the <term>factual substance <note>Original German: *das Sächliche*. Fechner is emphasizing that he wants to focus on the actual "things" or "content" of the argument rather than getting lost in the technical definitions of words.</note></term> of what is meant to be understood; and it is only with this substance that we shall deal here.

If I indeed wanted to write a **metaphysics of faith**, I would have to go deeper into conceptual connections on all sides, return to the most abstract ideas, and proceed from the "ultimate" as if it were the "first." But where could that lead? Since it has seemed to me that all metaphysics, dogmatics, mysticism, and mythology of faith lead into the darkness rather than out of it, I shall refrain from entering into their depths. What we seek lies above these depths.

In that broadest sense in which we first grasped faith, its concept is indifferent to its content. One can believe that there is a God and that there is a devil; that there will be war or peace; that if thirteen sit at a table, one will die this year—indeed, what can one *not* believe? It is unbelievable what all is believed in the world; and for everything that is believed, the opposite is also believed. The crassest **superstition** is still faith in this broadest sense of the word.

<note>Fechner lists several disciplines he intends to avoid:
- **Metaphysics**: The branch of philosophy dealing with the first principles of things.
- **Dogmatics**: The study of established religious doctrines.
- **Mysticism**: Belief in union with or absorption into the Deity through contemplation.
- **Mythology**: The study of myths or a collection of myths (Fechner uses *Mythik*).
He suggests that these "deep" approaches often obscure the simple truth he is searching for.</note>

<summary>Fechner argues against a purely metaphysical approach to faith, stating that such "depths" often lead to darkness. He defines faith in its broadest sense as being independent of content, noting that it encompasses both religious belief and common superstition.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, dogmatics, mysticism, mythology, superstition, factual substance, definition of faith</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from a general linguistic definition of faith—which can include everything from facts to superstitions—toward the author's specific focus on religious faith. It establishes the distinction between faith as a general psychological mechanism and faith as a spiritual orientation toward the divine.</meta>

## Faith and Knowledge in General.

But in a narrower sense—for instance, when one groups together **faith, love, and hope**; points to faith as a person’s final refuge; speaks of salvation through faith, of matters of faith, of sources of faith, or of "believing" people—one understands, and I understand here, by "faith" only the faith in the highest and ultimate things: namely God, the <term>Beyond <note>Original German: *Jenseits*. A common term in 19th-century German philosophy and theology referring to the afterlife or the transcendent realm beyond the physical world.</note></term>, and higher spiritual existences. These reach furthest beyond everything that is certain in those other ways <note>Referring to the "ways" of knowledge or sensory experience discussed on previous pages.</note>, and for that reason, we speak here primarily of faith. Thus, faith in the narrower sense is merely the highest region of faith in the broader sense.

From time immemorial, nations have attached <term>conditions of salvation <note>Original German: *Heilsbedingungen*. This refers to the requirements or states of being necessary for spiritual well-being or "salvation" in a religious context.</note></term> to the existence of the objects of this faith and to the act of belief itself, seeking the highest and most universal bond in such a faith. In this respect, it is also called **religious faith**, to distinguish it from **common faith**.

It is this faith in the narrower sense that we shall primarily deal with here; yet, since it shares the most general relationships and characteristics of faith in the broadest sense—which are independent of the nature of what is believed—it will be necessary to take these into consideration first.

To some, it might certainly seem that faith in the highest and ultimate things is altogether—

<summary>Fechner distinguishes between 'faith' as a general psychological state and 'religious faith' specifically focused on God and the afterlife, noting that the latter is the highest form of the former. He argues that while his focus is on religious faith, one must first understand the general mechanics of how belief works regardless of the subject matter.</summary>

### Faith and Knowledge in General.

...[is] of a different nature, has entirely different sources and consequences than the belief in any other things—in that which is here and there, what will or will not be tomorrow or next year—and that it is something absolutely above or even contrary <note>Likely referring to the theological concepts of *supra rationem* (above reason) or *contra rationem* (against reason).</note> [to reason], something specific in every respect. And should one really be able to somehow group it together with superstition? —

But first and foremost, faith in the highest and ultimate things is a <term>holding-to-be-true <note>Original German: *Fürwahrhalten*. A technical philosophical term, notably used by Kant, to describe the subjective acceptance of a proposition as true when objective certainty (proof) is lacking.</note></term> of the **existence** and the **nature** of the things to which it relates, just like any other belief, without being able to be made certain through logic and experience. Thus, it conceptually has something in common with [other forms of belief], and it will be the task of the following sections to show that the "specificity" of faith in the highest and ultimate things lies precisely in the fact that these things *are* the highest and ultimate. They are, however, the highest and ultimate aspects of that in which we all "live and move" <note>An allusion to the biblical phrase from Acts 17:28: "For in him we live and move and have our being."</note>, and the motives and grounds of every other belief thus culminate most highly in relation to them, finding their most complete convergence and conclusion there.

No faith can become as firm as the faith in these things; no motives as resounding, no grounds as binding, and no consequences as far-reaching and profound. Why? Because the motives, grounds, and consequences of faith in the most universal, highest, and ultimate things bear the nature of their objects: to be the most universal, highest, and ultimate, from which, however...

<summary>This page argues that religious faith, while sharing the same psychological mechanism as everyday belief (the act of "holding-to-be-true"), is distinguished by the absolute and ultimate nature of its objects, which provides it with unparalleled certainty and influence.</summary>
<keywords>religious faith, epistemology, Fürwahrhalten, ultimate things, motives of belief, superstition</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page, completing a sentence that describes religious truths as the ultimate points of convergence for all human belief and activity.</meta>

### Faith and Knowledge in General.

be, in which our whole life, <term>creative striving <note>Original: *Dichten und Trachten*. A common German idiom, rooted in the Lutheran Bible (Genesis 8:21), referring to the totality of human thoughts, intentions, and imaginative life.</note></term>, and aspiration rests and moves.

Faith is like a high pyramid. The motives and grounds of all faith converge from a broad base into religious faith as if into a final, governing peak; and he errs just as much who seeks the peak of the pyramid as something separate above the pyramid, as he who does not lift his gaze above the base; and he errs by half who only lifts it halfway.

One can believe **that** something is, and believe **that** something can be relied upon; in the latter case, faith is called **trust**. This is often the meaning of faith in the Bible. However, the one is rooted in the other form of belief, for how could one believe that something is reliable if one did not also believe that it exists. Thus the Bible says:

> “Without faith it is impossible to please God, for whoever would come to God must believe that he exists and that he will be a rewarder of those who seek him” (Hebrews 11:6).

Admittedly, faith in the first sense <note>Believing "that" something exists.</note> still requires an increase in <term>determining grounds <note>Original: *Bestimmungsgründe*. A philosophical term referring to the underlying motives or reasons that lead the will or the intellect to a specific conclusion or action.</note></term> in order to become the latter <note>Belief as trust.</note>; but these grow naturally out of true faith in what is right. Thus, it will not be necessary to separate the consideration in this regard.

What then is **knowledge** in contrast to faith? Here too, it is important to distinguish between a narrower and a broader meaning. What is known in the narrowest and

### Faith and Knowledge in General.

in the strictest sense—and thus that which is absolutely or objectively certain—is, in contrast to what is believed, only that which is impossible to imagine or think of otherwise according to the <term>principle of identity <note>Original: *Satze der Identität*. A fundamental law of logic stating that a thing is identical to itself (A=A). The author uses it here to mean something that cannot be thought of in any other way without contradiction.</note></term>; that is, everything recognized through immediate experience, valid logical inference, or the latter based on the former.

* In this sense of knowledge, I know that a sensation of red, green, or yellow exists in the world if I have it myself; there is nothing to dispute there; what is there, is there. I know that every triangle includes a total of two right angles; for I cannot think of it otherwise without falling into contradiction with the previously conceived conditions of the triangle; for the same reason, in the case of a sphere whose diameter I know from experience, I know its circumference and its volume. Whether others see the orange lying before me as just as yellow as I do, however, I cannot strictly speaking know; I only believe it almost as firmly as if I knew it. And as long as I have not personally perceived the validity of a logical inference—having recognized for myself the impossibility of thinking otherwise according to the <term>requirements of conceptual agreement <note>Original: *Foderungen begrifflicher Einstimmung*. This refers to the logical necessity where concepts must align perfectly for a conclusion to be considered "known" rather than just "believed."</note></term>—I can certainly believe, but not know, that it is valid and that what is inferred from it is correct.

Of that which we certainly know—besides the indeed very extensive realm of mathematical truth—

<summary>The author defines strict "knowledge" as objective certainty derived from direct experience or logical necessity, such as mathematics. He distinguishes this from "belief," noting that even common assumptions about how others perceive the world are technically matters of faith rather than absolute knowledge.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the limits of knowledge from page 6. The OCR begins mid-word with the suffix "-ten," likely completing the phrase "we know" (wissen). There is a handwritten checkmark in the right margin next to the discussion of mathematical truth.</meta>

### Faith and Knowledge in General.

...we actually know very little at the core; and regarding mathematical truths, it must furthermore be considered, partly that they are only a matter of strict knowledge for a few, while for others they are more or less only a matter of faith; and partly that they say nothing about existence itself, but only say: if this is so, then that is so. Mathematics cannot prove that a space of three dimensions exists in which lines can be drawn or boundaries can be established, nor that triangles or circles exist; rather, it can only prove that, insofar as space, triangles, and circles exist in the sense of their definitions, this or that follows from what is given. For everything that happened before us, will happen after us, or happens and exists far from us, the certainty of knowledge in that strictest sense is lacking; according to its established definition, in the realm of the <term>experiencable <note>Original: *Erfahrbaren*. Refers to anything that can be perceived through the senses or tested by observation.</note></term>, it does not extend beyond what is immediately experienced and its logically analyzable, combinable, and developable content.

In the meantime, there are principles for the generalization of experience—laws that are themselves only gained through the generalization of empirical data, and which are confirmed all the more in experience the longer, further, and more thoroughly we pursue them. These principles and laws, and what follows from them, are also usually counted as part of the domain of knowledge in a broader sense, and we may include them as such in the following. The entire

<summary>The author argues that mathematical certainty is conditional and that strict knowledge is limited to immediate experience, though we may broaden the definition of "knowledge" to include general scientific laws confirmed by experience.</summary>
<keywords>mathematics, three-dimensional space, empirical knowledge, logic, generalization, scientific laws, existence</keywords>

### Faith and Knowledge in General.

**Natural science** aims for the production of such knowledge, although the knowledge produced in this way ultimately always depends on the **faith** that the generalization on which we build, and the conclusions drawn from it, will find further confirmation—just as they have been confirmed thus far within the realm of the <term>empirical <note>Original: *Erfahrungsmäßigen*. This refers to the "world of experience," or things that can be tested and observed through the senses.</note></term>. For neither through logic nor through experience can it be proven that such confirmation *must* occur in the future.

Who can say that it has been proven, or is even provable, through experience or mathematics—or both combined—that the **law of gravitation** holds through all of space and will hold through all of time? Yet it has proven itself valid as far and as long as we have been able to track it through the heavens and through the ages. This establishes a faith that is almost equal in firmness to the strictest knowledge: the belief that it will continue to hold true. Because of this, we count it as a matter of our knowledge—indeed, as a matter of strict, <term>exact knowledge <note>In the 19th-century context, "exact" science usually referred to fields like physics or astronomy that relied on precise mathematical measurement.</note></term>.

In most of what is called "knowledge," faith nevertheless enters conditionally, insofar as that knowledge relies on the assumption of something believed. Thus, all our **historical knowledge** presupposes faith in the credibility of the sources; our entire empirical science assumes the faith that others have observed correctly and have reported only what they truly saw; our entire psychology, insofar as it is not merely that of a single in- <note>The text cuts off here mid-word; it is likely the beginning of *Individuums* (individual).</note>

<summary>The author argues that even "exact" scientific knowledge, like the law of gravity, is ultimately rooted in faith—specifically the belief that nature will continue to behave consistently. He suggests that most fields of study, including history and psychology, rely on a necessary trust in the observations and records of others.</summary>

### Faith and Knowledge in General.

...existence as an individual, it also presupposes the faith in the souls of other people. And what would remain of all our **science** and scholarship if all this faith fell away?

Therefore, the man of knowledge should not despise faith too much. Faith has a share in all of his knowledge; withdraw it from him, and knowledge itself collapses. <margin>||</margin> His task as a man of knowledge may be not to banish faith, but to replace it as far as possible with knowledge. As far as possible. And if knowledge is not sufficient to replace a faith, the question may be whether that faith is replaceable at all or not; accordingly, the task may be to overthrow it from the side of knowledge, or—if not to provide a foundation for it—at least to support it.

After all this, is faith really nothing more than an imperfect knowledge?

But that would be to poorly recognize the essence of faith. Rather, just as what we believe about a matter always reaches beyond what we know of it, the <term>determining grounds <note>Original: *Bestimmungsgründe*. This refers to the underlying motives, reasons, or evidence that lead a person to reach a conclusion or hold a belief.</note></term> of faith generally also overreach those of knowledge. The inadequacy of the latter, which occurs with every act of faith, can be supplemented by other determining grounds. This occurs to such an extent that a <term>taking-to-be-true <note>Original: *Fürwahrhalten*. A technical term in German philosophy (often associated with Kant) describing the mental state of accepting a proposition as true, whether based on subjective or objective reasons.</note></term> of the matter is achieved, which in its firmness is often not inferior to that which rests on grounds of knowledge, and [reaches] an **objective certainty**...

## Faith and Knowledge in General.

...[but] looks more at what lies behind the immediate than at the immediate itself, and the one-sided observer sees it from only one side.

But before we attempt to show the immediate before the distant, and the immediate from all sides, we encounter a second error after the first—one as consequential as the first can be. Through this, one of the three sides <note>The author uses "Seiten" (sides/aspects), implying that any subject must be viewed from multiple perspectives to be understood fully.</note> that we will examine more closely in the following pages reveals itself.

<margin>O</margin>
Just as it would be erroneous to want to make faith dependent solely on grounds of knowledge, and to see in faith nothing but an imperfect knowledge, so too would it be erroneous to entirely exclude the grounds of knowledge from the <term>determining grounds: "Bestimmungsgründe," the underlying reasons or motives that lead a person to a specific conclusion or action</term> of faith, and generally to see in the one something merely external to the other. We found that something of faith enters into most of our knowledge; conversely, what we know about a matter can play a very important role in our faith. Indeed, knowledge has an influence everywhere—though often a hidden one—and in conflict with other determining grounds of faith, it carries a weight that may, depending on the circumstances, outweigh or be outweighed by other factors.

<margin>|</margin>

# Faith and Knowledge in General.

<margin>|</margin> <note>This text continues a thought from the previous page, likely regarding the sun.</note> it has risen today, yesterday, the day before yesterday—every day since human memory. Such an <term>induction: "Induction," the logical process of drawing a general conclusion from a set of specific observations</term> can never be complete; but the inherently incomplete knowledge that rests upon it supplements itself, according to a psychological law, into a belief that is all the more certain and firm the less supplementation it requires.

What makes us believe in a soul in all other people, besides our own soul—the only one of which we truly have knowledge? It is because we see that their bodies and physical expressions resemble our own, which we know to be bound to spirit and mental activity. This <term>argument from analogy: "Analogieschluß," a conclusion based on the similarity between two things</term> can no more establish knowledge in the strictest sense than that <term>inductive inference: "Inductionschluß," reasoning from specific instances to a general rule</term>; but what is lacking there is again supplemented, as if by itself, into faith.

The faith in the highest and last things <note>The "highest and last things" usually refers to theological or metaphysical concepts such as God, the afterlife, or the ultimate purpose of the universe.</note>, of course, is not of such a kind that it would only require that weak subjective supplementation of the <term>grounds of knowledge: "Wissensgründe," the intellectual or factual evidence for a belief</term>, where we might neglect the gap and count what is believed as knowledge itself. The grounds of knowledge reach much less far here; the other grounds of faith gain the upper hand by far; yet they, for their part, would not suffice without the service of the grounds of knowledge and never come into their own without them.

Therefore, even the person of faith should not despise knowledge too much. Without it, their faith would become an empty phrase or a bubble.

<summary>This page argues that faith is not the opposite of knowledge but rather its completion; even simple beliefs about the world and other people rely on logical processes like induction and analogy to bridge the gap between observation and certainty.</summary>

<keywords>induction, analogy, soul, grounds of knowledge, psychology of belief, faith vs knowledge</keywords>

# Faith and Knowledge in General.

Ultimately, it is not the total absence of <term>grounds of knowledge: "Wissensgründe," the intellectual evidence or facts that support a conclusion</term>, but rather their insufficiency for reaching perfect knowledge or objective certainty in the sense described above, and the supplementation of this deficiency through psychological or other grounds, that characterizes faith. We should only speak of a lack of intellectual evidence for faith in a relative sense—specifically, when such evidence, as is often the case, cannot compete with the other reasons for believing.

Since the <term>determining factors: "Bestimmungsgründe," the underlying reasons or motives that lead to a specific mental state or conclusion</term> of knowledge are included within those of faith, yet faith possesses further factors beyond them; since the entire realm of faith is much larger than that of knowledge, and its influence on life, feeling, and thought surpasses that of knowledge; and since only faith, and not knowledge, extends to the highest and last things <note>The "highest and last things" refers to ultimate metaphysical questions like God, the soul, and the purpose of existence.</note>—one could find reason enough to view knowledge as an imperfect form of faith rather than the other way around. One might even assign faith a higher position than knowledge, if it were not for the fact that any dispute over the priority of things that only exist with and through one another is ultimately idle and a misunderstanding of their true relationship. As much as faith towers over knowledge, if you take away all knowledge from it, you are left with nothing but pure <term>superstition: "Aberglaube," a belief based on irrationality or fear rather than reason or true faith</term>; indeed, you would not even have the raw material for superstition anymore. Take away all faith from knowledge, and you have

<summary>This page defines faith not as a lack of reason, but as a psychological extension that supplements incomplete knowledge. The author argues that faith and knowledge are interdependent, suggesting that faith without knowledge collapses into superstition, while knowledge is effectively a subset of the broader realm of faith.</summary>
<keywords>faith and knowledge, grounds of knowledge, objective certainty, superstition, determining factors, epistemology, philosophy of religion</keywords>

# Faith and Knowledge in General.

...to mathematical emptiness, only materialistic abundance remains; indeed, you stand alone with your soul in a world without a soul beside, above, before, or after you. People call faith "blind" in contrast to knowledge; it is indeed so in relation to knowledge, just as a person is blind in relation to their eyes—that is, when considered apart from their eyes. True knowledge, however, is the eyes of true faith. So, would faith only find its path through knowledge? But conversely, can the eyes find a path without the whole person? However, I do not wish to multiply metaphors where one might ask whether or to what extent they are accurate, regarding a matter that is inherently clear to anyone who directs a clear gaze toward the relationship between knowledge and faith.

People will also readily admit all of this, yet some will exclude precisely the subject for which it is most valid and intended to be asserted here: faith in the <term>highest and last things: "die höchsten und letzten Dinge," a philosophical term for ultimate concerns such as God, immortality, and the purpose of the universe</term>. "What can knowledge achieve there?" says one person; "Nothing; therefore, we must demand nothing of it in this matter." Another says: "Since nothing can be known here, it is simply <term>superstition: "Aberglaube," meaning irrational or false belief</term>." And so, the one discards knowledge, while the other discards faith, regarding those very things where only the most perfect union of all faculties can lead to what is simultaneously the truest, the best, and the highest.

# II.

## The Domain of Faith in the Narrower Sense.

---

Even though faith in the <term>highest and last things: "die höchsten und letzten Dinge," referring to ultimate metaphysical concerns like God and the purpose of existence</term> falls under the same general definition as any other kind of faith, it remains, in its essence, something extraordinary that stands above all other forms of belief. It possesses many qualities that it shares with no other.

First and foremost, it has in common with certain other beliefs that it is a <term>faith in spirits: "Geisterglaube," a belief in the existence of a spiritual realm or spiritual beings</term>; for even in the <term>Beyond: "Jenseits," the afterlife or transcendent world</term>, we are, after all, dealing with spirits. Connected to this is the fact that—at least <term>on this side: "diesseits," referring to our current earthly existence</term>—it is destined to always remain faith. This is because every person on this side will only ever be able to have direct knowledge of their own earthly soul. In contrast, there are other types of faith which are faith today but become knowledge tomorrow, or which at least promise eventual success in the endeavor to elevate them to the status of certain knowledge. Such success is, by the very nature of the matter, impossible here.

In the meantime, this is not the only faith in spirits, and therefore not the only one to which this applies. The same applies—

Conversely, then, the existence of the latter could be just as certain as the former; and it will be shown that we do, in fact, possess entirely corresponding reasons for both.

Yet, one type of faith always remains something of a *higher level* than the other; the reasons for the *one* must be expanded and intensified to become reasons for the *other*, and the interests of the *one* surpass those of the *other*.

Faith in other human souls holds for us the most immediate and specific interest; faith in animal souls and potential <note>The author, Gustav Theodor Fechner, was famous for his "panpsychist" view that plants and even the Earth itself possess souls.</note> plant souls is already more remote; and faith in souls on other celestial bodies is the most distant, of only incidental interest. However, faith in the highest and ultimate spiritual powers possesses a theoretical and practical interest for humanity that is more powerful and overarching than any other faith. It masters all interests attached to it—it is the most general, the highest, and the last. This is because the objects of this faith possess such qualities; the most distant is included only insofar as this faith, in its universality, encompasses even the most remote things.

According to this faith, the existence of the entire spiritual world—indeed, the world in general—is completed in God; it finds its bond, its peak, its <term>primal ground: "Urgrund," meaning the original, foundational source of all being</term>, and its principle therein.

### The Domain of Faith in the Narrower Sense.

...whatever names one might use for Him <note>Referring to the "highest spiritual power" or God discussed on the previous page.</note>, one seeks those names which signify the Highest in relation to the Whole. Through this, faith works its way down even into the life of the day-to-day, and thereby certainly gains influence over our most immediate and specialized interests. Conversely, faith in the souls of our neighbors—expressed through the **state**, **morality**, and **custom**—rises toward higher and more general interests. In short, the **world of faith** concerning the highest and ultimate things is an inherently interconnected world, much like the real world of lower, common things that we know—it is simply a higher world situated above this lower one.

At times, here and there, one or the other of the three aspects of faith may fall out of the bond; this cannot happen forever or within the collective faith of humanity—but when it does, it occurs only because that aspect falls out of faith entirely or survives only poorly as a remnant of the whole faith; for each part can only retain its power through its connection with the others, just as every limb withers when it is [severed] from the whole... <note>The text breaks off here and continues on the next page.</note>

The departed Buddha himself counts as an idol;—where God is missing, idols cannot easily be missing;—but that they have no God above their idols will be one of the reasons that the end of days will see no more Buddhism; and all other reasons will more or less depend on this. Among us, some believe in God without believing in an afterlife and personal spirits between us and God; but how dead, cold, abstract, empty, and helpless is this faith; they believe, so to speak, in God only for God's sake, and would not lose much along with that "poor God."

### III.

## Motives and Grounds (Principles) of Faith in General.

The determining grounds of faith, as we have said, exceed <note>original: "überreichen"; meaning to reach beyond, surpass, or extend further than.</note> those of knowledge; and given the great inadequacy of the determining grounds of knowledge regarding the highest and ultimate things <note>Refers to metaphysical concepts such as the soul or the nature of the divine, which cannot be proven through sensory observation alone.</note>, and yet the so universally widespread and powerful faith in them, we must assume extraordinarily powerful determining grounds for believing in these things. What can they be?

The believer in revelation <term>Offenbarungsgläubige: a person who bases their religious certainty on divine communication or scripture rather than rational deduction</term> is quickly finished with the answer. Faith in these things has been revealed to humanity by God Himself. Well, in the final instance, everything—and certainly, primarily, faith in God—will depend on God Himself. Indeed, every true faith depends in a certain sense on a "revelation" on the part of the existence of that which is believed. Or how could one validly believe in the existence of even a single tree belie- <note>The text ends mid-word ("glau-") and continues on the following page.</note>

-ve <note>original: "ben"; this is the conclusion of the word "glauben" (to believe) from the previous page.</note>, of which no one has ever seen a leaf or heard its rustling. Initially, yes; it says so in the Bible, according to the one who firmly believes in the word of the Bible; and he did so further through his Son, who is one with him, and additionally through supernatural inspiration of prophets, evangelists, apostles, popes <note>original: "Päbsten"; an archaic spelling of "Päpsten."</note>, saints, councils, and reformers. Yet, since all of this is itself merely a matter of faith—and for others, rather a matter of disbelief—the motives and grounds of such faith, insofar as they exist, must also be found among the general motives and grounds of faith that we shall consider in what follows; thus, we cannot begin by simply presupposing them.

Let us be frank: the believer in revelation <term>Offenbarungsgläubige: a person who accepts religious truths based on divine communication or sacred texts rather than reason alone</term>, who leans upon the word of the Bible—or perhaps even upon Luther or the Pope <note>original: "Pabste"; an archaic spelling</note>—as if upon a rock from which not a single crumb may fall for fear that the entire faith would collapse, will have to oppose this entire framing of the task from the very beginning. He cannot tolerate that which he considers the firm starting point of all judgment, and the highest authority above all judgment, to be subjected to any further assessment or questioning; for him, no higher authority exists to conduct such an inquiry. And truly, he is right to demand such a thing from religion; we ourselves will eventually make this same demand. However, while this certainty is already present for him, another person asks *if* and *where* it exists; for many things claim to be "revelation." To dismiss the question is no answer to it; we, however, only wish to answer the one who asks, and to help the one who searches.

More specifically, one can distinguish the determining factors of faith into **motives**, which drive one toward faith, and **grounds**, which justify it. The articulation of a ground we call an **argument**. Motives and grounds together—or what is common to both—are, as I have already said, the **principles of faith**.

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>Motives and Grounds (Principles)</header>

...faith in general anything other than motives, or more in the way of <term>grounds: logical justifications or reasons (Gründe)</term> than that which enters into it from knowledge—and which nevertheless never reaches the level of <term>certainty: absolute, objective proof (Gewißheit)</term>?

In truth, faith has no grounds that are anything other than the completion of motives; consequently, it possesses no grounds that suffice for proof, but only those that suffice for **conviction** <note>original: "Ueberzeugung"</note>—otherwise, it would be knowledge and not faith. Therefore, regardless of how faith might be grounded and supported, doubts from the perspective of knowledge remain possible. Such doubt may be foolish, yet it always remains possible without coming into contradiction with logic and experience; this is inherent in the very concept of faith itself. Despite everything that speaks for the existence of God, and everything that speaks for the universal validity of the laws of nature, the <term>materialist scientist: a thinker who believes only physical matter exists, often rejecting the divine</term> still doubts the existence of God, while the orthodox theologian doubts the universal validity of natural laws <note>The author likely refers to the theologian's belief in miracles, which are seen as exceptions to natural laws.</note>; indeed, the latter does not merely doubt, he denies it. One doubt may be as foolish as the other, yet both remain equally possible; the error of the one can be proven as little as that of the other. Only the **folly** <note>original: "Thorheit"</note> of it can be proven, by showing that it possesses to the highest degree that which is elsewhere universally regarded as foolish.

A fool is he who seeks society away from men, on paths where he can only hope to encounter similar, exceptional folly; one lets the fool go his way. A fool is he who [seeks] that which is harmful...

<summary>This page argues that faith is defined by conviction rather than absolute proof, meaning that doubt—whether of God or of natural laws—remains logically possible even if it is considered "foolish" by society.</summary>

In matters of faith, however, many people apply a different—indeed, the opposite—standard. One person considers all people who believed before him, or who believe around him what he himself does not believe, to be fools. Another considers all those fools who do not, like him, prefer a harmful unbelief to the most useful faith. And then there is the person who says: **I believe because it is absurd.** <note>original: "credo, quia absurdum est"; a phrase famously attributed to the early Christian author Tertullian, suggesting that the very impossibility of a religious claim is a reason to believe it.</note>

For all of these, there is no argument. For all argumentation in favor of faith can only be based on making that which is considered folly or wisdom in the smallest and most common things valid as such in the highest and ultimate things as well, and in this sense, elevating the <term>motives: the psychological or personal drives for belief</term> of faith themselves to the status of <term>grounds: logical or objective justifications (Gründe)</term>.

In truth, a primary task of the following pages will be to show how the grounds that justify faith are merely the highest generalization, synthesis, culmination, clarification, and proper arrangement of the motives that drive one toward faith. In short, they are what I call the **completion of motives**. This means that man may and should believe for the very same reasons—indeed, for the sake of everything—for which he actually...

...believes; — and how beautiful and good it is that it is so. 

But it is also necessary to show that within this process, a decisive limitation, correction, and purification of these motives through one another is inherently included. That which is insufficient and even <term>invalid: lacking logical weight or soundness (untriftig)</term> when partial or isolated—and most <term>motives: personal or psychological drives (Motive)</term> are such—can, when grasped completely, fully, and in the greatest <term>generality: the state of being universal or all-encompassing (Allgemeinheit)</term>, provide full <term>validity: the quality of being logically sound or cogent (Triftigkeit)</term>. Instead of rejecting the "half-measure" or partial reason that drives a person here and there toward faith because of its invalidity, one should instead merely supplement and fulfill it until it reaches full validity. What has its truth and <term>justification: the logical defense of a belief (Rechtfertigung)</term> as a single moment, a side, a part, or a step toward the whole, can become false and invalid if it is elevated to the status of the whole or the summit; instead of rejecting it for its invalidity, one simply needs to reduce and limit it to the specific degree at which it remains valid. However, the various motives themselves perform this limitation and, consequently, this correction of each other.

The African <note>original: "Neger"; a term used in the 19th century that is now considered derogatory. <meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 31 regarding the nature of faith. While the previous page distinguished between subjective "motives" (like those of an African tribesman worshipping a specific tree) and universal "grounds," this page seeks to find a middle ground. It suggests that "pagan" beliefs are not entirely false, but rather "fragmented" truths that need to be unified into a whole.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header># Motives and Grounds (Principles)</header>

...for does the <term>pagan: a historical term for someone following a non-Abrahamic, often nature-based religion (Heide)</term> believe that the <term>matter: the physical substance of the world (Materie)</term> of all those things is itself God? He only means that they are the carriers of a <term>spiritual power: an underlying non-physical force or agency (geistige Macht)</term> hidden behind them, similar to his own, but reaching far beyond it. And is it then an error to say that the entire world is the carrier of a divine spiritual power hidden behind it?

The error of all that <term>individual faith: a belief system centered on specific objects or parts rather than a universal whole (Einzelglauben)</term> is thus only this: that which rests fully only in the whole is sought in the individual, or shifted into the individual. Supplement the faith of all pagans with one another and link it by taking the divine spiritual power—which the pagan attaches to the individual object—and attaching it instead to the whole; then you have the right faith. On the other hand, reduce the power of the individual (which the pagan expands to the scale of the whole) down to its proper measure, and you again have the right faith. Every <term>natural object: any physical entity in the environment (Naturgegenstand)</term> does indeed possess a part of the power with which God, reigning in all things, reaches beyond everything human; it simply does not possess the whole, the final, the highest power.

Furthermore, this fragmented <term>pagan faith: the diverse, localized beliefs of polytheistic or animistic peoples (Heidenglaube)</term> is not the only kind of faith; from another side, there is a faith in God as a unified spirit who is elevated high above the world of matter, seeing it as his footstool, his offspring, or his refuse. <note>original: "Abfall"; in 19th-century philosophy, this often refers to the idea of the physical world being a "falling away" or a lesser byproduct of the absolute spirit.</note> If the <term>unity of the divine essence: the concept of God as a single, indivisible being (Einheit des göttlichen Wesens)</term> is missing in the pagan faith, then this other faith lacks a living relationship to nature; yet one can supplement and correct the other.

<summary>The author argues that pagan nature-worship is not a complete error, but a fragmented truth where divine power is incorrectly limited to individual objects rather than the whole of nature. He suggests that true faith reconciles the pagan's connection to nature with the monotheist's understanding of a single, unified divine spirit.</summary>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s investigation into the origins of faith. He has just argued that "pagan" religions are fragments of a larger truth; now, he shifts toward a methodological proposal: instead of proving God exists and then explaining why people believe, he suggests we should study the psychological motives of belief first to find the path toward proof.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>on Faith in General.</header>
<sig>3</sig>
<meta>Fechner, Motives of Faith.</meta>

...in that which everyone lacks and in which everyone falls short; and so faith in a God <term>omnipresent: present everywhere at all times (allgegenwärtig)</term> and <term>all-ruling: governing all aspects of existence (allwaltend)</term> in nature will arise as the true faith—a faith which all Christians also profess in word, without, admittedly, being truly earnest about the meaning of that word.

Even if we can trace the ultimate <term>origin: the fundamental source or "ground of arising" (Entstehungsgrund)</term> of faith—provided it is a true faith—back to the actual <term>existence: the objective reality of a thing (Dasein)</term> of the thing believed in, this unified ground immediately splits in its effects into several <term>determining factors: the specific reasons or impulses that lead to a conclusion (Bestimmungsgründe)</term> or motives. One can attempt to trace how these motives for believing in the <note>original: "die höchsten und letzten Dinge" (the highest and ultimate things); a common philosophical phrase for God, the soul, and eternity.</note> highest and ultimate things depend upon the existence of those things. However, one must then presuppose the existence and the manner of existence of these things as already given—a presupposition that must first justify itself by raising these motives to the status of true, logical grounds. Therefore, instead of beginning with the existence of the highest and ultimate things as a "given" and deriving the motives and grounds of faith from it, we must rather begin with the motives of faith as the "given" facts. From these, we shall establish the grounds of our faith. Afterward, if valid grounds can be found for believing in the existence and a specific manner of existence of these highest and ultimate things, we remain free to reflect backward on how the motives and grounds within us could have emerged from that reality.

<vocab>Faith, God, Origin, Motives, Presence/Being, Existence, Fechner, Determining Grounds</vocab>

<summary>Fechner argues that instead of assuming God's existence to explain why people believe, we should study the human "motives" for faith as a starting point to prove the existence of "ultimate things." He criticizes Christians who claim God is omnipresent but do not take the implications of that word seriously.</summary>

<keywords>methodology, omnipresence, psychological motives, existence of God, religious philosophy, Fechner, inductive reasoning</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page follows Fechner’s transition from the origins of faith to the logical "principles" or motives that justify it. He is establishing a parallel between our belief in other people's souls and our belief in God.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
# Motives and Reasons (Principles)
<meta>catchword: see-</meta>

Generally speaking, and as I have already stated, the motives and reasons for belief in these things—that is, the <term>highest and ultimate spiritual existences: a philosophical reference to God and the immortal soul (die höchsten und letzten geistigen Existenzen)</term>—are no different than those for any spiritual existences, or indeed for things in general; they are merely heightened and <term>peaked: reaching a culmination or highest point (gegipfelt)</term> above all others. And why should we ultimately consider the existence of higher spirits and a highest spirit to be less certain than the existence of our **neighbor-spirits**, if we find corresponding motives and reasons for them, only in a heightened and peaked form? 

<note>Fechner uses the term "neighbor-spirits" (Nachbargeister) to refer to other human beings. He argues that since we cannot "see" the consciousness of our friends or neighbors, but believe in them anyway, belief in an invisible God follows the same logic.</note>

Insofar as they are such, they cannot, of course, lie as plainly on the hand or surface as those for the latter; rather, it will be necessary to direct one's gaze higher and keep it focused more on the heights than when it concerns belief in neighbor-spirits, without, however, leaving the realm of motives and reasons that this belief possesses. Furthermore, there are no obstacles that would stand more in the way of belief in higher spirits and the highest spirit than in the way of belief in neighbor-spirits, were it not for that very greater height of the motives and reasons themselves.

No one sees God; why then believe in Him? But do you see the soul of your brother? Yet you believe in it; therefore, the fact that you do not see a God in relation to the world can, at any rate, be no reason to believe in Him any less than in the soul of your brother. From the latter, you do not even demand to see it; from God, it see- <note>The German text breaks off with "schei-", likely the beginning of "scheint" (seems).</note>

<summary>Fechner argues that believing in God is logically similar to believing in the existence of other people's souls, as both are invisible yet inferred through reason. He suggests the only difference is the "height" or scale of the evidence required.</summary>

<keywords>motives of faith, neighbor-spirits, spiritual existence, invisibility of the soul, existence of God, religious epistemology</keywords>

<header>### Motives and Reasons (Principles)</header>

<note>The first point is a continuation of the previous page's discussion on why we believe in souls.</note> 1) [Different] nations also believe in plant souls because this belief has been instilled in them from childhood; we do not, because the opposite has been instilled in us.

2) Because we need a belief in the souls of other people <note>original: "Menschenseelen"</note>, find fulfillment in it, and indeed, could not practically function without it. Regarding animal souls and plant souls, this motive—as well as the others—carries less weight; therefore, belief in them is also less widespread. Even the existence of animal souls is denied here and there.

3) Because analogy, <term>empirical conclusions: "Erfahrungsschlüsse," or logical deductions based on what we have observed</term> in general, and reason based on that experience lead us to assume spirits in other bodies that correspond to our own spirit. Even if we do not require another body to be fully similar to our own in order to assume it has a soul, we still look for a certain similarity in specific traits that we assume are characteristic of the <term>existence of a soul: "Seelendasein"</term>. Of course, one can still question and debate—and people certainly do—exactly which traits these are.

No other motives for believing in <term>neighbor-souls: "Nachbarseelen," the souls of beings outside ourselves, such as fellow humans, animals, or plants</term> can be found. These reasons—and no others—which prompt us to believe in these neighboring souls, sometimes from one perspective and sometimes from another, are also the very things that justify this belief, provided they are correctly understood. However, one might still argue over which interpretation is the complete one and which is incorrect.

## IV.

### Historical, practical, and theoretical *principles* of *faith* in general.

Whatever names the *motives* of faith <note>original: "Glaubens," which can mean "faith" or "belief" depending on context; here it refers to the general state of holding something to be true.</note> might bear, and whatever there may be to believe in, they ultimately trace back to three, which for the sake of brevity I distinguish as *historical*, *practical*, and *theoretical*. For now, I will formulate them briefly and only in broad strokes, in order to attach the more precise details later.

*Historical Motive*. One believes what we are told, what was believed before us, and what is believed around us.

*Practical Motive*. One believes what it pleases us, serves us, or <term>benefits: "frommt," a word suggesting something that is both useful and spiritually or morally edifying</term> us to believe.

*Theoretical Motive*. One believes that for which one finds determining grounds in experience and <term>reason: "Vernunft," the human faculty of logic and intellectual synthesis</term>.

None of these motives can be reduced to the others without force. The historical, it is true, appears from certai— <note>The word is cut off at the page break: "gewis-" likely completed as "gewissen" (certain).</note>

<vocab>Faith/Belief, Motives, historical motive, practical motive, theoretical motive, experience, reason</vocab>

<summary>The author categorizes all reasons for belief into three distinct motives: tradition (historical), utility (practical), and evidence-based logic (theoretical). He argues that these three pillars of faith are independent and cannot be simplified into one another.</summary>

<keywords>faith, belief, historical motive, practical motive, theoretical motive, experience, reason, tradition, utility, epistemology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 38, completing the sentence regarding why the "historical motive" for faith might initially seem derivative before arguing for its practical primacy in human development.</meta>

->### Historical, practical, and theoretical Principle &c.<-

viewpoints as not original; for in order for another to communicate their faith to me, they must themselves already possess determining grounds for faith and for the communication of that faith, which would then need to be questioned. If these grounds, in turn, happen to lie in a communication received from elsewhere, this only pushes the question further back, and it would then seem that only the theoretical and practical motives remain as the final occasions for faith and the sharing of that faith. 

However, in the sense of a specific <term>faith in revelation: "Offenbarungsglaube," belief based on truths communicated by a divine source rather than discovered by human logic</term>, the first communication occurred from God himself to humanity, either directly or through supernatural inspiration. According to this view, the historical motive would instead be the most original of all. 

Even if we set aside the entire obscure question of the first origin of faith within humanity and instead ask how faith first reaches every person today, it remains certain—and will be elaborated upon further—that the child, to whom faith is communicated by parents and teachers, does not accept it on the basis of theoretical or practical motives. The child simply accepts it because it is given to them, so that in any case, for every individual today, the historical motive is the most original.

Instead of a one-sided dependence of one motive upon the others, we must rather recognize, within certain limits, a <term>mutual interdependence: "Wechselabhängigkeit," a philosophical concept where multiple factors support and validate one another simultaneously</term> of all three upon each other.

# Historical, practical, and theoretical [Aspects]

The entire formation of ***faith***, which has developed through a long-term influence of the ***theoretical*** and ***practical motives***, possibly originating from a ***direct revelation***, is transferred ***historically*** all at once to those newly entering humanity <note>i.e., children or new members of a community</note>. Should ***faith*** have to be generated anew in everyone solely through the mediation of the ***theoretical*** and ***practical motives***, how weak, impoverished, and divided it would be; indeed, in how few would it even come into being at all. The entire ***capital of faith*** <note>original: "Glaubenskapital"—the collective spiritual and intellectual "wealth" accumulated by humanity over centuries</note> of humanity is inherited ***historically*** within humanity, preserved as a whole and in great portions. From another perspective, however, if the ***theoretical*** and ***practical motives*** did not compel one to hold fast to, propagate, and even develop ***faith***, then the ***historical*** element would lose its effect; the ***capital***, wherever it may have originally come from, would consume itself and dwindle. It is like with the ***blood***; the circulation of that which is already present gives power and life and strength, not the ***nourishment***; yet the ***blood*** cannot draw the power and life and strength of its circulation from itself; it requires ***nourishment***.

Thus, the ***historical motive*** could achieve as little without the ***theoretical*** and ***practical*** as vice versa, and ***factually***, none has taken the development and formation of ***faith*** solely upon itself. But this cooperative and interwoven effect of the motives nevertheless prevents

# Principle of Faith in General.

[...It is impossible] not to track the mode of action of each <note>referring to the three motives: historical, theoretical, and practical</note> independently, up to certain limits; indeed, in order to know how they work with and through one another, one must know what each produces from its own side. This is especially true because the harmony which the three ***motives*** demonstrate in a general and total sense—in calling forth and maintaining ***faith*** as a whole—does not exist when it comes to specifics or in relation to the individual components of ***faith***.

Rather, in specific cases, we see here this motive, there that motive appearing as predominant or even exclusive—even hostile toward the others—and the conflict of the ***motives*** is as frequent as their compatibility. In ***Catholicism*** and ***orthodox Protestantism***, the ***historical principle*** is the predominant one; in ***Neo-Catholicism*** <note>Refers to 19th-century movements attempting to reconcile Catholic tradition with modern philosophy</note> and philosophical religious doctrines, the ***theoretical*** [is predominant]; in the teachings of ***Confucius*** and state religions as such, the ***practical*** [is predominant]. 

What the ***historical motive*** would have us believe often fails to satisfy the ***practical*** and ***theoretical motives***, as it appears to us as neither beneficial to our ***salvation*** <note>original: "Heile"—can mean both spiritual salvation and general well-being</note> nor grounded in the nature of things. On the other hand, nothing is more common than the alliance of the ***historical*** and ***practical motives*** against the ***theoretical motive***, and therefore nothing is more common than the ***conflict of faith with knowledge***; and again, nothing is more common than for one of the three to want to bend the other two under its yoke. Thus, in one-sided arrogance, the ***historical [motive]*** demands that which has once become fixed and would [be...]

### Historical, practical and theoretical

[...against the theoretical, the historical motive presents] the most unholy things as necessary for salvation <note>The German word "Heil" carries a double meaning of both spiritual salvation and general well-being; the author plays on this by contrasting it with "Heilloseste," meaning the most disastrous or unholy things.</note> and wishes to lead all of science on a leash. The **practical motive** determines and monitors teachers and teachings according to its own purposes, thereby controlling the direction of the **historical** propagation of faith and the influence of science upon it; and the **theoretical motive** interprets and over-interprets <note>original: "deutet und deutelt"—the latter implies a forced, pedantic, or hair-splitting interpretation</note> the historical **sources of faith** in its own sense, sacrificing goodness to truth.

Not only can the three motives conflict with one another, but each can also conflict with itself; and just as the struggle between **sects** is often harsher than that between the religions to which they are subordinate, so it is with the conflict of these motives.

Thus, the **historical motive** conflicts with itself in the belief in the **Quran** and the **Bible**; within the former, again in the faith of the **Shiites** and **Sunnis**; here, in the belief in the **Pope**, in **Luther**, and **Calvin**. The **practical motive** conflicts in the shaping of faith by **priests** and **rulers** for their own benefit versus for the salvation of all, and in the favoring of this side of advantage here and that side there. Finally, the **theoretical motive** conflicts within the various **philosophical doctrines of faith**, as many as there may be.

The total result of humanity’s faith is the outcome of the cooperation and counter-action of these three motives, both with each other and within themselves. Taken as a whole, it is and remains a positive, powerful result; indeed, the counter-action of the motives itself contributes to making it power- <note>The text breaks off mid-word "gewaltig," meaning powerful or mighty.</note>

It is the case of the eternally changing and swaying sea, filling itself again through its own evaporation, and becoming all the more powerful through the struggle of its waves. Whatever changes or wavers within it in detail, it remains on the whole an eternal sea, from which all rivers secretly draw, only to pour what was drawn back into it at the end of their course. And even those things within it that run against one another are, at their core and as a whole, connected.

But the sea has existed from the beginning and will exist eternally; the small buckets may exhaust themselves with bailing; yet what they pour out simply runs through the air back to the sea.

### Historical, Practical, and Theoretical Matters

...brought forth faith in humanity, and to trace them in broad strokes, it is necessary to clearly establish their own nature and their relationship to one another, and this is the immediate task. To this end, however, they must above all be viewed where they are immediately clear and beyond doubt to observation—that is, how they operate within human consciousness, rather than how they might be rooted in a consciousness of God, a general world order, or an idea of history and things, wherein we may ultimately seek their root or the ground of their general character <note>original: "allgemeinen Zuges"</note>.

For by descending to the individual effect of these motives within people in our consideration of faith, it is not to say that faith in humanity arose through an **atomistic** <term>Atomistic: A philosophical approach that tries to explain complex phenomena by breaking them down into their smallest possible parts or individual units, rather than seeing them as a single whole.</term> summation of motives in single individuals. Rather, it means that the general, higher-reaching reasons that brought forth faith in humanity—whatever they may be—have asserted themselves as these specific motives in every person under their influence. Only these, however, enter directly into experience; only from these can we ourselves infer the nature of the more general reasons and thus characterize them through their effects, whereas they would otherwise remain obscure. Therefore, our gaze must first be directed toward this, so as not to [tangle ourselves] with mystical and

# Principle of Faith in General

...[tangle ourselves in] idle speculations that presuppose what should only be concluded from the motives and reasons; whereby it is not only permissible but a necessity reveals itself to ascend from the working of motives in individuals to that which works more generally in humanity, which we will ultimately find only in the existence of the believed highest and final things <note>This refers to the ultimate objects of faith, such as God or the afterlife.</note> themselves.

Thus, with our empirical <term>Empirical: Based on observation and experience rather than theory or pure logic.</term> consideration of the nature and activity of the motives in their individuality, their combinations, and conflicts, a coherent history and a higher conception of their activity in humanity is neither excluded nor replaced; rather, it is prepared and supported; and the conclusion in a higher conception will emerge quite naturally in the course of the following observations.

Each of the three motives can be elevated to a true reason—one capable of being expressed in an argument—and thus be raised above fluctuation, division, conflict, error, and confusion; even if it remains nothing other than what we have said: the highest generalization, summary, peak, clarification, alignment—in short, the completion from one side and the authoritative limitation from the other side of a corresponding motive. # V.

## The Historical Principle.

One person tells it to another. That is the concise expression of the historical principle. Whether spoken or written, it is all the same. In this way, faith propagates from one human being to another. But how did it first arrive among humanity?

One can only ever start from the "first" <note>The "first" refers to the ultimate origin or the starting point of a sequence.</note> if one already knows it; however, one must begin with what comes later if the beginning is unknown, and see if one can find within it the means to draw conclusions about that first origin. Yet, the means of concluding the first emergence of faith will always remain as uncertain as those regarding the first emergence, the first state, and the first development of humanity itself. The choice between the completely opposing hypotheses <note>original: "Hypothesen." Fechner is likely referring to the 19th-century debate between the idea of an original divine revelation and the idea that religion evolved naturally from primitive human psychology.</note> that exist regarding this will always remain a matter of faith—a faith whose general grounds we do not wish to simply believe here, but rather to understand through knowledge. Therefore, let us at least postpone that question, in order to later with

<summary>Fechner introduces the "historical principle" of faith—the transmission of belief through human testimony—and argues that while its ultimate origins are as mysterious as the origin of humanity itself, we must study its current forms to understand its roots.</summary>
<keywords>historical principle, tradition, origin of faith, human development, religious hypotheses, Gustav Theodor Fechner</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "historical principle" from page 49. The author had just argued that because the ultimate origin of faith is as mysterious as the origin of humanity itself, we should instead examine how faith is transmitted today.
