## The Historical Principle.

...return to it later with some points; and, instead of first asking how faith first came to humanity, let us first ask only how it first comes to every human being today.

It is with the emergence of faith as it is with all things. In all things, the manner of the first emergence is distinct—and must be essentially distinguished—from the manner in which the emergence of that thing repeats itself. Once something has come into being, it spares, so to speak, the effort and cost of its initial creation during its subsequent reappearances—if not entirely, then at least for the largest and most essential part—by containing within its very existence the conditions for the repetition of its existence, held, as it were, as if preserved <note>original: "aufgehoben." This is a significant philosophical term in German (notably used by Hegel) which means to simultaneously cancel, preserve, and lift up to a higher level.</note> within itself.

What effort was required to create a statue, a painting, or a poem for the very first time! But once the work exists, it is effortlessly cast, copied, recited, and printed a thousand times over.

Only after thousands, indeed perhaps millions or billions of years of the earth’s existence, could a human pair emerge. Once there was one pair, it was easy to populate the earth, and soon it will be difficult to prevent its overpopulation.

No one knows how languages emerged, and no one would be able to invent them anew; now that they have been invented, every child learns them through play.

<vocab>Faith, Emergence, Humanity, Statue, Painting, Poem, Languages, Overpopulation</vocab>

<summary>Fechner argues that while the original creation of things like art, life, and faith is incredibly difficult and mysterious, their subsequent reproduction and transmission through history is easy and natural.</summary>
<keywords>emergence vs. repetition, historical transmission, origin of language, overpopulation, preservation (aufgehoben), cultural reproduction</keywords>

### The Historical Principle.

How much effort it often costs to light the fire in a stove. Once it is burning, one must take care that the house does not burn down.

To produce a plague <note>original: "Seuche." This can refer to an epidemic, a contagion, or a pestilence.</note> generally exceeds human art; to dampen a plague is no less difficult.

In all of this, one can ask about the first reason for existence, and usually one will not find it; the reason for later existence is easy to find; it lies simply in that which is already there.

It is the same with all things; it is also the same with faith.

No one knows how faith first arose, though one can certainly imagine this or that about it. One can also ask whether it would be possible to find or invent it anew. Now that it has once emerged—whether true or false—it propagates <term>propagates: the German "fortpflanzt" refers to both biological reproduction and the spreading of ideas, fire, or physical waves.</term> itself, just as man propagates, as language propagates, as fire propagates, as contagion propagates. No one is born with faith in God, but everyone is born with such a general and unconditional disposition to believe everything they are told, that it is quite natural for them to also believe in God when they are told of such a being. The more a child is expected to believe, the more willingly it will believe.

For all the faith implanted in a child, the child requires no reasons; indeed, it would not understand reasons. Nor is it the beauty, the majesty, the wholesomeness, or the comforting nature of a faith that causes a child to believe. After all, the child believes all the more firmly in ghosts the more it is made to fear them. The psychological reality may simply be this: the child learns to understand words and speech only by connecting them to facts; now, conversely, it connects the fact to the word and the speech. Or perhaps: the child imitates everything an adult does until it has acquired the skills and abilities through that imitation to move beyond it; much is, so to speak, imitated within the child automatically. Thus, the faith of the adults is also imitated within the child. I am quite happy, however, to allow for someone to grasp the psychological basis of this fact even more deeply. Here, it is held—

Instead of having to first learn faith, the child must *unlearn* faith; and this happens gradually when the person repeatedly experiences that the word can lie. Then the self-evident nature of the association between reality and the word dissolves, the spirit of credulity <note>original: "Gläubigkeit," which can mean both religious faith and a general tendency to believe things without evidence (credulity).</note> gradually weakens, and can finally even turn into its opposite. Women, in particular, always remain children in matters of faith, <note>This assertion reflects common 19th-century patriarchal views that categorized women and the "common masses" as more emotional and less capable of critical reasoning than educated men.</note> and the faith of the masses can be led by any prophet with a sufficiently strong voice, lively gestures, and power of speech, like a stream of water through a channel.

This is especially true in the field of things where no experience, no discovery of an error through facts, is possible. The credulity of man retreats to those areas when it is displaced from all fields of experience. And the fewer reasons the ignorant man has for his faith, and the less he understands of the reasons for it, the more likely he is to let himself be killed for it or to kill others for it, because then even fewer counter-arguments can be asserted against it.

# The Historical Principle.

Even one whose thought has matured, who has learned doubt, does not remain withdrawn from the influence of the **historical motive**; <note>The "historical motive" refers to the weight of tradition and the authority of past generations in shaping current beliefs.</note> the prevailing opinion around him and the authority above him exert an involuntary power over him, and the whole faith that was implanted in him as a child is not easily lost again. Rather, it involuntarily determines much of the development of his intellect and his conclusions.

This is especially true when it concerns matters that, despite all his experience, also exceed the scope of any logical conclusion; <note>The author is referring to "transcendental" or metaphysical questions—like the nature of the soul or the afterlife—which cannot be proven or disproven by science or logic.</note> and so we see even many of the sharpest thinkers, after a vain struggle to establish what is true and best in the field of the highest and ultimate things through their own reason, finally despairing of this. They then submit themselves anew, with conscious will, to the power of the **historical principle**, which promises to return to them the peace and trust of a child. Thus, the end returns to the beginning.

Here we see how the **practical motive** <note>The "practical motive" refers to the personal utility of faith—the comfort, social stability, or hope for salvation that belief provides.</note> enters to revitalize and strengthen the historical one. Furthermore, those who themselves implant faith in another seek to make it more plausible by drawing upon other motives; but if the historical motive relies on these other motives, the reverse is no less true. To the common people and the child, reasons for faith are offered, and their salvation is tied to that faith, and

# The Historical Principle

...and practical motive, yet generally it is not held onto without reasons. By the child, in the lower classes of people and nations, yes; but that is not what sustains it in the long run. The child does not remain a child forever; nations grow up, and in every nation one part grows beyond the others, and one portion of nations rises above the rest, and the adults rule over the children and over the **faith** of those children and that part of the people who remain at the stage of childhood. Among adults, however, faith must pass its trials. One knows that it often does not pass them in individual cases, but it always passes and survives them as a whole. It has survived a **French Revolution** <note>The French Revolution (1789) often involved radical dechristianization and the promotion of a "Cult of Reason."</note>, it has survived the **year 1848** <note>The Revolutions of 1848 were a series of political upheavals across Europe that often challenged traditional religious and monarchical authority.</note>, it has survived the assault of the **materialists** <term>materialists (original: Materialisten); those who believe that only physical matter exists and deny the existence of a spiritual or supernatural realm</term> of all ages; it will survive everything. And if some, after long examination with **reason**, even sacrifice their reason to faith, then there must be—quite apart from reason—the most significant grounds for believing. If, above all, priests and rulers strive to instill it, and if even those who do not possess it themselves strive to instill it, then there must also be very general, compelling, and binding reasons for instilling it. Even if **historical transmission** <term>historical transmission (original: historische Fortpflanzung); the process by which traditions and beliefs are passed from one generation to the next</term> in itself is not a sufficient reason to consider that which is transmitted to be true, the universality and the necessity of this transmission can nonetheless point back to general and compelling reasons for its existence.

# The Historical Principle

And further: if faith, as it has been historically propagated, is generally full of errors and fables, and one faith runs contrary to another, then the specific definitions and forms of faith must be distinguished from its general and essential points. The relative agreement throughout all times and peoples—that there is a Divine **above** and a Beyond **after** this human world, and that man’s highest and final <term>salvation</term> <note>original: Heil; refers here to ultimate spiritual well-being or the state of being "saved."</note> is placed in his relationship to them—is indeed quite marvelous. Therefore, for this—not for the changing and conflicting aspects of faith—a universal validity and a compelling force of reasons must be assumed. And however much we may give up of the specific forms of faith, we do not therefore have to give up faith in its entirety. One should not throw the baby out with the bathwater; should one throw God out with the bathwater? <note>This is a play on the common German and English idiom *das Kind mit dem Bade ausschütten*, meaning to discard something valuable while trying to get rid of something unwanted.</note>

The great achievement of the contradictions that appear in <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: Wissen; in this 19th-century context, it refers to systematic, scientific, or philosophical understanding.</note> is not to abolish knowledge, but to promote it; for with every resolution of a contradiction, knowledge rises one step higher. Should it be any different in faith?

<meta>This page continues the argument that the historical endurance of belief in God is unique. While the previous page addressed why we shouldn't "throw the baby out with the bathwater" when religious forms conflict, this page explores why belief persists at all if it were merely a "fable."</meta>

# The Historical *Principle*

...otherwise. Where is the fable, the fairy tale, that has elsewhere gained such wide distribution, such permanence, or has produced such firm belief? To be sure, there are perhaps a few children’s fairy tales and animal fables that have been told in India since antiquity just as they are among us; but these are also quite exceptional rarities, and their distribution is always quite meager compared to that of the belief in God <note>original: Gottesglaube</note>. Most importantly, everyone—both in India and here—regards them as mere fables. Rather, the historical *motive* <note>original: Motiv; here referring to the historical momentum or driving force behind an idea.</note> would not be able to spread the great fable of God and the afterlife so far, nor maintain it in power for so long, if it were truly a fable. For here, the following consideration enters, suited to lead us finally from the *motive* to the *argument*.

Error and truth have this in common: both can be propagated historically, and their spread becomes easier the further it has already progressed; faith maintains itself more easily the longer it has already been held. However, a difference exists: with truth, this progression continues indefinitely, while with error, it only reaches certain limits. This is because, as a true belief spreads, something remains constantly helpful and conducive to it, whereas something constantly works against a false belief. This opposing force grows alongside the spread and duration of the belief and finally, of necessity, outweighs it to the benefit of the true faith. And it is not difficult to find what this is.

Why do we call a belief true and good?

<summary>The author argues that the global endurance of belief in God proves it is not a mere "fable," as fables lack the universal "conducive" power of truth that allows a belief to persist indefinitely.</summary>
<keywords>fable, fairy tale, belief in God, historical motive, truth vs error, endurance of faith</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical argument from the previous page, which asked why we call a belief true. The author is moving from the "motive" (why people believe) to the "argument" (why that belief is actually valid based on its long-term survival).</meta>

# The Historical Principle

...because it corresponds to the nature of things and the needs of humanity. Why is it false and bad? Because it stands in contradiction to them. What follows from this even *from first principles* <note>original: *a priori*; reasoning based on theoretical deduction rather than observation.</note>? The more a belief spreads and the longer it lasts, the more opportunity it will offer and find to develop and prove its harmony or its conflict with the nature of things and of humanity; its most important effects and consequences can even only develop in proportion to its spread and duration. From harmony, a support for its further spread and continued preservation must arise; from conflict, a counter-reaction against them must emerge, which grows with the spread and duration of the belief. In a word, it is the relationship to the theoretical and practical *principles* <term>theoretisches und praktisches Princip; the author refers to logical consistency (theoretical) and moral or functional utility (practical) as the two pillars that test a belief's validity.</term> that determines final success. <margin>x</margin> The historical principle <note>original: Das historische Princip; the momentum or power an idea gains simply by being established in history and passed through generations.</note> itself aids bad and good, true and false beliefs equally; but in relation to these, it stands in an opposite relationship to the theoretical and practical principles. The ultimate goal is the harmony of all things in all respects; and this can ultimately only take place with a true and good belief.

It is here with belief as it is with fire. Fire spreads all the more easily the further it has already spread, but it also all the more easily consumes its fuel; and would have long ago gone out on the earth

<summary>This page argues that while historical momentum helps any idea grow initially, only truth can survive indefinitely because it aligns with human nature and reality, whereas falsehood eventually creates a counter-reaction that destroys it.</summary>

# The Historical Principle

...be, if new fuel did not always grow back. The longer it lasts, the further it spreads, and the more its conflicts with the nature of humans and things develop—as its harmful consequences accumulate and spread—the closer it moves toward its turning point <note>original: *Wendepunct*; the author implies a tipping point where the momentum of a false idea finally fails.</note>. And so we see one error, one fable after another fall, while the truth increasingly steadies and strengthens itself, gaining more and firmer ground.

<margin>x</margin>
Every bad belief, and everything bad within a belief, eventually reaches that turning point at some time, beyond which its further spread and continued duration increase the conditions for its decay more than those for its growth. The theoretical and practical principles—which initially fought powerlessly against the force of the historical principle <term>historical principle; the weight of tradition and the momentum an idea gains simply because it has been accepted for a long time.</term>—gain more and more ground. Individual cases of unbelief or deviating belief among individuals, and even among nations and eras, cannot be taken into consideration against the preponderance of a universally widespread faith that preserves and further develops itself through the passage of time. These instances are explainable by the one-sided or partial influence of reasons that have detached themselves from the general context, whereas a faith that is generally predominant and steadily maintained presupposes a universal, coherent, and lasting influence of grounds.

According to this, one can draw a conclusion about the truth and goodness of a faith without it being necessary to clearly develop the grounds lying within the realm of existence <term>Existenzgebiete: the objective reality or domain in which the causes of belief are found.</term> which have come to take effect in humanity; their effect itself proves their existence. And herein the historical argument differs from the theoretical and practical arguments, which we will come to speak of later. It concludes from the effect of the grounds the presence of those grounds.

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 75, contrasting the benefits of modern intellectual advancement with the risks of over-specialization and the loss of holistic unity.</meta>

-># The Historical Principle<-

on the other hand, the danger grows of losing oneself in details and becoming fragmented. There is a risk of confusing the increasingly necessary division of labor <note>original: *Theilung der Arbeit*; here applied to intellectual and scientific inquiry</note> with a division of the *object* of labor; of confusing increasingly sharp distinctions with actual separations; increasingly high abstractions with "higher entities"; and that which is conceivable in itself with that which exists in itself. In doing so, we face the danger of losing the real unity of existence. 

Perhaps our time has succumbed to this danger more than any previous one. In this regard, a "naturally-grown" <note>original: *naturwüchsige*; referring to a primal, organic, or intuitive way of thinking that hasn't been dissected by modern analysis</note> perspective—which does not yet separate what it cannot even clearly distinguish, and which rarely classifies or abstracts—might have an advantage and a justification over an advanced era. Meanwhile, the pinnacle and the goal of progress will undoubtedly allow us to regain the most unified connection possible, while simultaneously maximizing the clear distinction of all parts and aspects of existence gained during that progress. Thus, according to the law of the "contact of extremes" <note>original: *Berührung der Extreme*; a philosophical concept where opposing poles eventually meet or resolve into one another</note>, progress will in a certain sense return to its point of origin, even as it reaches its greatest deviation from it in another sense. 

Body and soul, soul and spirit, organic and inorganic, man and earth, earth and heaven, man and God, God and world, this world <term>this world: *Diesseits*; the physical, earthly realm of current experience</term> and the beyond <term>the beyond: *Jenseits*; the spiritual realm or the afterlife</term>, the finite and the infinite—all of these are today not only distinguished but also separated in a way that, in part...

-># The Historical Principle<-

even sharply opposed to one another—distinctions of which the earliest era knew nothing, and of which the final era will undoubtedly want to know nothing more.

Where a conflict now occurs between "naturally-grown" <note>original: *naturwüchsiger*; referring to ideas that develop organically and intuitively within a culture rather than through deliberate scientific analysis</note> views and those of a more advanced era, it is necessary, first and foremost, to pay attention to the nature of the peoples involved. The naturally-grown views of those peoples from whom progress itself has developed must be regarded with a completely different eye than the views of those who—like the fetish-worshipers <note>original: *Fetischanbeter*; a term used in 19th-century scholarship to describe indigenous religions that focused on inanimate objects, often used disparagingly to denote a lack of "civilized" development</note>—are simply doomed to succumb to the progress of culture <term>culture: *Cultur*; in this context, it refers to the overarching advancement of human civilization and intellect</term>. Such groups have historically proven their own lack of validity through their lack of capacity for development. Meanwhile, the fact that the former group [the ancestors of modern civilization] established the very foundation for progress makes it appear possible that this progress only temporarily lost some of those perspectives that were rightfully held at the starting point.

And now, secondly, one must look at the nature of the difference. Are these truly unified perspectives that, by the very nature of progress, could have been lost? Or are they perspectives that, like those of today's exact natural sciences, could only be gained *through* progress? Are these matters about which the era of progress itself is in agreement and clarity, or matters about which it is in conflict and uncertainty? In this regard, the firmly established views of today's natural science do indeed differ greatly from those of a boundless discord...

Whatever nature this progress may take, it will necessarily encounter the inertial resistance <term>inertial resistance: *Beharrungswiderstand*; the tendency of established historical traditions or institutions to resist change and maintain the status quo</term> of the historical principle before it becomes true historical progress. However, if it deserves to break through, it will eventually succeed, propagate, spread, and take root, even if it does not do so on the first attempt—for all first attempts fail—and thus finally prove itself historically. <meta>This page transitions from the historical survival of ideas to the internal psychological mechanisms that drive belief. ## VI.
### The Practical Principle.

---

"Humanity believes what it finds pleasing, useful, or beneficial to believe"—this was the brief expression of the <term>practical motive</term> <note>original: *praktischen Motivs*; the driving force behind a belief that is based on its usefulness or the satisfaction it provides to the believer.</note>.

Now, according to a <term>psychological law</term> <note>original: *psychologischen Gesetze*; the author is referring to what we might today call "wishful thinking" or "cognitive bias," where our desires influence our perception of reality.</note>, we are most inclined to believe in the existence or the imminent arrival of that which we simultaneously believe will please, serve, or benefit us; the belief itself contributes—either directly or through its consequences—to our satisfaction, and the tendency toward this satisfaction involuntarily provides a motive for that belief.

At first glance, one might think such a motive could indeed drive a person to *action*—to create or bring about that which is most satisfying—but not to *belief*, as if the thing were already accomplished. Yet experience teaches us otherwise, even in the matters of daily life.

<note>The author is highlighting a fundamental tension in human nature: while logic suggests we should only believe what is proven, our emotions often lead us to accept as true whatever makes us feel better or more secure.</note>

<vocab>practical principle, psychological law, faith, satisfaction, motive</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the "Practical Principle," arguing that human belief is fundamentally driven by a psychological desire for satisfaction and utility. The author observes that people are naturally inclined to believe things are true simply because those things are beneficial or pleasing to them.</summary>
<keywords>practical principle, psychological law, motivation, belief, satisfaction, utility, wishful thinking</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Practical Principle" introduced on page 82. While the previous page defined the psychological drive to believe what is beneficial, this page provides concrete examples of how this motive leads to self-deception and the success of charlatans.</meta>

->### The Practical Principle.<-
->6 *<-

Let us reflect on how human hopes are largely dictated by one's desires; how a person gladly believes everything that agrees with these wishes, and reluctantly believes what contradicts them. We see how one exerts all their **ingenuity** <note>original: *Scharfsinn*; referring here to the cleverness or mental sharpness used to justify a preferred belief.</note> to find reasons in favor of the former and against the latter, willingly deceiving oneself and allowing oneself to be deceived. Nowhere has it been difficult for a person to find "reasons" when they were looking for them; and the darker the field in which they move—the less that experience allows for a direct refutation—the more room they have for these reasons, or the more easily they believe without any reasons at all.

We sometimes see entire colonies, lured by the false pretenses of adventurers, marching toward their own ruin in distant lands; it is the **practical motive** <note>original: *praktische Motiv*; the drive to satisfy a need or desire through belief.</note> that leads them toward this destruction. They hope for and believe that the future and the far-off distance will provide the desired things that the present does not offer. This is why lotteries, treasure hunters, miracle doctors, alchemists, and charlatans of all kinds do such good business; they exploit the practical motive of humanity, which inclines a person toward belief all the more easily the more advantageous the thing to be believed appears to them. And whoever promises the most outbids the others.

The very same force, however, which proves to have such great power in the realm of **superstition**, proves the same—indeed, even greater—

->### The Practical Principle.<-
->6 *<-

...power in the realm of faith in the highest and ultimate things <note>original: *höchsten und letzten Dinge*; a reference to eschatology, or the final matters of soul and cosmos.</note>, so that one can indeed ask from the outset whether it is not also an even greater superstition. The conditions for the effectiveness of the motive are the most favorable here, and thus its achievements are also the greatest in this domain.

For not only are these objects of faith the most obscure, most exceeding and surpassing our experience; not only is the conclusion here the most uncertain and the range of opinions the widest, but belief—and the way that belief is shaped—also has the greatest and deepest influence on a person’s current satisfaction, as well as the most important **practical consequences**. In short, for the **practical motive** <note>the psychological drive to believe what is beneficial or comforting rather than what is strictly proven.</note>, this is in every respect the most effective point of attack and the most fertile ground. Where human help no longer suffices, one longs for divine help; and because one longs for it, one believes in it, and continues to believe even when it does not come despite all prayer. One simply calls upon another belief for help: that what God does not give in this life, He will give in a future life; and so one also believes in another life. Who can refute our faith? Where there is no proof, there is also no refutation. Thus, faith can take its course undisturbed, and one belief can heighten and support another. What a person would most like to see and the least...

->### The Practical Principle.<-

...sees the least, he most prefers to believe, and in religious faith, he sets no limits for himself in this regard.

Thus Paul says (Hebrews 11:1): "faith is a certain confidence in what one hopes for, and a lack of doubt in what one does not see." — And Pascal says roughly (*Pensées* p. 243): reason can decide nothing as to whether a God exists or not. From this perspective, one can wager whether there is a God or not.

<margin>Red vertical line in margin highlighting the passage on Pascal's wager</margin>
But one must wager that there is a God, and live in this faith, because by doing so one wins everything if God truly exists, but loses nothing if God does not truly exist; whereas in the wager that God does not exist, one wins nothing if one is right, and loses everything if one is wrong. — And Cato in Cicero's work (*On Old Age*, chapter 23): "But if I am mistaken in this, that I believe the souls of men to be immortal, I am mistaken gladly; nor do I wish this error, in which I delight while I live, to be torn away from me." <note>original: "quodsi in hoc erro, quod animos hominum immortales esse credam, lubenter erro, nec mihi hunc errorem, quo delector, dum vivo, extorqueri volo."</note>

They all characterize faith by its **practical motive** <note>the idea that belief is driven by human needs and benefits rather than abstract logic</note>; and while it expresses itself consciously here, it works unconsciously everywhere.

In fact, without the practical motive, all **theoretical motives** <note>logical or evidence-based reasons for belief</note> would not have much effect; meanwhile, in the service and following of the practical motive, they have the easiest time, and often enough naively confess during their presentation that they are not actually needed, and that they are

->### The Practical Principle.<-

...not to need them. It is already sufficient that one needs faith, and for that, it is sufficient that one believes in something useful.

If faith in God did not serve us in some way, how would it occur to us to seek an invisible God above us, around us, and within us? How would we succeed in finding Him? Because we need God, we seek Him and find Him—and we find reasons for it: bad ones, good ones, or we simply demand no other reason than that we need Him. But without needing Him, we would not find Him, because we would not seek Him; and many a person who had lost Him found Him again, simply because they needed Him. Once we have found God, we also shape our conceptions of Him in the way that satisfies us most, without regard for other types of reasoning.

It is remarkable what power the **practical motive** <note>the idea that human needs, desires, and moral requirements are the primary drivers of belief</note> demonstrates in this regard. If we look around the world, we see **evil** in a thousandfold forms, in the widest distribution, and often ruling with overwhelming force: physical and moral evil, plague, famine, war, the devastation of water and fire; we see calamity inflicted upon the righteous and the unrighteous alike; we see the most pious prayers go unanswered; we see innocent children bear the punishment for the guilt of their parents; we see the gluttons and the violent in possession of wealth and...

This "practical motive" persists even when faced with the overwhelming presence of worldly suffering and injustice.</summary>

->### The Practical Principle.<-

...power, see the truth persecuted; indeed, what do we not see that clearly strikes the eye and speaks against the existence of a simultaneously almighty, all-wise, and all-good God. And yet we see the doctrine of the almighty, all-wise, and all-good God proclaimed everywhere in schools and from pulpits, and thousands upon thousands of people basing their last, only, and best comfort on faith in such a God. Why? Because man needs this comfort.

It is no different with faith in an afterlife <note>original: *Jenseits*, literally the "beyond" or "yonder side"</note>. Everything we see most sharply contradicts this faith; for everything to which the life of the soul was visibly attached decays with death. Consciousness already vanishes when the blood in the brain merely stagnates, and remains gone as long as it is stagnant. And both, blood and brain, rot completely in death. The eye with which we see, the ear with which we hear—what becomes of them? But man wants to live on, to feel on, to think on, to progress; he needs faith in an afterlife as comfort for the sufferings of this life <note>original: *Diesseits*, literally "this side" or the material world</note> and as a goal-setter for his path through this life, as an enticement toward the good and a deterrent against evil in this world. And so the desire to live, the need to live in hope and to maintain a good direction in life, drives him toward faith beyond death.

->### The Practical Principle.<-

No one wants to miss finding again those whom he loved here; no one wants to give up the memory of the "now" entirely; no one wants to see his unmerited sufferings go unrequited. One person wishes himself on the sun, another on Venus, a third would like to make a journey through all the heavens; where desire leans, faith leans also. To most, however, nothing in the visible heavens nor on earth is good enough for the good, nor terrible enough for the wicked; so they believe in Paradise and Hell; and every nation hopes in Paradise for that which it deems best, and creates a fear of that which it deems worst.

Thus Falconer <note>likely referring to William Falconer (1744–1824), a physician who wrote on the influence of climate and geography on human character and religion</note> made the peculiar observation that in hot countries, where one suffers much from the heat, Hell is always imagined as a hot place; in cold countries, on the other hand, where cold makes itself felt more as an evil, it is imagined as a cold place. And if not everywhere, the observation holds true on average.

The ancient Scandinavians, to whom the lust for battle and glory surpassed everything, but who also knew how to appreciate the joys of the feast and beautiful women, painted their Valhalla <note>original: *Walhalla*, the majestic hall in Norse mythology where heroes slain in battle are received</note> accordingly; there, they are still fighting, eating

->### The Practical Principle.<-

...and drinking, and the Valkyries <note>original: *Walkyren*, female figures in Norse mythology who choose who may die in battle and lead them to Valhalla</note>, beautiful maidens, offer the filled horn.

When the missionaries described the Christian heaven to the Greenlanders <note>Inuit peoples of Greenland</note> instead of their own heaven, they replied to them: “Your heaven, your spiritual joys, may be good enough for you; but for us, that would be boring. We must have seals, fish, and birds; for our soul can exist as little without them as our body can. We would not find these things in your heaven, therefore we want to leave your heaven to you and the worthless part of the Greenlanders; but we want to go down to Torngarsuk <note>a powerful spirit or deity in Inuit mythology, often associated with a realm of abundance under the sea</note>; there we will find an abundance of everything without the slightest effort.” (From Prichard’s *Natural History* <note>referring to James Cowles Prichard, a 19th-century ethnologist</note>).

In contrast, Plato <note>the influential ancient Greek philosopher</note> believed that an investigation of eternal truths and the free, unhindered contemplation of the immeasurable celestial bodies and all the wonders of nature—which are either unknown or at least enigmatic to us—would bring bliss to pure, liberated souls.

Plato, too, like the others, believed what he desired; the only difference was that he desired something higher, nobler, and better than the Greenlanders.

But whoever thinks they have no need for God and the afterlife <note>original: *Jenseits*, meaning the world beyond</note> usually does not believe in them either; and in general, we see that those who deny both also strive to show that we do not need either, even if this—

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "practical principle" of belief, moving from the individual's desire for a pleasant afterlife (discussed on page 89) to the social and political utility of religious faith. It explores how belief is maintained and propagated through both selfish and noble motives by influential groups like priests and rulers.</meta>

->### The Practical Principle.<-

...will always remain mere exceptions; for on the whole, the effect of the practical motive <note>the "practical motive" refers to the human tendency to believe things that are useful, comforting, or beneficial to one's life and social standing</note> outweighs everything else in favor of religious faith.

No less than the creation, maintenance, and shaping of faith within each individual, the propagation of that faith to others stands under the influence of the practical motive; and upon this connection between the practical motive and the historical principle <note>the "historical principle" refers to the weight of tradition, established history, and the passing down of ideas through generations</note> depend the greatest achievements of both.

One does not only believe for oneself what pleases, serves, and benefits <note>original: *frommt*, an archaic term meaning to be of use, benefit, or to serve a pious purpose</note> us, but one also makes others believe what pleases, serves, and benefits us to have them believe. This occurs either according to a selfish motive, insofar as their faith promises advantages to ourselves, or according to a nobler motive, insofar as we consider that faith beneficial to them and, at the same time, consider it beneficial to ourselves to work for their own good. And while both motives often sharply contradict each other in specific instances, they generally and as a whole coincide, insofar as the personal advantage of faith aligns with the general advantage.

Priests find it to their advantage that the people believe in God; for their prestige, their power, and their income depend upon it. Rulers find it to their advantage that the people believe in God, because fear and reverence for a higher power <note>referring to earthly authorities</note> can no longer exist if reverence for the Highest Being <note>God</note> vanishes; because the

->### The Practical Principle.<-

...belief in God gives them the means to present their authority <note>original: *Würde*, meaning dignity or high office</note> as one ordained by God, and the laws they enact as sanctioned by God. They are well aware that the stability of the social order, which they represent and upon which their own security depends, is tied to the preservation of religious faith. Priests and rulers also find it to their advantage that the people believe in an afterlife <note>original: *Jenseits*, literally "the beyond"</note>, so they may entice them with future rewards and deter them through future punishments when the prospect of earthly consequences is no longer sufficient. It is primarily these leaders who have painted Heaven and Hell with the most enchanting or terrifying colors at their disposal—for no rational reason other than to entice and intimidate—and we know what immense effects have been achieved by this.

Priests and rulers stand at the head of the people; they direct education and organize instruction. Thus, we see even those who do not believe anything themselves making arrangements to ensure that religious faith is maintained and propagated among the people. To many, religion is merely a political institution which they uphold—indifferent to the truth of what is believed—for no other reason than that they find it useful. How many Popes must there have been who believed nothing, and yet strove to root out heresy along with the heretics?

->### The Practical Principle.<-

Napoleon certainly believed in nothing except his star <note>meaning his personal destiny or fate</note> and yet did not let religion fall. And when it had fallen during the time of the Revolution—when only the Cult of Reason <term>Cult of Reason: an atheistic belief system intended to replace Christianity during the French Revolution</term> and Human Rights were valid—the highest representative of this very cult finally knew how to maintain it only by re-establishing religion.

Let us hear what Robespierre said in the Convention during the worst period of the Terror <note>original: *Schreckenszeit*, referring to the "Reign of Terror" (1793–1794) during the French Revolution</note> on May 7, 1794, turning, as it were, against the shadows of the atheistic Hébert and Cloots*):

> “What could move you to proclaim to the people that there is no God? Why is it useful in your eyes to persuade man that a blind force rules over his destiny, and punishes vice and virtue at will? That his soul is but a light breath that vanishes at the gate of the grave? Does the thought of his annihilation instill in him purer and loftier feelings than the thought of his immortality? Does it give him more respect for his equals and for himself? More devotion to the fatherland? More boldness to defy tyranny? More contempt for death and pleasure? You, who mourn a virtuous friend, do you not believe that the most beautiful part of his being has escaped death...”

->### The Practical Principle.<-

"...of God, whom they threw out of the temples? What else but chaos, nothingness, and death!"

The speaker concluded his address with the draft for a **decree** <term>Decree: *Decrete*; a formal order from the revolutionary government with the force of law</term> to the effect:
> "the **French people** <term>French people: *franzöſiſche Volk*</term> recognize the existence of a **Supreme Being** <term>Supreme Being: *Höchstes Wesen*; a deistic concept of God intended to provide a moral foundation for the Republic without the "superstition" of the Catholic Church</term> and the **immortality of the soul** <term>Immortality of the soul: *Unſterblichkeit der Seele*</term>; it recognizes that the most worthy worship of the Supreme Being is the practice of the duties of man.... Thirty-six festivals should be organized to remind man of the deity and the dignity of his being," etc.

In this way, the belief in God and immortality was decreed from above, according to the pure **principle of utility** <term>Principle of Utility: *Nützlichkeitsprincipe*; the philosophical idea that the value of an action or belief is determined by its practical usefulness to society</term>, just like other generally useful institutions. And even today, nothing is more common—and often nothing more just—than the accusation against governments that they favor this or that religious direction for no other reason than because it suits their political interests, allowing the people to be held together and led like a herd.

But now, the **social order** <term>Social order: *geſellſchaftliche Ordnung*</term>, which requires religious faith—the fear of a Highest Being above earthly authorities—does not only benefit rulers and priests; it benefits the people themselves. Or let us rather say, it serves the people’s salvation; provided we understand "salvation" <note>original: *Heil*, which carries the double meaning of spiritual "salvation" and temporal "well-being"</note> as a benefit from the highest perspective, one that is no longer usually called mere "utility." <meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Practical Principle" or the principle of utility regarding religious faith. It shifts from Robespierre's specific decree to a broader historical analysis of how rulers and priests have used both noble and selfish motives to maintain religious belief in society.</meta>

->### The Practical Principle.<-

The idea that higher than temporal and earthly goods <term>Temporal and earthly goods: *zeitliche und irdische Güter*; material or worldly benefits as opposed to spiritual salvation</term> are linked to faith for humanity can also motivate priests and rulers from a nobler motive—and this is at least as often the case as from the baser motive: to maintain faith among the people and take every possible measure to do so. The selfish and nobler motives meet here in their objective in such a way that they are often hardly distinguishable in the outcome.

Alongside the practical motives come the practical means for spreading the faith. More peoples have likely been converted by **fire and sword** <term>Fire and sword: *Feuer und Schwert*; a common historical idiom for military force and violent coercion</term> than by conviction; and even if fire and sword could not implant conviction in the peoples, they could nonetheless ensure historical propagation. History speaks of pagan kings who converted to **Christianity** <term>Christianity: *Christenthum*</term> to obtain a Christian **princess** <term>Princess: *Fürstentochter*; literally "daughter of a prince"</term>; the people had to convert along with them, for better or worse. Even today, princesses convert from one religion to another to turn from princesses into empresses. Priests attach the threat of eternal punishment to unbelief, and the promise of future reward to faithfulness, and thereby not only coerce weak minds but also make strong spirits hesitant in their unbelief. **Heretics** <term>Heretics: *Ketzer*; those whose beliefs deviate from established church doctrine</term> are burned; the **orthodox** <term>Orthodox: *orthodox*; conforming to established, traditional doctrines</term> clergymen receive the best positions. And even if all this has little direct effect in [creating] faith...

<summary>This page examines the practical motives and methods used by authorities to spread religion, noting that noble spiritual goals and selfish political interests often produce the same results. It highlights how coercion, "fire and sword," and social incentives like political marriages or career advancement have historically been used to enforce religious conformity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the "Practical Principle," moving from the role of rulers and priests in maintaining faith to the role of art and imagination in making those beliefs attractive and transmissible across generations.</meta>

96

->### The Practical Principle.<-

...to generate, it achieves a great deal to maintain it historically.

To all these practical motives, from which faith counts its origin and dissemination, is added a lighter one—one that even alleviates the weight of the other motives themselves: the delight with which the creative imagination <term>Imagination: *Phantasie*; in this context, the faculty of creating mental images or art</term> of poets and artists works on the shaping and embellishment of religious ideas, and the easy accessibility they gain through this. After such shaping—whether through beauty or through the stimulation and engagement of the imagination, which finds a pleasure in its own activity even in the horrific <note>The author suggests that even "horrific" religious imagery, such as depictions of judgment or suffering, provides a type of aesthetic engagement or "pleasure" to the viewer</note>—these ideas are able to reproduce in the minds <note>original: *Gemüthern*; refers to the emotional and spiritual disposition of a person</note> of others the same delight from which they were formed. Meanwhile, through their vivid expression, they facilitate comprehension, becoming communicable to large communities all at once and capable of being passed down through generations. Thus, in this too, the historical and practical motives work together.

But what value and significance, one asks again, does the entire faith of humanity then have? A faith which has been largely made and established by priests and rulers for the sake of their own and the people's advantage <term>Advantage: *Vortheil*; implying political or social utility rather than purely spiritual truth</term>, just as it seemed to serve their benefit at the time; which poets and artists then further [shaped] according to their pleasure and for the people’s...

### The Practical Principle.
->Fechner, *Motives of Faith*<-

...embellished with pleasure, as it best pleased them, and in which people find satisfaction for the sake of benefit and pleasure. Is a thing any truer because belief in it benefits us or others, or because it pleases us to believe that it is true? What do utility and pleasantness have to do with truth? Is it not, rather, often the case that the worst, the most undesirable thing, is the truest? If parents believe a child who died in a distant land is still alive, this belief serves to satisfy them; does that make it any truer? Just as one can deter a child from evil through the fear of God, one can also do so through the fear of a <term>Bogeyman: *Popanz*; a frightening figure or "bugbear" used to scare children into obedience</term>; is one belief, then, truer than the other? Is the faith of humanity not really, essentially, only a political institution and poetic machinery, resting upon a fiction which—like the <term>Imaginary in mathematics: Refers to imaginary numbers (like the square root of -1), which Fechner notes are "fictions" that nevertheless allow mathematicians to reach real, useful results</term>—yields useful conclusions, but remains nonetheless something imaginary?

Indeed, one might ask, is not the utility itself, for the sake of which faith is spread, more imaginary than real? And thus, is the fiction upon which its dissemination is based not a double one; or are its advantages at least not outweighed by its disadvantages? In fact, we have already recalled how unbelievers contest the necessity and wholesomeness <note>original: *Heilsamkeit*; meaning the salutariness or "healing" quality of faith for the soul and society</note> of religious faith for humanity itself...

### The Practical Principle.

...tested. <meta>The text begins with the suffix "-ten," likely completing the word "bestreiten" (to contest) from the previous page.</meta> According to these critics, focusing on <term>supersensible things: *übersinnliche Dinge*; realities existing beyond the reach of the physical senses, such as God or the afterlife</term> leads us away from the proper attention to and care for earthly affairs, which is where our lives must actually take place. They argue that religious ideas entice the spirit to sink from rational and provable considerations into hollow daydreams. They claim that a sense of human dignity and a conscience educated in that spirit can replace the impulses one seeks in religious faith. Furthermore, acting out of regard for future reward or future punishment contradicts a pure principle of morality. The horrors that have been committed—and are still committed today—in the name of religion are, for them, poor evidence for the "wholesomeness" of religious faith. 

During the capture of Jerusalem by the **Crusaders**, hundreds of thousands were slaughtered in the name of Christ. The **Thirty Years' War**, a memory of utter horror, was waged in the name of religion. The prisons of the **Inquisition**, the stakes of the heretics, the persecutions of the **Huguenots** <note>French Protestants who were violently persecuted by the Catholic state in the 16th and 17th centuries</note>, the monasteries stuffed with idle monks and nuns, the self-torture of the **fakirs**—all of this, they say, clings to the very faith whose blessing is so highly praised. The benefit to priests and rulers is not denied; but does the people exist merely for their sake?

In all of this, there is at first glance much that seems plausible. To show, however, that an argument can nonetheless be supported by the practical motive, we have two things to...

<meta>This page continues the defense of religious faith against the skeptical arguments raised on the previous page. ### The Practical Principle.

7 *

...to show: first, that the utility of faith is not merely imaginary or outweighed by greater disadvantages; second, that—if not the utility—the **blessing** of faith truly speaks for its truth; in that we understand by **blessing** <note>original: "Segen"</note> simply the greatest, highest, and most reassuring aspect of that which is individually called utility.

Regarding the first point, it can be said in general: first, that all the harm that has spread throughout humanity from religious faith is linked to what is still false in faith, or what has been wrongly made dependent upon it. These disadvantages themselves cannot be a reason to reject faith, but only to improve it—an effect they have indeed exerted on the whole throughout history. Second, however much foolishness, falsehood, or reprehensibility the faith of a people may still contain, it is always far better than if they had none at all; for the disadvantages would not decrease in that case, but increase. Third, the <term>substitutes: *Erſatzmittel*; secular alternatives such as purely rational ethics or scientific materialism</term> proposed to take the place of faith were either only able to arise under faith’s general influence, or they cannot remotely approach or replace its effectiveness.

All this is, of course, as clear as day, so that it hardly needs demonstrating; and all demonstration for those... <note>The text ends abruptly here, mid-sentence.</note>

In the spirit of such a religion, I certainly see charitable institutions, hearts inclined toward mercy, the most self-sacrificing activity for others, the highest achievements of art, a shield of laws over humanity, but nothing of those disadvantages that are blamed on religious faith—disadvantages which, in truth, are only the fault of its temporary and local defects. > way to effect world improvement <term>Weltverbesserung: a 19th-century term for utopian social reform, often used skeptically by traditionalists</term>, and to provide his anti-religious principles—which had found no acceptance in **England**—with a following, permanence, and expansion on **American** soil. He taught that all religions are based on a mere delusion; thus, Christianity too is nothing but a deception, the origin of which is a fable, and which only creates obstacles for pure morality and the development of the human spirit. Since he required no venue for the worship of a God or for meetings for religious purposes, he immediately had the church still found in the place converted into a workshop, and remodeled the adjacent building into a dance and concert hall as well as an apartment for philosophical lectures. Soon, various persons of both sexes were found who shared these beliefs with him, especially since he combined them with a not-exactly-rigorous morality and proclaimed perfect equality and a community of goods. Enthusiasts, vagabonds, swindlers, and lazy good-for-nothings registered as members of the society and enjoyed themselves for a while at the common expense. Those with better intentions, who had consumed the little money they had brought with them, found themselves deceived in their hopes, became morose and dissatisfied with their lot, and resigned from the society." <note>This passage describes Robert Owen (1771–1858), a Welsh social reformer who founded the utopian community of New Harmony, Indiana, in 1825. ### The Practical Principle.

if indeed free communities <term>freie Gemeinden: independent religious groups that emerged in 19th-century Germany, often rejecting traditional dogma or state-church control</term>, miserably enough to be sure, exist here and there within the bosom of a larger community of faith—and how much longer will they continue to exist?—it is only in the way that small cavities full of gunpowder can exist within a solid environment. <note>The author uses a volatile metaphor here: these secular or independent groups are compared to pockets of gunpowder, suggesting they are inherently unstable or even dangerous to the surrounding "solid" religious structure of society.</note>

Even in a time, among a people, where no single individual believed anything anymore, the preservation of the forms of faith within the State could still serve to provide a—granted, weak—foothold for it; however, the feeling of this weakness would lead, time and again, to the strengthening of faith and thus to a stronger internal hold, and has often led to this in times that seemed to approach that extreme.

<margin>+</margin>
<margin>|</margin>
Certainly, just as the blessing of a true and good religious faith exceeds every other blessing, so too are the disadvantages of a false faith greater, more far-reaching, and more deeply felt than those of any other belief; and this is what allows one to lay so much blame at the door of religious faith. But even with the worst religious faith that still deserves the name, I say that the advantage on the whole is greater than the disadvantage in the particular; it is better for a people to believe in something at all—something that offers a foothold, a hope, a help, a bond above that which is earthly and temporal—than to believe in nothing that cannot be shown and proven. <note>This reflects a common 19th-century conservative viewpoint: that social "cohesion" (the "bond") provided by religion is more important for the stability of the nation than the scientific "proof" of that religion’s tenets.</note>

### The Practical Principle.

The British government on the Gold Coast <note>The Gold Coast was a British colony in West Africa, known today as Ghana.</note> once took the opportunity, in a large assembly of the people, to expose the system of lies and deception of their fetish priests <term>fetish priests: a 19th-century European term for traditional West African religious leaders who mediated with the spirit world through sacred objects</term> through striking and obvious evidence. “The impression,” a report on the matter concludes, “that it made on their spirits was a powerful one. It was impossible to see the only support, the staff of these people, so broken without having pity for them. Many left the castle sad and dejected, and they were heard saying to one another: what can we do now in sickness and in need? To whom can we turn for help? Our gods are no gods, and the priests have deceived us...” (From Cruikshank’s *Gold-Coast*, *Ausland* 1854, No. 9). <note>The author is quoting from Brodie Cruikshank’s "Eighteen Years on the Gold Coast of Africa," which was serialized or reviewed in the German journal *Das Ausland* in 1854.</note>

Thus, even in their quite raw belief, these people found a foothold—one which they could certainly exchange for an unspeakably better one, but which they could not do without without feeling completely broken. What, then, will a better faith be able to achieve in the same sense!

If we look closer, the advantages, the blessing, and the salvation that come from a good faith—and to protect and support only such a faith can ultimately be the task—are generally twofold from a twofold perspective. On the one hand, the community finds in such faith a point of unification; on the other, every individual finds a point of support and a guiding goal; on the one hand, there lies

<margin>Red vertical line</margin>

<margin>Red vertical mark</margin>
However, these advantages are very easily misunderstood because people do not see or look for them where they actually lie. The individual pays little attention to the advantages that benefit the whole—in which he himself nonetheless participates daily, even if he does not think of it for a single day—because the benefit does not manifest itself as something specifically for him alone. And many a person does not recognize the great advantage that faith could provide him even in the particulars of his life, because it does not provide him with even the smallest material benefit; yet faith can only provide the greatest benefit insofar as it renounces providing even the smallest. For as a faith in the highest and final things, it grants advantages only according to those highest and final relations; one must not seek them in what is low, narrow, short, or small.

### The Practical Principle.

...even the building of human society would collapse if one withdrew the religious foundation from it, which truly reaches so deep that most who live in the building do not see it at all.

I once read a story; I no longer remember it exactly, nor does it matter. King Alexander received a gift of three hunting dogs of the noblest breed from a barbarian king. He let the first one into his menagerie <term>menagerie: "Thierzwinger," an enclosed area for keeping wild animals</term> and set a deer before it; the dog remained lying calmly. Alexander, in his anger at the sluggish animal, ordered the dog to be killed. The same happened with the second dog; the third he spared for the time being. When the barbarian king heard of it, he said to Alexander: "You let a deer into the enclosure; let a lion or a tiger in, and you will see what kind of dog you have." Thus, religious faith remains idle during life's trifles, and awakens to all the greater vigor <note>The original German "Thatkraft" suggests an active, driving energy for deeds</note>, the greater the stakes become.

He who believes that there is a God who—not in the immediate moment, but in the course of time—directs everything for the best, who finally compels and banishes the resisting forces, and at last makes even evil itself a means for the better; [he who believes] in a God who, however great the evil may be, has even greater means—reaching over from this world <term>this world: "Diesseits," referring to the current earthly life</term> into the hereafter <term>hereafter: "Jenseits," the life beyond or spiritual realm</term>—to turn it, to extinguish it, to reconcile it, and a will and a wisdom that [go] hand in hand with this...

In faith in such a Master, we see many people who trust in God go through thick and thin, certain of a good destination if they walk with a good spirit, and who lie down every evening with the consciousness and feeling of being in God’s hand, finally dying in such faith. A sublime sight! Contrast this with the one who, in the feeling of his human dignity but without faith in a higher protection or a future existence, lies down and dies. A sorrowful sight!

...faith that there is a God with a consciousness of everything and above everything, can be represented by a belief in any unconscious world order <term>world order: "Weltordnung," a philosophical concept of a universe governed by laws but lacking a personal divine mind</term>, no matter how purposeful; the peak of all those feelings would thereby be lost.

<margin><note>vertical red line in margin</note></margin>
And just as no surrogate can be found for a conscious God who stands in relation to our own consciousness, neither can one find a substitute for the belief in a conscious future life that stands in relation to our current life—at least not one that could replace it from a practical standpoint, or that would be capable of achieving even remotely the same results. Using the example of the **Assassins** <note>The "Assassins" (Nizari Ismailis) were often used in 19th-century philosophy as a historical example of how absolute faith in a promised paradise could motivate extreme, even "terrible," earthly actions</note>, we saw what the worst kind of faith in an afterlife is capable of producing in terms of effects—the worst effects, certainly, but also the strongest. The thought of **posthumous fame** <term>posthumous fame: "Nachruhm," the secular hope that one's name or works will live on after death</term> can achieve something; I do not deny it. But how weak is the thought that others will think of us—the eternally dead—with love, respect, and veneration, when compared to the thought that we ourselves will think; that we will live again with those with whom we live now, and that we ourselves will share in the consequences of our own actions.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the practical motives of faith. Having argued on the previous page that a belief in a conscious God and an afterlife provides the strongest practical "interest" or motivation for moral action, he now addresses the common philosophical objection that "truth" and "human interest" (utility) are separate or even conflicting pursuits.</meta>

# The Practical Principle.

...things? One must seek the truth impartially and without regard for the consequences of what one finds; only thus can one hope to find it. Where interest <term>interest: "Interesse," here meaning personal benefit, utility, or what is advantageous to a person</term> comes into play, man becomes blind.

So I hear many say, and research often proceeds in this spirit, for the sake of the interest of truth, moving away from everything that man’s true interest desires, as if the two could ever conflict with one another. And at other times, one indulges again in lofty speeches about the harmony of goodness, beauty, and truth in their ultimate foundations; yet they remain mere speeches, because one lacks and fails to see the perspective and the core of this harmony.

To be sure, where man’s interest comes into play, he becomes blind to the highest truths; but his blindness does not lie in the fact that he follows his interest in opposition to the truth, but rather that he does not follow his interest *enough*. He follows short-term, narrow, low interests at the expense of the larger, higher, broader, and more lasting interests, in which ultimately the support, the security, and the foundation of those lesser interests rest; or, he follows his individual interest in opposition to the general interest, which encompasses his and everyone’s interests in a coherent whole. Only in this way does he fall into conflict with the truth.

Everywhere else we find, after all, that the most correct knowledge <term>knowledge: "Kenntniß," referring to factual or theoretical acquaintance with reality</term> of what is, also serves man practically...

<note>The text ends mid-sentence, suggesting the argument continues on the following page to explain how "correct knowledge" of reality serves as a practical guide for human behavior.</note>

<summary>Fechner argues against the idea that seeking "truth" must be divorced from human "interest," suggesting instead that true harmony exists when we pursue our highest, most universal interests rather than narrow, selfish ones.</summary>
<keywords>truth, interest, utility, ethics, harmony of transcendentals, goodness, beauty, individual vs. general interest</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument from the previous section, asserting that the utility of a belief is not a distraction from truth, but rather its most reliable indicator. Fechner is arguing for a "practical principle" of verification: if a belief leads to the best outcomes for all of humanity over the long term, it possesses the highest claim to truth.</meta>

# The Practical Principle

...serves him best, leads him best. Why should we not conversely conclude that what serves man best and leads him best is also what is truest? We find this principle confirmed all the more, the more extensively we seek its confirmation, and the more we move from the individual to the whole; thus, we can expect its most certain confirmation when we apply it to the entirety of existence. 

A single error can satisfy an individual person for a time; however, the further an error spreads—the more people feel, think, and act in its spirit, the further and deeper its practical consequences reach, and the more permanently it develops within those consequences—the greater and more profound its disadvantages become for everyone. This is not wild metaphysics, empty conceptual games <note>original: "Begriffsspiel"</note>, or clumsy dogmatism, but simply the clear expression of a universally valid fact. The opposite must be said of the truth. 

Accordingly, truth will most certainly be found in that which, if accepted as true by all, so determines the willing, thinking, and feeling of all that the most healthy and blessed consequences for everyone, and for all of humanity, arise from it. Such a faith we call the best, and therefore the best faith must also be the truest. Insofar as the best faith is an ideal for humanity, a faith will approach the ideal of truth more closely the more it approaches the ideal of goodness.

<sig>8*</sig>

<vocab>The practical principle, truth, error, metaphysics, dogmatism, humanity, faith, ideal of goodness</vocab>

<summary>Fechner argues that the truth of a belief is inextricably linked to its moral and practical utility, suggesting that a faith which produces the most beneficial outcomes for humanity is the one most likely to be true.</summary>
<keywords>practical principle, utility of faith, pragmatism, collective well-being, ideal of goodness, truth and morality</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s exploration of the "Practical Principle." He is testing his theory—that the utility of a belief indicates its truth—by looking at the consequences of false beliefs versus the historical persistence of faith in God and the afterlife.</meta>

### The Practical Principle

Indisputably, the belief parents might cherish—that a child who died in a distant land is still alive—does not become truer simply because it comforts the parents. But does this belief, through this single benefit <note>original: "Leistung," meaning a functional achievement or output</note>, truly possess the character of truth demanded by the practical principle, which is never satisfied by isolated benefits alone?

On the contrary: if all people were to think and act indefinitely <note>original: "ins Unbestimmte," implying without limits or universally</note> upon the belief that the deceased were still alive, overwhelming disadvantages would arise. These disadvantages would grow the longer and more extensively people acted upon that belief, and even the parents' initial comfort would not hold up in the long run. This failure is due to the belief's inherent falsehood. 

Even greater disadvantages would necessarily arise if all of humanity were to think and act indefinitely upon the belief that a God exists who does not truly live, and that they themselves will live in the future when they will actually be dead. The comfort that humanity drew from this for a time would likewise fail to endure. Just as the error of believing in a living God and an eternal life for everyone would be monstrously larger than the error of believing in a single living person and their temporary life, the disadvantages for everyone would also have to be monstrously greater. 
<margin>|</margin>
The more such a belief spread and the deeper it took root, the more these disadvantages would have to grow. But quite the opposite is true: the widest distribution, the longest duration, and the greatest practical effectiveness of faith in God and the hereafter brings...

<vocab>Faith, practical principle, God, hereafter, parents, disadvantages, error, eternal life</vocab>

<summary>Fechner argues that while a false belief might provide temporary comfort to an individual, a universal false belief would eventually cause massive social harm; conversely, the long-term historical benefits of faith suggest its underlying truth.</summary>
<keywords>Practical Principle, utility of faith, religious pragmatism, grief, social consequences of belief, existence of God, immortality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s argument for the "Practical Principle." On the previous page, he argued that if the belief in God and the afterlife were false, its universal adoption would have led to disaster rather than the stability and comfort seen throughout history.</meta>

### The Practical Principle

...the greatest blessing, replaceable by nothing else; and the longer it has existed, the more it gains permanence. <note>Fechner is concluding the thought that the long-term utility of a belief in God is proof of its truth.</note>

To contradict the previous principle <term>The Practical Principle: the idea that a belief's long-term practical success is a reliable indicator of its truth</term> means to contradict the most universal experience we can have, as well as every rational interpretation we can give to it.

Whatever power may have created humanity, the fact remains: it created them in such a way that they can survive and develop prosperously, but only if they behave in accordance with the context <note>original: "Zusammenhange," referring to the interconnected fabric of the universe</note> in which they were created. If they do not, they are driven back to it by the disadvantages they face. Their own spirit must help them to behave this way by basing its calculations on what actually is and will be within this context. Does anyone truly believe that this power created man in such a way that he can only survive and develop properly if—exceptionally, in regards to that power itself and man's ultimate goals—he bases his calculations on something that neither is nor will be? Could it even be in the nature of a spiritless power <note>original: "geistlosen Macht," likely referring to a purely materialistic or atheistic view of nature</note> to create creatures with such a constitution that they cannot properly exist without the belief in a creative spirit? It would be too absurd; yet some take this absurdity for wisdom.

I once read how the larva of the male stag beetle <term>Hirschhornkäfer: The European Stag Beetle (Lucanus cervus)</term>, during its pupation, [builds] a larger casing

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s argument for the "Practical Principle." ### The Practical Principle

...builds <note>Fechner is referring to the larva of the stag beetle, which constructs a cocoon larger than its current size.</note> it larger than it needs to fill with its curled-up body, so that the horns that will one day develop also have room. What does the larva know of its future life, of its future horns? Just as little as we know of our future life and our future way of being within it; yet it already prepares its house for it now, and without doing so, it would be unable to develop properly until that time. Does one believe that the same power that created the stag beetle and man placed truth in the beetle’s instinct and a lie in man’s faith <note>original: "Glaube"</note>, which allows him to build and plan his current life in the direction of the future one more than would otherwise be the case? This is a faith that develops as necessarily within humanity and is as necessary for humanity’s development as that instinct develops in the larva and is necessary for its development.

To be sure, the belief in immortality does not develop as necessarily in every individual human being as instinct does in every individual stag beetle. But in humanity as a whole, it develops just as necessarily; and it is in this very fact that it stands above instinct: it develops out of the context <note>original: "Zusammenhange"</note> of conscious life regarding the common endpoint and goal of this life, which has the same ultimate foundation as the life of the beetle and its instinct.

### The Practical Principle

After all this, I summarize as follows: first the practical motive, then the practical argument *).

Man believes in the existence <note>original: "Dasein"</note> of God and what is connected with it, because this faith is as advantageous, beneficial, blessing-bringing, and practically necessary for the individual person as it is for human society 
<margin>x</margin> 
as a whole; because neither the individual nor humanity as a whole can prosperously exist or prosperously develop without it. Through it, the individual finds a support <note>original: "Anhalt"</note> reaching to the most extreme cases of life, 
<margin>L</margin> 
human society finds its most general and binding hold <note>original: "Halt"</note>, and indeed every earthly human interest finds its summit and goal in it. In that man partly feels this unconsciously and partly perceives it consciously, the individual is partly driven to partake in the advantages of faith through the act of believing. Furthermore, there arises an impulse for those who preside over larger and smaller circles of human society—such as parents, teachers, and rulers <note>original: "Regenten"</note>—to propagate it in these circles, to maintain it, and even to spread it beyond them. Finally, for those whose spiritual power <note>original: "Geisteskraft"</note>, intelligence, and goodness lead the way for others, there is an impulse to further develop faith in such a way that it becomes even more rich in blessing—
