# The Practical Principle

...becomes more easily accessible; in what ways this motive interacts with the historical one and plays into its hands. In short, man believes in God and what is connected with it in religion because he needs the belief in it, and the belief in it serves man.

That this faith is a true one, however, is grounded as follows with the following consequences:

1) Every erroneous or deficient premise <note>original: "Voraussetzung"</note> proves itself to be such by the fact that, when accepted as true, it brings about disadvantages or damages human happiness through the influence it gains over our thinking, feeling, and acting; it entangles us in disagreeable moods and perverse actions, which partly result in immediate displeasure <note>original: "Unlust," a psychological term Fechner used to describe the opposite of pleasure or satisfaction</note> and dissatisfaction, and partly bring about indirect consequences of displeasure. In contrast, the truth of a premise proves itself as such through the opposite of all this. This principle is validated all the more, the greater the influence error or truth gains over our feeling, thinking, and acting, the larger the circle of people it encompasses, and the longer the duration over which it extends; whereas an error, provided it does not significantly interfere with our other feeling, thinking, and acting, may indeed appear satisfying and even useful for a single individual or a small circle of people for a short time. Now, however, it is shown.

<summary>Fechner argues that the truth of a religious belief is evidenced by its practical utility; just as errors eventually cause social and personal dissatisfaction, true beliefs are those that consistently promote human happiness and stable development over time.</summary>

# The Practical Principle

...precisely that religious faith, apart from the theoretical satisfaction it is able to grant, also brings with it all the greater, more important, and more far-reaching advantages—and unbelief, by contrast, disadvantages—for humanity and the individual, the further or deeper this faith or unbelief determinatively intervenes in the human soul <note>original: "Gemüth," referring to the seat of one's inner disposition, feelings, and character</note> and way of acting, and the longer the duration over which it extends. This is precisely why unbelief cannot maintain its validity in the long run across a wide circle of people. Thus, the faith that the objects of religious belief exist carries the mark of truth within itself.

<margin>|</margin>
2) The more specific formation of this faith falls under the same principle. Insofar as it is found that a particular form of religious faith contributes all the more to the happiness of humanity the more, the longer, and in the wider circle it gains influence over feeling, thinking, and acting, then this form—or aspect of the faith's formation—is to be regarded as true. In the opposite case, it is to be seen as false or deficient, so that ultimately only that faith can be considered the truest which is the most beneficial <note>original: "heilsamsten," meaning most wholesome or salutary for spiritual and physical well-being</note> to humanity in the totality of its relationships.

3) In the course of the development of faith, it can certainly happen, and has often happened, that faith—partly for the temporary advantage of individuals, and partly from an unsound <note>original: "untriftiger," meaning invalid, weak, or poorly grounded</note> view of what benefits the whole—

<summary>Fechner argues that the truth of a religious belief is evidenced by its practical utility; if a belief consistently fosters human happiness and social stability over time, it must be considered true. He concludes that the "truest" faith is the one that proves most beneficial to humanity's overall well-being.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that the practical utility and moral benefit of a faith serve as indicators of its truth. It discusses how narrow, individual interests eventually give way to a "general interest" through the testing of time and experience.</meta>

# The Practical Principle

...partly by virtue of an apparent conflict with theoretical reasons, takes on erroneous and thus detrimental <note>original: "unzuträgliche," meaning disadvantageous or unwholesome</note> forms for humanity. Man generally begins by having **particular interests** and by considering, declaring, and even implanting and imposing upon others the faith shaped thereby as the best. But in proportion as the advantages of the true and disadvantages of the false reach further and further in time and space, they affect—as already shown with the historical principle—all individuals who hold the true or false faith more and more heavily. They confirm those in **correct knowledge** <note>original: "Erkenntniß"</note>, bring others back from the false, and help or hinder further dissemination, so that ultimately only that faith can remain which best and most perfectly links all individual interests into a **general interest**.

4) Insofar as that which is to be considered the best for man is what is most suited to secure and promote man's satisfaction, **happiness** <note>original: "Glück"</note>, and well-being—not just in individual relations, for a short time, or for individual fractions, but in all aspects of human nature, for the totality of humanity, for unlimited duration, with a view to all consequences—the truest faith, established in the aforementioned way, can simultaneously be called the best. Generally, the conclusion of the **truth** of a faith can be drawn from its **goodness**.

**Vocabulary:** <term>Humanity</term>, <term>Particular Interests</term>, <term>Faith</term>, <term>Knowledge</term>, <term>General Interest</term>, <term>Happiness</term>, <term>Truth</term>

<summary>Fechner argues that while humans initially adopt beliefs based on narrow self-interest, the long-term consequences of these beliefs eventually filter out the false ones, leading to a universal faith. He concludes that the practical "goodness" and utility of a belief are valid proofs of its objective truth.</summary>

# The Practical Principle

5) With this, the development and formation of religious ideas simultaneously enters into the most harmonious and practical connection with the shaping of morality and of life as a whole. This is because the tendencies of morality and life also aim to assert and maintain that which is most beneficial and wholesome <note>original: "heilsamsten und gedeihlichsten," suggesting that which is both healing and conducive to prosperous growth</note> for humanity *). However, following the shape they take under the influence of the practical principle, religious ideas themselves emerge as the most important conditions for a flourishing development of morality and life. This is because the very viewpoint of their formation is to regard as valid that which, from the highest perspective, must have the most universal and thoroughgoing beneficial influence on the whole of human affairs.

In general, if anything has been proven through experience, it is the practical argument for faith. Just as we hold the law of gravity <note>original: "Gravitationsgesetz." Fechner frequently used analogies from physics to justify his "inner physics" or spiritual philosophy.</note> to be valid even in places where our experience does not reach—simply because it has proven true as far as experience *does* reach—we may also consider that practical argument valid without limitation. In this way, what the theoretical conclusion lacks in exactness is replaced by the exactness of a practical one.

->## The Practical Principle<-

...is able to further increase its practical effectiveness; it has the future because it has the truth, and the truth because it has the future.

And just as every philosophy will be rejected that offers a stone instead of the bread of faith <note>An allusion to the biblical passage in Matthew 7:9: "Or which of you, if his son asks for bread, will give him a stone?" Fechner suggests that people need spiritual nourishment, not cold, hard logic.</note>, every philosophy will be rejected that offers an empty hand. And are there not entire systems that know nothing more to say of God than that nothing can be said of him, and which command belief in him for practical reasons; yet, in doing so, they withdraw from faith everything that makes it practical. Or they even demand from the practical principle the very content of faith that they themselves ought to provide to the practical—which, on its own, can only pose empty demands. Such systems, however (Kant <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804), the Enlightenment philosopher who argued that while we cannot "know" God through reason, we must "postulate" His existence to make sense of morality.</note>, Herbart <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a philosopher and educator who followed Kant's lead in separating theoretical knowledge from the practical "claims" of religion.</note>), are the beginning and end of today’s philosophy.

To be sure, where is philosophy to obtain the content of faith with which it must fulfill this practical demand? I think: from the same place where faith has always obtained it—and from somewhere it must surely have it. We shall seek it in the following principle.

Against the belief in a personal, conscious persistence <note>original: "Fortdauer." This refers to the survival of the individual soul or consciousness after death.</note>, materialism possesses reasons that lie ready at hand and on the surface. They are excellent, except for one bitter point; I would believe these reasons myself—and it remains a matter of faith whether we shall not exist just as much as whether we shall—if they did not [run] against the...

## VII.

### The Theoretical Principle *)

The brief expression of the theoretical principle was this: one believes that for which one finds **determining grounds** in **experience** and **reason**.
Now, one can seek to demonstrate rationally that historical and practical reasons justify our religious faith, just as was done in the preceding sections—albeit in a different manner than in the traditional historical and practical arguments. But after everything that [has been said] regarding historical and 

---

*) I have already treated this principle in the work *On the Question of the Soul* <note>original: "Ueber die Seelenfrage" (1861), where Fechner argues for the existence of souls in plants and the world-at-large.</note> (Chapter VII) and implemented it throughout the entirety of *Zend-Avesta* <note>Fechner's 1851 work *Zend-Avesta: Or on the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter*, which is his primary philosophical treatment of the "day-view" of the world as living and soulful.</note> to such an extent that a mere reference to them might suffice, were it not the case here that I must present this principle in connection with and as a mutual supplement to the other two principles <note>The three principles Fechner usually refers to are the historical, the practical, and the theoretical.</note>, whereby this current writing itself becomes a supplement to those earlier works. However, one can only expect to find the same ideas here that were found there, expressed in different turns of phrase and elaborations; given the firm standing of this principle, it permits nothing else but different turns and elaborations.

<summary>Fechner defines the theoretical principle of belief as the requirement for faith to be grounded in both empirical experience and logical reason. He notes that this discussion serves as a necessary supplement to his previous major works, "Zend-Avesta" and "On the Question of the Soul."</summary>

**Reason** and **experience** we have named as the levers of this principle. **Reason**—for insofar as the highest and ultimate realities, in their capacity as the highest and ultimate, are not objects of direct experience, and because another source of their knowledge <note>original: *Erkenntniß*; the philosophical process of attaining certain knowledge or cognition.</note> is sought beyond the historical and practical principles, some form of inference <note>The German *Schluss* implies a logical conclusion or deduction drawn from evidence.</note> is required to reach them. **Experience**—for insofar as they are indeed the highest and ultimate aspects of reality, experience is required as the foundation for that inference. Through logic and mathematics alone, one can arrive neither at a faith in **God** and **immortality**, nor at anything other than a playful conceptual proof <note>original: *begriffsspielerischen Beweise*; Fechner is critiquing abstract logical proofs that lack empirical substance or "meat," treating them as mere intellectual games.</note> and empty notions of them. ...the shaping of faith on a theoretical basis <note>Fechner is discussing how we build a rational system of belief as opposed to one based purely on emotion or tradition.</note>, that one proceeds too little from experienceable reality—a fault of modern philosophy and theology—or that one remains standing too much upon it—a fault of the **heathens** and **materialists**. By both paths, one loses either God entirely or the full content and scope of God, and finally prefers to throw away the whole principle that brought about the loss; but only its reprehensible application is to be rejected.

What is commonly called **religious experience** is essentially only the internal experience of the practical impulses and effects of faith <note>original: *praktischen Antriebe*; the inner motivations or "drives" that lead a person toward religious feeling.</note>. How a ground for faith should be based on this was shown in the previous principle itself, from an empirical perspective. Here, however, **experience** is asserted in a broader sense and a different direction. This is not about an experience of the proofs for the existence of the highest and ultimate things—which lie in our need for faith in them and the consequences of a completed faith—but rather an experience which, lying in the most general facts of **existence**, regardless of whether we might wish to believe, already believe, or not, leads to faith and can thus come to the aid of that religious experience which is often asserted only too one-sidedly.

### The Theoretical Principle

...moments <note>The text begins mid-word, likely "Momente" (elements or factors), continuing from the previous page's discussion on the formation of faith.</note> occurs, it is necessary to consider the motives that are to be concluded and perfected within it.

If we look at how they are actually found, they lie as far as possible from all metaphysics upon which the philosopher might wish to base faith, if he intends to support it for others. No starting point from the abstract concepts of **Being**, the **Absolute**, the **I**, the **thing-in-itself** <term>thing-in-itself: *Ding an sich*; a concept from Immanuel Kant referring to reality as it exists independent of human perception.</term>, the most perfect and thus most real being <note>original: *vollkommensten und hiemit realsten Wesens*; this refers to the "ens realissimum," a traditional philosophical designation for God.</note>, simple things, absolute causality, or the self-actualizing primal possibility has ever led humanity to a belief in God. All such things have only followed after faith, without ever being able to catch up to faith again in their consequences, unless they allowed themselves to be dragged along by it; and in many cases, they have led away from it entirely.

Rather, what has been added as a supplement to the historical and practical motives to generate and shape faith is an inference <note>original: *Schluss*; in this context, it refers to a logical conclusion or a mental leap from the known to the unknown.</note> which humans use and draw daily in life, both consciously and unconsciously, which—so to speak—draws itself within them on every occasion, and which, generally speaking, runs as follows: because this is, or was, so that is, or was, or this will be; because this is so, or was so, this or that is or will be so. Every observed case provides humans with an involuntary occasion and a foothold to set their expectations for other cases accordingly, by looking for identical or non-identical conditions...

<summary>Fechner argues that faith does not originate from abstract metaphysical concepts like "the Absolute," but rather from the simple, everyday logical inferences humans use to predict the unknown based on the known.</summary>

### The Theoretical Principle

<margin>A vertical red line has been drawn in the right margin, marking the first paragraph.</margin>

...expecting <note>The text continues from the previous page’s discussion of how the human mind draws conclusions from experience.</note> similar or dissimilar consequences based on the similarity or dissimilarity of conditions, and in the same sense proceeds backward from the consequences to the causes. Humanity reasons in this way in the field of the most common things, and also reasons this way in the field of the highest and ultimate things—or rather, reasons from that common field into the higher one, and thereby finally completes all its conclusions. My house was built by someone; the world, too, must have been built by someone. The world is larger than my house, so it must also be a greater "someone" who built the world. My body moves under the influence of my feeling and will; so too will the sun, moon, the sea, and the wind move under such influence, but under the influence of a more powerful feeling and will, because they themselves are more powerful. I live now and change only from one day to the next; I shall also live in the future and only change even more. My life depends on my breath and the warmth of my body; where they go in death, the soul will go. Every king has his servants; God, too, will have his servants.

Not only the belief in the existence of God, the hereafter <term>hereafter: *Jenseits*; refers to the afterlife or the world beyond the physical.</term>, and higher entities, but also all conceptions of their mode of existence are based, consciously or unconsciously, on analogies and inductions <term>induction: *Inductionen*; the logical process of drawing a general conclusion from specific observations.</term> of this kind. <page-num>140</page-num>

# The Theoretical Principle

That which is based on experience <term>experience: *Erfahrungsmäßiges*; the raw data of the senses and everyday life.</term> always serves as the foundation; generalization always leads beyond it. However, when it concerns something greater and higher, it is a generalization coupled with an expansion and an elevation <term>elevation: *Steigerung*; the process of taking a human quality and raising it to a higher or divine degree.</term>. And while, without a practical motive <note>In Fechner's philosophy, the "practical motive" refers to the human need for comfort, hope, and moral order.</note>, there would hardly be any occasion to venture such a leap beyond experience at all, the practical principle for its part would not be able to shape faith or hand it down to the historical principle <note>The "historical principle" refers to the tradition and established doctrines passed down through generations.</note> without such generalizations. Rather, without them, it would create entirely hollow ideas—as has always been shown whenever it has presumed to create faith on its own.

On the other hand, it is not without interest—and we have already pointed this out earlier—how in every different nation the conceptions of faith change according to their different spheres of life and ways of living. The cruder a people, the cruder their generalizations; the more limited and lower they are, the more limited and lower are their expansions and elevations from there into the realm of faith. But even the faith of the most cultivated nations owes its content entirely to the generalization, expansion, and elevation derived from their own sphere of life and way of living, and without this, it would be completely empty.

Along this path, conflicts easily arise between the theoretical motive and the practical motive, as well as within the theoretical motive itself, from which conflicts with the historical also grow and

However, whether the <term>practical motive: the human drive toward what is useful or emotionally satisfying</term> works in conflict or in harmony with the <term>theoretical motive: the drive toward what is logically consistent and observable</term>, it can always only act as a guide for the direction; the actual execution in a given direction remains a matter of the theoretical motive. Be it <term>Sheol: the ancient Hebrew underworld of the dead</term>, Paradise, or Hell, things proceed there according to the analogy of conditions <term>here below: *diesseits*; the earthly realm</term>; and whoever tries to escape this with their faith only enters from another side or leaves the realm of imagination entirely. No matter how unbridled the imagination may seem, it can only work with the materials and forms offered to it by the theoretical motive.

If one looks around at what has actually been achieved in this way, one might at first despair of all faith. The most foolish and contradictory views of God, the <term>hereafter: *jenseits*</term>, angels, and devils have come into being through this, and have found even wider distribution than what the reason of the most rational has sought to put in their place; for in this regard, hardly one individual satisfies another. Are they not then right who say that the source of faith in the highest and ultimate things is not to be sought here at all, but rather to be avoided?

# The Theoretical Principle

Indeed, are not considerations like the following perfectly right?

> Even in everyday life, we err daily as we attempt to infer the distant, the future, and the "other" from the "now" and "here," from this or that specific instance, based on <term>analogies: the inference that if things agree in some respects, they will likely agree in others</term> and <term>inductions: the process of reaching a general conclusion from specific observations</term>. How then can we dare to use such methods on the highest and ultimate things? <note>Fechner refers here to "ultimate things" (*die letzten Dinge*), a theological term for the nature of God, the soul, and the afterlife.</note> In such matters, the task grows to an immeasurable scale, and we lack the help available to us in earthly matters—namely, the ability to confirm or correct our conclusions through experience. Instead, we are compelled to continue inferring into the realm of the uncertain, building even more uncertain structures upon that which is already unsure. By the very nature of the matter, all such conclusions have a reach only within the finite, whereas the objects of religious faith bear the character of infinity.

And yet, it is a strange thing that man involuntarily returns to this spring again and again. Even those who reject this source on principle cannot help but draw from it when they wish to say anything more about the highest and ultimate things than that they are "unspeakable." Yet that is precisely what they want to do; they even tie the concept of salvation to the very things they say about these mysteries. Should a necessary source be deemed reprehensible? them enough or that we use them incorrectly; however, the
better we use them, the more and the better we achieve with them.
Now, the only difference regarding the highest and ultimate things
compared to the most common ones is, first, that what is
sufficient for the common is not yet sufficient for these;
and we err all the more easily and gravely, the more we
believe we have sufficient means; therefore, we must expand
and elevate the means for this, rather than throwing them away.
And further: that an <term>experiential verification: the process of proving a theory through physical observation or experiment (*Bewährung*)</term>
of what has been inferred is, by its very nature, not to be
demanded here, because such proof is no more available for the
<term>spiritual: the "Geistige," referring to the realm of mind, soul, and the divine</term>
above me than it is for the spiritual alongside me.
Therefore, we must—it cannot be repeated enough—
replace it with the harmony of what has been inferred
with the requirements and consequences of the other principles.

<summary>Fechner argues that human reason should not be abandoned when contemplating the divine; instead, it must be refined and harmonized with other principles, as direct physical proof of spiritual truths is impossible.</summary>

# The Theoretical Principle
Fechner, Motives of Faith. 10

There is, to be sure, no other way of <term>verification: the process of proving a theory through physical observation or experience (*Bewährung*)</term>; but this way does exist.

Now, however, the entire theoretical foundation and shaping of faith almost always founder on one of two cliffs, to which the errors we showed at the beginning lead: that one elevates the highest and ultimate things above the lower and common ones—conceptually, factually, or both at once—as if they had nothing at all in common; and that one confuses them with the lower or common things themselves, as if they were merely one thing among others, instead of <term>subsuming: the German *aufzuheben*, a philosophical term meaning to simultaneously cancel, preserve, and elevate to a higher level</term> the lower into the higher, thereby bringing them to <term>completion: the state of being rounded off or finalized (*Abschluss*)</term>. <note>A vertical red line begins in the right margin of the original manuscript at this point, highlighting the following argument.</note> And indeed, if God were truly incomparable to everything in His world—torn away from the world, hovering above it, while the world, fallen away from God, floated beneath Him, as many imagine—the world in which our experiences move, then no inference from the world to Him would be possible. No inference would be possible from the completion of our own small spiritual world within an "I" to the completion of the entire spiritual world within an "I"; nor from the dominion of our spirit over a small part of the physical world to the dominion of a Spirit over the entire physical world. Yet theoretically we have no reason to assume the highest existence lacks a conceptual and factual connection with all existence; and practically, a God is useless who has no specifiable and traceable relationships to His world and to His creatures

<summary>Fechner argues that God must not be viewed as entirely separate from the world; if He were, human logic could never bridge the gap between our limited experience and the divine. He suggests that the human spirit's relationship to the body serves as a logical model for God's relationship to the universe.</summary>

# The Theoretical Principle

...has [done so], and has always historically been presented in such relations; and even those who did not wish to do so, did it nonetheless.

And indeed, if the transition into the beyond <note>original: *Jenseits*</note> occurred beyond all comparability with all transitions in this world <note>original: *Diesseits*</note>, and if in the beyond itself all relationships and laws that apply in this world ceased to exist, then none of the inferences that we shall draw from the one to the other would be possible. But from time immemorial, people have sought analogies for the transition into the beyond within this world, and have shaped their conceptions of the future life based on the present one; practically, we require a faith that maintains the relationships from this world into the beyond; and everywhere else we find through experience that the present contains the conditions of the future, and we infer from them the future as far as possible. Now, to be sure, our capacity is not even sufficient to deduce how the orange follows from the foliage, the butterfly from the caterpillar, a life after birth from the life before birth, or a memory from an intuition <note>original: *Anschauung*; in German philosophy, this refers to the immediate sensory perception of an object</note>; how, you ask, should we be able to infer how a life in the beyond follows from this life? # The Theoretical Principle

Professors at their university chairs; I turn from the **Rationalists** to the **Orthodox** and **Pietists** <note>These represent the three main theological camps of Fechner's era: the Rationalists (reason-based), the Orthodox (dogma-based), and the Pietists (emotion/devotion-based).</note> by turns; I rummage through the dusty proofs for the **existence of God**; I study the philosophical systems of **Kant**, **Fichte**, **Schelling**, **Hegel**, **Herbart**, **Schopenhauer**. Yet, I search in vain as well for where I might find a clear understanding <term>Erkenntniß</term>, let alone a sound application, of the aforementioned principle; I find only a wavering between two positions or a combination of both main errors in its application—a shipwreck now on one cliff, now on another. How can a sound argument be established if its underlying principle is neglected or violated?

Regarding the existence of a God—as demanded by the historical and practical arguments, through which the other main tenets of faith naturally follow—we can reach a conclusion based on the **theoretical principle** in two ways, and only two ways, even though each of these ways, in its generality, encompasses a variety of specific paths. First, by moving from the world of our own spirit <note>original: *Geist*. In German philosophy, this encompasses mind, spirit, and consciousness.</note>—the only world of which we have immediate knowledge—through the world of spirits, in which we believe as firmly as if we knew it to be true based on the totality of the three reasons...

<header>The Theoretical Principle.</header>

...therefore, rise to the world of a spirit; secondly, by moving from the fact that our own body both reflects and supports a spirit—the only case of knowledge <note>original: *Wissensfalle*. Fechner means this is the only instance where we have direct "inside" knowledge of the relationship between mind and matter.</note> we have of this kind—we rise to the conclusion that the entire world is the reflection and support of a spirit in a higher sense.

For these are the only starting points available to us from the side of knowledge. They prevent us from concluding "from the void into the void," allowing us instead to move from what is full to what is even fuller, thereby unlocking the true content of faith. These are the only foundations upon which an argument can be built that completes our theoretical motivations according to the principle mentioned above.

Depending on whether we proceed from the first starting point solely within the spiritual realm, or whether we start from the second and consider the body as the reflection and support of the soul, we can distinguish two main parts, sides, forms, or applications of the theoretical argument. For brevity, I will call them the **argument from the spirit** and the **argument from the body**. Fundamentally, and in their ultimate conclusions, they remain a single, unified argument.

A few more words before establishing them.

I have often had occasion to point out that each of us knows only one single soul—our own—directly through experience. The consequence of this is that for any inference <term>Schluß</term> regarding other souls or spirits—whether they be those of our neighbors or higher beings—there is no induction <term>Induction</term> <note>Induction is the logical process of drawing a general rule from many examples. # The Theoretical Principle.

...needs identical cases as a foundation, but rather only **analogy** is at our disposal. Yet analogy cannot be dispensed with in this field. For how could we even form a concept of a spirit <term>Geist; referring here to the conscious mind or intellect</term> beside or above our own spirit without linking it to the experience of our own spirit?

Now, every single analogy, taken on its own, is deceptive; yet the inference <term>Schluß; a logical conclusion</term> regarding the **souls of our neighbors** <note>original: *Nachbarseelen*. This refers to Fechner's "neighbor-souls" concept—the minds of other people which we cannot perceive directly but must infer exist.</note> is binding. And what makes it binding? Let us look at the small scale, not to seek something different, but only to demand a larger version of it when inferring something greater. It is binding because for the "small" soul, it gathers exactly those elements that one might wish to discard when considering the "greatest" soul, even though they simply need to be gathered on the largest possible scale. It is binding because it does not rely on a single analogy nor a mere sum of isolated analogies—a circle, after all, shares its closed nature with me, a statue its external form, and a wound-up clock its internal cycle; yet none of these things individually constitute a soul. Rather, it is binding because it is a self-contained and harmonious **system of analogy** across all points that can reasonably be considered regarding the soul's own essence and how it is reflected and supported by the physical body; *) — and fin-

Through this, and through this alone, does the inference regarding the existence of the small human **neighbor-souls** <note>original: *Nachbarseelen*. Analogy can be dispensed with here just as little; individually, it is just as deceptive; what is required is likewise a system of analogy within itself and in conjunction with the conclusions and demands of the other principles.

In the inference regarding other human souls, the analogies draw themselves; the system creates itself, so to speak. A little human <note>original: *Menschlein*; a diminutive, emphasizing the smallness of man compared to the divine.</note> is so small, stands so related to us, and is so clearly before us; everything about it is familiar to us at first glance. With God, and with higher spirits and those of the hereafter, everything is large and vast and initially unfamiliar; here, it is necessary first to show the connection of what must be observed, to first make everything clear and familiar.

<page-num>153</page-num>

# The Theoretical Principle.

Now, however, everything cannot be shown all at once in the way it is ultimately to be grasped as a whole; thus, it is also not to be demanded all at once. The argument from the spirit <term>Geist; referring here to both the individual human mind and the broader divine spirit</term> as from the body can only develop in a sequence of moments <term>Momente; individual stages or contributing factors in a logical progression</term>; nothing individual within it is binding. The binding force lies in the bond—not merely of the individual parts of each argument, but of both arguments together, which are indeed only two sides of one single argument. It lies not only in this bond, but also in the connection of this argument with the other two; and finally, in the fact that the three arguments provide all three principal articles of faith <note>original: *Hauptstücke des Glaubens*. These are the foundational tenets of belief, often referring to the parts of the Catechism or Creed.</note> within one and the same bond. Thus, the binding force grows with every step, yet it resides in no single step, but only in the entire progression.

Whoever wishes to follow this connection will grasp it at the conclusion and, at the exit of the entire path, will finally find the entrance to a worldview <term>Weltansicht; a comprehensive philosophical outlook</term> in which faith and knowledge <note>original: *Glauben und Wissen*. A central theme in 19th-century German philosophy, seeking to reconcile religious belief with scientific understanding.</note> coexist without contradiction, and where each finds support and promotion in the other. Whoever seeks the result of the conclusion in its isolated details—seeking the house within a single stone—will never possess it.

We might also remind ourselves that the same analogy that leads us from the small and lowly things we know toward a faith in the greater and higher, simultaneously elevates <term>aufhebt; a key philosophical term meaning to abolish something while simultaneously preserving it on a higher level</term> the small and lowly into the greater and higher. Consequently, knowledge, in this regard...

<summary>Fechner explains that the validity of his "theoretical principle" comes from the cumulative strength of its interconnected arguments rather than any single piece of evidence. He argues that a true worldview reconciles faith and knowledge into a unified, non-contradictory whole.</summary>

Taken on its own, it remains a bold and daring ascent that leads us from below upward; who would venture to say it is secure? But let us only remember: the ladder for climbing does not stand in thin air; it is held from above as well as from below. From above, in that it leads into that same realm of faith which we possess historically <note>Referring to tradition and scripture</note> and require practically <note>Referring to moral and emotional needs</note>; meanwhile, from below, in that it leads there starting from the reality of experience. In contrast, faithless materialism ascends a ladder held only from below <note>relying solely on physical evidence</note>, while faith-desolate idealism descends a ladder held only from above <note>relying solely on abstract ideas or pure thought</note>.

However, if the theoretical argument requires this support from the practical and historical, it also performs a reciprocal service for them by preventing the excesses of the practical and theoretical motives <note>The text here mentions the "theoretical" motive again, likely referring to the internal logic of the arguments</note>, under which no argument could endure. What might man not demand of God? Indeed, what does he not demand? How many things within the world itself—this world governed by God—would he wish were different? Yes, what does he not wish for? And what, finally, should prevent him from believing in a God and a world exactly as he desires them, if not for the necessity of meeting the demands of the practical and historical arguments simultaneously with those [of the theoretical...] <note>The sentence ends abruptly at the bottom of the page.</note>

->**The Argument from Spirit.**<-

But since the theoretical only allows such a view of God and the world as can withstand the sight of reality with all its flaws and evils, it passes naturally into the practical and historical argument. According to these, truth is not merely what a person desires or believes here and there, at this or that time; rather, it is what is accepted as true by all of humanity for all time. It is that which is able to guide their thinking, feeling, and acting in such a way that the most desirable state for humanity as a whole follows from it, leaving no further reason to look beyond it. For this to happen, however, that which is actually true must be accepted as truth; for only in this way can man best position himself in relation to it, and only then can that position endure.

## The Argument from Spirit.

Our spirit <term>Geist; in German, this word encompasses both "mind" and "spirit," referring here to the seat of human consciousness and intellect</term> presents itself as a realm of diverse and changing sensations, memories, mental images, concepts, thoughts, drives, strivings, and wishes, which subordinate and coordinate with one another, linking and separating, harmonizing and conflicting. It is, in truth, a world in miniature.

This small world of particulars is, for its part, only a single detail within the great spiritual world <note>original: "Geisterwelt"</note>, in which...

repeats, on a larger scale and in a higher sense, what occurs in the smaller world. For in the great spiritual world <note>original: "Geisterwelt"</note>, too, realms organize themselves above and below one another, joining and separating, harmonizing and clashing with each other; and whatever from that world falls into each small world of a single spirit is, on one hand, only the final branch, and on the other, the root of that which belongs to the large world and passes through it in broad strokes. Thus, the larger realm is simply the larger, higher, more general version of what we find in the smaller one. Now, over all the particulars in the small world, there extends a single feeling of the *I* <term>Ich; the ego or the self-conscious center of identity</term>, a unified *consciousness*, and a unified *will*. The two spheres of visual and auditory sensations seem to have nothing in common; yet they share this: that the *I* knows of both in the same way, yet knows more than both together; and over all the conflict of details, there reaches a striving to settle and reconcile this conflict, to bring every individual feeling and thought into such harmony that the *I* may find satisfaction in it.

Therefore, while we cannot know what will never be a matter of factual knowledge, we may nonetheless believe—as we already have reason to believe anyway—that over all the particulars in the great spiritual world, in a corresponding sense, a single feeling of the *I*, a unified *consciousness*, and a unified *will* shall extend, in cor— <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "ent-", from "entsprechender" (corresponding).</note>

<page-num>157</page-num>

<header>The Argument from the Spirit.</header>

responding but also in a correspondingly higher sense, just as the great spiritual world <term>Geisteswelt; the collective realm of all minds and spirits</term> is something far above the small one. The circles of consciousness <term>Bewußtseinskreise; the individual spheres of awareness belonging to different people</term> of various people seem to have nothing in common; yet they will share this: that that "I" <note>The author refers here to a divine or universal Ego</note> knows of all of them as if they were its own, only knowing more than all of them combined. And over all the conflict of nations and generations, of knowledge and faith—which in the great realm assumes so much larger dimensions and reaches higher than in the small one, yet reaches into it and rises out of it—there will also reach a greater and higher striving, to lead this conflict toward reconciliation <term>Versöhnung; the process of resolving contradictions into a higher unity</term> through its governance in history. The task is greater, the time of fulfillment longer than in the small realm—even infinitely long, if you will—but the means within God are also greater—you may believe infinitely great—and fulfillment in eternity <term>Ewigkeit; the state of being beyond time</term> is therefore more perfect and more certain.

This satisfies the mystic, who wishes to immerse himself entirely in God and seeks the most complete satisfaction in that total immersion. He is, after all, already immersed in God; yet he must also recognize that merely "being" in God is not sufficient to satisfy God or to find the most perfect contentment. Just as our own small spirit rejects countless things that enter it as mental representations <term>Vorstellung; a mental image or idea</term>, and seeks to remove what is contrary to its feelings, so it is even more so within God’s great Spirit. Rather, it is necessary to clearly recognize God’s mind according to the highest and final relations and to do justice to it. Only then will the mystic partake in that full blessedness <term>Seligkeit; a state of supreme spiritual happiness or salvation</term>, which he would otherwise—at too cheap a price—wish to tie solely to the feeling of simply being in God.

# The Argument from Spirit.

This satisfies the rationalist <term>Rationalist; in this context, someone who believes religious truth must be accessible through human reason</term>, who demands a rational way to reach God and clear conceptions of Him. Reason cannot find a simpler way to God than our argument, nor can it gain clearer conceptions than what this argument provides. However, reason will have to be humble enough not to try to descend to God from an abstract emptiness <note>original: "abstracter Leere"; Fechner likely refers to purely theoretical or metaphysical speculation that lacks a basis in lived experience</note>, but rather to ascend to Him from firm ground; and in this ascent, it must not rely on itself alone, but must rise in such a way that the historical and practical demands are fulfilled alongside its own.

This also satisfies the believer in revelation <term>Offenbarungsgläubiger; often called a "supernaturalist," one who believes that spiritual truths are communicated directly by God to humanity</term>, who wishes to owe the communication of the highest truths to God Himself through the inspiration of spirits favored by Him. For, even if the small human spirit does not place its entire essence into every thought or every feeling—if not every moment of its life sets the direction for the whole or even for a large sequence of events, and not every moment is defined by the highest and final relations—yet such moments do exist even in the small human spirit and life. Thus, in the infinite Spirit, instead of the small moments of the small spirit, there are entire spirits—who are themselves merely moments of the Great Spirit—in whom the will, knowledge, and essence of the divine Spirit, according to the highest and final relations, [manifest] in an influence upon the whole or a great sequence... <note>The sentence continues on the following page.</note>

Now one can only ask: and why, then, does God the *Almighty, All-good, All-wise* not abolish evil suddenly? And further ask: why is evil present at all with and in such a God?

Here we must believe—because we can believe nothing better or more rational—that the existence of evil and the impossibility of its sudden removal are just as inextricably entwined with the ultimate conditions of existence itself as are the striving and the progressive...

# The Argument from the Spirit.

When a human being is born, their <term>Geist; spirit</term> <note>In German philosophy, "Geist" encompasses mind, spirit, and intellect.</note> begins simultaneously from "Above" and from "Below"—from Above with the total unity of <term>Bewußtsein; consciousness</term>, and from Below with the individual details of sensory <term>Anschauungen; intuitions</term> <note>The immediate way we perceive objects through our senses.</note>, <term>Empfindungen; sensations</term>, feelings, and impulses; and nothing more. As life progresses, "mediations" insert themselves between the two. The "height of spirit," as we call it, grows the higher these mediations between Below and Above ascend; the "breadth of spirit" grows the more the base grows from which they ascend. And how does the spirit gain these mediations between Below and Above? Whatever has entered into the realm of <term>Sinnlichkeit; sensory experience</term> eventually fades away to make room for the new; yet that which has faded awakens again on a new level, in a new spiritual state. That very fading is the reason it can awaken thus: it continues to live on from then on in memories, continues to act in <term>Phantasievorstellungen; imaginative representations</term>, and enters into higher <term>Begriffs- und Ideenverbindungen; combinations of concepts and ideas</term>, <term>Zweckvorstellungen; purposive ideas</term> <note>Mental representations of a goal or end-point.</note>, determinations of the will, and strivings—both determining them and being determined by them—as a spiritual "after-echo" alongside the echoes of earlier faded intuitions, sensations, feelings, and impulses. Through these ascending mediations, the spirit thus links the sensory base within itself and with the spiritual "peak" in various directions at once. Pain attaches itself to the memory of every evil desire and deed,

Thus, within our own spirit, we already possess a second, higher world above a lower world; this second world grows out of the first and populates itself, so to speak, with the souls that rise up from the corpses of the first. Let us call the first, for brevity's sake, the <term>Anschauungswelt; world of intuition</term> <note>original: "Anschauungswelt." In German philosophy, this refers to the world as we immediately perceive it through our senses.</note>, and the second the <term>Erinnerungswelt; world of memory</term> <note>original: "Erinnerungswelt." The German word for memory, *Erinnerung*, literally implies "in-warding" or making something internal.</note>, though these names are too narrow for the subject itself.

And so we may believe—something which, by its nature, can never become a matter of objective knowledge, since in doing so we believe only what we have reason to believe from another perspective—that in the <term>göttlicher Geist; divine Spirit</term> as well, in a corresponding but higher sense, there will be a world of memory above the world of intuition. This implies that our entire earthly human life of intuition and memory itself belongs only to the lower world within God. We may believe that in the world of the Great Spirit, every spirit—after the extinguishing of the limited, earthly-sensory conditions of its existence—will continue to live and act in a higher realm, in a higher <term>Zuständlichkeit; state of being</term>. There, it will enter into a community higher than the earthly one with the spirits of those who have <term>Dahingeschiedener; passed before</term> and, according to the measure of its earthly <term>Trachten; striving</term> and deeds, will bear away either <term>Pein; pain</term> or <term>Seligkeit; blessedness</term>.

# The Argument from the Spirit.

All this could again be a mere fiction <note>original: "Roman," literally "novel," but used here to mean a product of imagination or a poetic invention.</note> and yet it cannot be, for the same reasons that a well-founded faith in God cannot be; it returns to us what history gave us and what we must demand. For did we not already believe otherwise, that upon death we enter into a higher kingdom, to meet our loved ones there, to find retribution there—and do we not require this faith? From where else, indeed, would we obtain the guarantee that independent spirits in God can ascend into an afterlife in the same way that dependent <term>Geistesmomente; moments of spirit</term> <note>In this context, these are individual thoughts, memories, or mental flashes that exist within a person's consciousness.</note> ascend within us? Without this, we might fear that we are intensifying our hopes only to ascend into a void. Since we thus find everything again through this reasoning, and cannot find what we already possessed and needed in any other way, what prevents us from trusting the only path that makes it possible—and even more than possible? 

Naturally, since we do not contain whole spirits with a higher realm above them within ourselves, such whole spirits cannot "ascend" within us. However, if God’s superiority over finite spirits consists partly in the fact that He contains them within Himself, along with a higher realm above them, why should they be any less able to ascend within Him than the dependent moments of spirit ascend within us? This is especially true since the independence of spirits within God is only relative, just as the dependence of spiritual moments within us is relative.

# The Theoretical Principle.

The conditioned nature of our memories; after all, they do not float in a vacuum; they reside in the brain; they attach themselves there to the consequences of that which <term>Anschauung; perception</term> <note>In this philosophical context, "Anschauung" refers to the immediate, sensory experience or "looking upon" the world.</note> has attached itself to, whatever it may be, and will undoubtedly not be able to link together in concepts and ideas without the circles of the effects underlying them linking together on the physical side. <note>The author is arguing that mental associations require corresponding physical connections in the brain's "circles of effects" (what we might today call neural pathways or traces).</note> Thus, we may also expect the corresponding reality of this—only, like everything in this domain, on a larger scale—for our future <term>Erinnerungsleben; life of memory</term> in God, and we shall find it when we come to the **Argument from the Body**. But for now, let the **Argument from the Spirit** first take its final step, in order to reach the third main article of faith and to bind it together with the other two in the same bond.

The realm of our perceptions does not only allow new material to constantly rise into our life of memory and intervene in it with new determinations, but conversely receives determinations, guidance, meaning, and higher leadership from there; indeed, it is entirely interwoven with it by way of association. Into everything we see, the memory of everything related and akin to it that we have ever seen enters silently, thereby turning the green patch into a forest and the white patch into a house; otherwise, the eye would see nothing but meaningless <term>Farbenflecke; spots of color</term>. The entirety of our earlier life of perception thus continues to act through its <term>Nachklängen; echoes</term> <note>Literally "after-sounds," referring to the lingering resonance or traces of past experiences.</note> in the later life; the same

<summary>This page argues that memories require a physical basis (the brain) to connect ideas and suggests that a similar physical-spiritual correspondence exists on a divine scale. It also explains how memory provides the necessary context to turn raw sensory data, like spots of color, into meaningful perceptions like houses or forests.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Argument from Spirit," elaborating on how memory integrates past and present perceptions to create a coherent consciousness. # The Argument from Spirit.

Memory links countless <term>Anschauungen; perceptions</term> <note>In this philosophical context, "Anschauung" refers to the immediate, sensory experience or "looking upon" the world.</note> through what it has in common with them, and unnumbered memories combine with one another to give meaning to new perceptions and to arrange them within the spiritual context. Indeed, the whole of today’s perceptual life is only able to steadily continue yesterday’s because the entire context of the former continues to act in the present through memories and the <term>Begriffe; concepts</term> and <term>Ideen; ideas</term> grown out of and above them. But while the former life is thus continued in the present and further developed in its original sense through the mediation of the life of memory, the life of memory simultaneously receives new <term>Bestimmungen; determinations</term> <note>In German philosophy, these are the specific qualities or characteristics that define a thing.</note> from the present.

Thus, the life of memory drives its roots downwards into the life of perception, from which it draws its sustenance; yet at the same time, it grows upwards far beyond it and, like the crown of a tree above the roots, has its own realm. Instead of being lost in those interactions with the life of the senses, it rises in detachment from them to the greatest clarity—to a self-coherent consciousness. There, what first entered from the world of perception is reflected upon, brought into context, and further refined; and as the entire life of memory itself rises to ever greater heights, it also reaches back into the life of perception from that greater height.

# The Theoretical Principle.

And so we may again believe what we cannot know: that it will be so, in a higher sense, in the Kingdom of God. The entire <term>geistige Cultur; spiritual culture</term> <note>This refers to the collective intellectual, moral, and artistic achievements of humanity.</note> of the past carries over into the present and exerts a determining influence on every new human being from the very beginning and continuously thereafter; and each person only ever acts anew with, against, or in addition to that which they have absorbed into themselves from the past.

But God knows of everything, and the spirits of the <term>Dahingeschiedenen; departed</term>, who have ascended into Him, are the bearers of the consciousness of these effects within Him. Through this, His <term>Jenseits; beyond</term> <note>The transcendent realm or afterlife.</note> intervenes in His <term>Diesseits; here-and-now</term> <note>The physical, present world.</note>—each spirit acting through that which originated from them. They continue to develop what they began here together, with God’s help and acting as God’s help in the here-and-now, but no longer bound by the old limitations. 

Everything—be it ideas or other influences of a spirit who lived previously—that appears separated in the here-and-now consciousness of a thousand people currently living, becomes linked in the "beyond" consciousness of that same spirit from whom it originally issued; and this, in turn, links together the spirits of the here-and-now. However, the here-and-now consciousness of the origin must first be extinguished before the "beyond" consciousness of the consequences can awaken; for the one only awakens as the other transforms into it. Thus, the spirits of the here-and-now can perceive the consciousness of the spirits in the beyond that links them together just as little as they can perceive the same of the...

Thus, every <term>abgeschiedene Geist; departed spirit</term> <note>Fechner refers to the souls of those who have died, who he believes continue to exist within a higher divine consciousness.</note> links a whole host of living people and helps lead them. In this, it meets other departed spirits—either harmoniously or in conflict—though no single spirit can entirely lead a whole human being. Rather, each spirit guides each person only in those areas where the person allows themselves to be led. 

And while the spirit leads the person in its own direction as far as it is able, it also receives new influences through that person's life. The spirit sees through the person's eye and hears through their ear whatever concerns them both, and it receives back the very thought it inspired in the person, now changed and reshaped. For a person in <term>diesseitige; this world</term> is not merely a passive <term>Tummelplatz; playground</term> for spirits from the <term>jenseitiger; beyond</term>, even though it is a fact that we are shaped in countless ways by the <term>Fortwirkungen; continuing effects</term> <note>This refers to the "after-effects" or the living legacy of a person's thoughts, works, and character.</note> of spirits who came before us. Indeed, who can purely separate what belongs to themselves from what they have inherited from those predecessors? It is impossible to separate the two precisely because they are not purely separate. 

Therefore, it is not a <term>Glaubenssache; matter of faith</term>—and it is important to make this distinction—that countless spirits of the past influence every human being through their continuing effects; this is instead a fact upon which faith may rest. In fact, however, this faith is also capable of a beautiful and beneficial development in a practical direction. <meta>This page continues the transition from the physical "Argument from the Body" to the "Argument from the Mind," emphasizing how individual human spirits are nested within a larger, universal spirit, much like the body is nested within the physical world. It refers back to Fechner's major works, "Zend-Avesta" and "The Soul Question."</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
# The Theoretical Principle.

...ever occurs within this small part of the world, the spirit shares in through <term>mutual conditionality; Wechselbedingtheit</term>. <note>Fechner is describing a "psycho-physical" relationship where mind and matter are two sides of the same coin; what affects one affects the other.</note> We recognize the existence of other finite spirits by the fact that they are bound to similar limited parts of the world with similar processes. However, the part of the world to which our own spirit is bound stands in such relationships—firstly of similarity, secondly of effect, thirdly of origin, fourthly of **partial inclusion**, and fifthly of purposeful integration—and generally in a state of subordination to the world as a whole (which encompasses both the organic and inorganic in a higher organic connection). Because of this, we cannot help but regard the world as the larger, higher, broader, and more general version of that to which our own spirit is attached. Consequently, we must also think of a larger, higher, broader, and more general Spirit than our own in relation to it, while simultaneously thinking of our own spirit as being partially included and subordinate within it. Thus, the argument from the body and the argument from the spirit coincide entirely.

# The Argument from the Body.

...to suffice with a few general considerations of its foundation.

The previous argument gave Christianity its due; this one gives Paganism its due. And the latter also has its right—not, indeed, in those aspects where it contradicts the fundamental ideas of Christianity, but in what can be <term>sublated; aufgehoben</term> <note>Fechner uses the philosophical term "aufheben," which means to simultaneously cancel out, preserve, and lift something to a higher level of understanding.</note> within those ideas. For this to happen, however, these ideas must expand beyond the boundaries that have been drawn for them, and it is this expansion that the current argument demands.

Just as God, in the prevailing Christian view, is detached from spirits, He is also detached from Nature—the very Nature into which Paganism not only fully immerses Him, but into which it splits and fragments Him. This mixing of God with Nature, this splitting and fragmenting into Nature, is the error of Paganism; that separating arrogance over Nature is the error of Christianity.

It is true that even in the Christian view, there is talk of an omnipresent and all-ruling God, without whom no hair falls from our head and no leaf from a tree; yet this is taken as little in earnest as the words stating that "in God we live, move, and have our being" <note>original: "in Gott leben, weben, sind." This is a reference to Acts 17:28, which Fechner views as a literal truth rather than just a metaphor.</note>. Rather, Nature is thought of as having fallen out of God—indeed, as having defected from Him—and left to its own inherent forces, even if these were originally placed within it by Him. There were even times,

<meta>Red pencil markings in right margin and under some words.</meta>

<summary>Fechner argues for a reconciliation between the Christian view of a transcendent God and the Pagan view of an immanent God within nature, suggesting that God is truly omnipresent and that nature is not separate from the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, Paganism, Nature, Omnipresence, Sublation, Transcendence, Immanence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Argument from the Body," specifically transitioning into the theoretical principles that reconcile the materialist view of nature with the spiritual view of God's omnipresence.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>
# The Theoretical Principle.

...where it belonged to the **Christian style** <note>The author refers to a specific theological trend that emphasizes the "fallen" or corrupt nature of the physical world to make God's holiness seem greater by comparison.</note> to exalt God by deeply degrading Nature. Yet once again, our argument will compel us to take those words seriously—words that the Christian, at the very least, cannot help but utter—while simultaneously resolving and clarifying the contradictions that prevent him from being earnest about them.

Our body only holds together, and everything within it functions, insofar as a **spirit** is present; the materialist himself cannot deny this, and it changes nothing if he prefers to call the spirit a *result* rather than a **connecting principle**, as we prefer to call it; regardless, the body can only hold together and function through this result. Thus, the entire world will also only hold together and everything within it function insofar as a spirit is present; and again, it would change nothing if the materialist wanted to call this spirit a "result"—which another calls the <term>primal ground; Urgrund</term> <note>A philosophical term for the original source or fundamental cause of all existence.</note> and we call the highest connecting principle of the whole. The matter remains the same.

This is the most general viewpoint of the **omnipresence and all-activity of God in Nature**, which does not exclude the possibility that He also acts within it according to special relationships.

Now, an objection arises: that the entire world instead holds together and everything within it functions according to the **laws of nature**. But our body also holds together and everything...

<summary>Fechner argues that just as the human body requires a spirit (or a "connecting principle") to function and hold together, the universe requires a divine spirit to maintain its coherence, regardless of whether one views this spirit as a cause or a result.</summary>
<keywords>omnipresence of God, materialism, spirit vs. matter, laws of nature, connecting principle, primal ground</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Argument from the Body," specifically exploring how the physical laws of nature and the presence of a spirit are not contradictory but mutually dependent, both in the individual human and the universal scale.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>

# The Argument from the Body.

...functions therein according to **physiological laws**, which belong to the **laws of nature**. Yet a spirit is present, and it remains true that the body only holds together so long as a spirit is present. Therefore, one cannot struggle against the other. Rather, the existence of that very lawfulness, by virtue of which the body holds together and according to which life functions within it, exists only alongside the existence of the spirit in man; it vanishes from the body of man just as the spirit vanishes from it. And so the more general lawfulness of the physical world, to which the body is subordinate, will be connected to the existence of a more general spirit, to which the human spirit is subordinate; this itself is naturally connected.

For the spirit also has its own side of lawfulness—were this not the case, how could **logic** and **psychology** exist?—and it is, again, natural that just as body and spirit exist together in harmony, their laws exist likewise. They do so in fact; otherwise, body and spirit themselves could not exist and function together at all; and clearly, the <term>teleological principle; the idea that nature acts with a purpose or goal</term> <note>The author is arguing that both physical biological processes and mental processes share a common "design" or "goal-oriented" nature, which he calls the teleological principle.</note> is common to both. Whether one says from a **materialistic** perspective that the spirit is bound to these laws, or from an **idealistic** perspective that the spirit gave these laws to itself and to nature when it established them; the fact we are concerned with here remains the same: the connection is present within us; and if it is present within us, it can and must finally also be present above us, in that from which...

<page-num>184</page-num>

->### The Theoretical Principle.<-

...from which our own body and spirit have sprung, and in which they are still partially included.

Of course, a clock also functions according to **laws of nature**, without a spirit being present within it. But with its regulated movement, it belongs to the whole of nature, within which a spirit *is* present; and without that, it could no more function than a leaf could fall from a tree or a hair from your head according to natural laws. Try to think of it otherwise, and you would have to contradict either the **omnipotence of God** or the <term>lawfulness of nature; the principle that the universe operates via consistent, discoverable rules</term>; thus, they harmonize with one another. <note>The author uses the clock metaphor—a favorite of 18th and 19th-century thinkers—to argue that even mechanical objects are part of a larger "ensouled" system.</note>

But now, it is said, for all this lawfulness, the spirit is also a free being, and we require a free God. We see the signs of **human freedom** in the free actions of man, which cannot be sufficiently explained by any laws of the spirit or of nature; where then are the signs of a corresponding freedom of God?

Signs of a freedom that is at once corresponding and equal are found nowhere else but in man himself, insofar as man with his freedom is himself contained within God; but beyond that, there are the fully corresponding signs of a higher freedom. For just as little as we can sufficiently explain the free actions of man through known laws of the spirit and nature, so too—and even less so—can we explain the **creation** of man himself, or the unpredictable course of **history** within humanity, even if we, just as in history... <note>The text breaks off here, suggesting that while history seems to follow some patterns, its overall "creation" and trajectory remain as much a mystery of divine freedom as a person's individual choices are to an observer.</note>

<summary>The author argues that the mechanical laws of nature (like a clock) are part of a larger system animated by God's spirit, and that human freedom is a subset of a higher, divine freedom manifested in history and creation.</summary>

# The Argument from the Body.

...of the individual spirit, we can foresee many things according to a general <term>lawfulness; the principle that the universe operates via consistent, discoverable rules</term>, but not everything; not the truly new, which must itself first lay the foundation for a new foresight. If, however, such things point to freedom in the individual person, why should that which applies to the whole of humanity—indeed, to the entire world of creatures—not point to a freedom *above* human and all created freedom? After all, the course of world history cannot be calculated simply by adding up the intentions and actions of individual people. <note>The author suggests that history has a "grand design" or direction that emerges beyond the sum of individual human choices.</note> And if lawfulness must somehow coexist with freedom in man—as it does in fact—will it not also be able to coexist in the same way beyond him? It does so just as factually, as long as one wishes to speak of freedom at all.

Of course, anyone who denies freedom in man in any sense will likewise have to deny it in God. But just as little as he can, through that denial, deny the spirit, the will, the possibility of choice, the actions that others call "free," or their connection and consequences—for these are facts—so too will he be unable to do so with God. He would only have to shift his understanding of the ultimate ground of all these things in God, just as he does in man. The question of **God’s existence**, His most essential attributes, and the significance they have for us, always remains independent of the metaphysical question of freedom, however much one might... <note>The text breaks off here, suggesting that the debate over "free will vs. determinism" should not distract from the fundamental reality of God's presence.</note>

...likes to ruin the clarity of one question through the obscurities of the other.

And so, the proper use of our <term>principle; a fundamental rule or starting point for reasoning</term>—to always draw conclusions from the factual only to the factual, and to see the greatness and height of the small and lowly things we possess within the greater and higher things for which we search—spares us from entering into the conflict between **Idealism** and **Materialism**, **Freedom** and **Necessity**, in which philosophical systems exhaust themselves without result. It leaves this conflict free to exist; we do not wish to criticize it; yet we keep our hand always upon the <term>factum; a proven fact or event</term>, leading the bride home while the others fight over her. <note>This metaphor suggests that by focusing on observable reality (the "fact"), one gains the "prize" of truth while others waste time on theoretical arguments.</note>

That is to say, wanting to arrive at what is closest to our knowledge by starting from what lies furthest from it; for what existed first will always only be found last. <note>The author is advocating for an inductive approach: we should study the world as it is now to understand its origin, rather than making up stories about the beginning to explain the present.</note> If, then, there is a path to knowledge here, it must be traveled in the opposite direction; yet, whether one even exists?

If we look closer, we see that the very view which lifts the spirit away from matter—in a sense different from the previous examples—is based more on the demand that it be saved for the <term>afterlife</term> <note>original: *Jenseits*; the world beyond or the state of existence after death.</note>—one assumes there is no other way—than on finding support in a factual foundation and thereby being able to offer one for that demand. Look at the **violin**; its tone could not arise without it; yet, once it has arisen, you can smash the instrument, and the tone echoes on into the distance, expanding its circle further and further, carrying its entire **unique character** <note>original: *Eigenthümlichkeit*; the specific qualities that make a particular sound or soul distinct from others.</note> into the distance, through thick and thin; it does, to be sure, finally fade for all external ears, because none can follow it or grasp its expansion; yet it does not cease to echo; it is still the same tone; the smooth air is sufficient to sustain it—that which required the most artistic of instruments to arise; and where it meets a string of a **kindred tuning** <note>This refers to the physical phenomenon of sympathetic resonance, where one vibrating object causes another to vibrate at the same frequency.</note>, it still causes it to ring.

To illustrate this vividly: throw many stones into a pond alongside a single one, and each circle of waves, as it crosses with all the others, will have its external world in the totality of the others in one sense, and in the whole earth surrounding the pond in another, broader sense. This is how it is to be understood.

### The Argument from the Body.

Our current body was also complete before it was born into this current life; it was born into consciousness only after the organs with which it was rooted in the mother's womb—those which had driven it forth—had died. So it is with our future body; it is already present, yet not yet present for a conscious life; for that to happen, the current body must first die\*). Now, it may seem different in that at birth, consciousness is ignited anew in the child, whereas for the dying person, death is meant to signify only a change in the seat of their consciousness. Yet we generally see consciousness arise only as it vanishes elsewhere—or after it had previously vanished (in sleep)—and vanish as it arises elsewhere, or vanishes in order to arise anew in the future. We may well recognize a general law in this, under which the awakening of consciousness in the afterlife <note>original: *Jenseits*</note> upon its extinction in this world <note>original: *Diesseits*</note> is subordinated. Even that first emergence of consciousness at birth can easily be interpreted as merely apparent. For it is enough to think that from the universal divine consciousness supported by the world, something is converted into the *specialization* <note>original: *Specialität*; the state of being a distinct, individual consciousness rather than a part of the whole.</note> of human consciousness with every new birth\*\*); thus, it is not merely the—
