# The Argument from the Body.

### Fechner, Motives of Faith. 14

...that an analogous law in the mental domain exists for the one in the bodily domain, and even that the one is connected with the other, insofar as the mental is supported by the physical. According to that law, while the same quantity of living force <term>living force: original *lebendige Kraft* or *vis viva*; a 19th-century term for kinetic energy ($mv^2$), which was then the standard measure of physical activity</term> (a measure of bodily activity in the exact sense, not to be confused with the "vital force" <note>original: *Lebenskraft*; the hypothetical non-physical force that animates living things, which Fechner, a pioneer of experimental psychology, sought to distinguish from measurable energy</note> of the philosophers) does not remain continuously the same in the world, it can rise and fall; however, the possibility of its restoration remains ever the same by virtue of constantly persisting real conditions for it, so that the living force cannot disappear in one place without emerging again either in another place or at another time; in short, the sum of the actual and—according to existing conditions—the still possible quantity of living force always remains the same.

There is no doubt that this law, being universally valid for all bodily activity, also applies to psychophysical <term>psychophysical: relating to the relationship between physical stimuli and mental phenomena; Fechner coined this term to describe the functional relationship between body and soul</term> activity—that is, the physical activity by which our mental activity is supported; there are even enough direct arguments for this\*); only, it is not yet proven thereby that it also applies to mental activity itself—that is, that the measure of actual mental activity, and the mental activity possible through reappearance according to the existing real conditions, also remains the same—because mental activity is not simply proportional to the underlying physical activity, as I have shown in my *Psychophysics*.

Rather, assuming a sustained total magnitude of psychophysical activity, a maximum of the mental activity supported by it occurs at a certain degree of distribution of the psychophysical activity, which—insofar as it concerns only simple sensory sensations—

---

\*) See *Psychophysics*. Vol. I. p. 37 ff. <note>Fechner is referencing his own seminal 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik*, which laid the foundations for modern experimental psychology</note>

<summary>Fechner argues that just as physical "living force" (energy) is conserved and transformed rather than lost, the psychophysical energy supporting human consciousness must also be preserved. However, he cautions that mental activity is not a simple 1:1 reflection of physical energy, but depends on how that energy is distributed.</summary>

...[handled], which would be the uniform one, but with respect to higher phenomena is a non-uniform yet unknown one \*). Should the world now continuously tend toward such a distribution—which can neither be asserted nor denied for the moment—then the measure of mental activity in the world would gradually grow, without that of the physical activity changing as a whole or in any way other than periodically; only the distribution would have to change. From this viewpoint and the principle that the **content of consciousness** of later spirits largely overlaps with that of earlier ones, the possibility of the creation of ever-new spirits could rest psychophysically <note>Fechner suggests that new souls can be "born" without requiring new energy, because they share the experiences and "mental material" of those who came before them.</note> without a continuous growth of **living force** <term>living force: *lebendige Kraft*; see previous page note on kinetic energy</term> in the physical world and without the extinguishing of the earlier spirits. However, only from further progress in psychophysics can we expect more certainty and light concerning this important question, which bears upon the consequences discussed here.

How much could be told of facts \*\*), which seem to prove that in exceptional cases a partial awakening of consciousness for its future broader sphere can already occur along with a partial falling asleep to its current narrower one—if only we were certain that they were indeed facts. <note>Fechner is cautiously referring to "clairvoyant" or near-death experiences where the soul seems to expand beyond the body.</note> Nothing could better support our faith than these wonderful cases in which people have always been inclined to see a prelude to the future way of existence; but with diligence...

I will set aside everything here that itself requires faith in order to serve faith. The prior faith is so strongly supported from other directions that it could, conversely, serve to support the belief in these miraculous cases. <note>Fechner refers to the "miraculous cases" mentioned at the end of the previous page—instances where people seemingly glimpse the afterlife. He argues his philosophical system is so solid it proves the miracles, rather than the miracles proving the system.</note>

Much remains an inexplicable mystery in all of this; who would deny it? Yet who could also deny that all the inexplicable things we believe regarding our future in God already exist within us in the present, though on a smaller scale and in a more limited sense. Thus, we are merely believing in the larger version of a fact within the larger God, in whom we must believe after all.

Who can explain how so many countless circles of consciousness <term>circles of consciousness: *Bewußtseinskreise*; the individual domains of subjective experience</term>, tied to just as many countless, intersecting physical spheres of influence <term>spheres of influence: *Wirkungskreise*; the physical areas or systems where an entity’s energy acts</term>, are supposed to coexist without interfering with one another. But even now, the very same spheres of influence that you worry might disturb you in the hereafter already intervene in your life on this side without confusing you; indeed, they themselves contribute to the content and development of your conscious life. What difference does it make if they also carry a consciousness of their own, about which you know nothing, simply because you know nothing of any foreign consciousness at all? <note>Fechner is arguing for panpsychism—the idea that because we cannot "see" the consciousness of other things, we shouldn't assume they don't have it, especially since their physical actions already help form our own minds.</note> And if, in a full concert, a trained ear from the outside is already capable of hearing a single tone from among all the crossing sound waves, how much better should those be distinguished which belong to one's own sub-

<margin>|</margin>
Do you really think that the waves of effect <term>waves of effect: *Wirkungswellen*; Fechner's term for the propagation of physical stimuli or vibrations through the nervous system</term>, which carry the memory of a rose’s image from the eye into your brain, still possess the bounded, physical shape of the rose? And yet, the memory mentally retains the shape of the rose; it is enough that the shape existed at the point of origin. And do you think that the memories, coming separately from a lily and a rose, still exist just as distinctly within you as the rose and lily do outside? It would seem impossible, especially if you perceived them one after another with the same point of vision <term>point of vision: *Augenpuncte*; likely referring to the fovea or the central point of focus in the eye</term>, which sends its waves of effect into the exact same parts of the brain—and yet, a distinct appearance of each still persists.

# The Theoretical Principle.

What other conception could satisfy the requirements of the historical principle <note>The "historical principle" refers to the idea that a theory should align with the long-standing traditions and shared beliefs of humanity throughout history.</note> in such a way? At the same time, it satisfies the practical principle in the same manner as the conception flowing from the "argument from the spirit," by providing a foundation for it. And if spiritual existence in the hereafter has any physical basis at all, it cannot be conceived of in any other way if it is to satisfy both arguments in conjunction.

Finally, the third main tenet of faith enters into this connection of its own accord. Just as every spirit in the afterlife spiritually links many who are living in this world and exerts an influence upon them, it does so physically as well; for everything from that spirit which enters into the living, and continues to move within them, has been carried in by physical effects and is still being carried forward. And all these rays that emanated from Plato into the world remain just as interconnected as they were at their point of origin, much like the wave around a stone thrown into a pond remains interconnected in its furthest propagation, despite all the refractions and reflections it may experience. # The Argument from the Body.

Or at least it is said—even if once again it is not believed—that it is true: an angel leads us on this side <note>original: *diesseits*; referring to our current existence in the physical world</note> on all our paths and finally carries us into the afterlife <note>original: *Jenseits*</note>. However, we must conceive of this as happening inwardly within that being, just as we previously thought of it happening outwardly; for the same holds true for our relationship to God and the afterlife. Furthermore, there is not a different angel for every single person, but rather for every different spiritual community <term>Geistergemeinschaft</term> <note>A collective of spirits or a shared spiritual consciousness that transcends the individual.</note> in heaven. As for the angels who guide each human being in particular—or rather, who guide human society in specific directions—one can seek them in those who participate in this spirit: the spirits of the departed <note>original: *Abgeschiedenen*</note>, by whom one allows oneself to be led.

In this way, everything becomes thinkable that we otherwise would not know how to think; everything harmonizes that we otherwise would not know how to bring together; and all words whose sound we previously only knew how to play with now become true.

---

Let it suffice; for I did not intend here to develop faith itself so much as the motives and arguments for faith; except that the development of every argument for faith naturally turns into a development of the faith itself.

And that is the incomparable, irreplaceable quality of these arguments: that in the foundations of the matter, they simultaneously provide the grounds for the matter’s development. The traditional arguments are easily counted off on one's fin— <note>The original German text cuts off mid-word: *abgezählt* (counted off).</note>

<page-num>220</page-num>

### The Theoretical Principle.

...counted; yet as soon as they are tallied, one is also finished with them.

Moreover, what was said regarding the historical argument applies to all the arguments and their interconnectedness: the universal remains more securely established by this than any individual detail can be established by it. However, the universal is so tightly interconnected that breaking even a small part of this connection means breaking the foundation of the entire faith.

But once this foundation stands as unbreakable, the dispute over details can certainly proceed and begin anew; indeed, it must be so. For faith is different from that first tower <note>A reference to the Tower of Babel (Genesis 11), which failed due to a confusion of languages and lack of cooperation among the builders. The author suggests that while Babel fell due to discord, the "Tower of Faith" is built through it.</note>; only out of the strife and misunderstanding of the builders does it finally rise firmly as a monument of reconciliation.

Then no word needs to be anxiously clung to any longer so that faith might stand; rather, only let every word be rejected that does not rest on the foundation of the three arguments; for through this, the foundation of faith and its eternal progress remain firm of their own accord. This entire book consists only in the assertion of this stronghold of faith <term>Glaubensveste</term> <note>original: *Glaubensveste*; a "fortress of faith" or an unshakeable doctrinal foundation.</note>, and nothing else from it need be retained.

### VIII.

## Position of an Exact Doctrine of Body and Soul (with Respect to the Nerve Question) Regarding Matters of Faith.

What I just said collectively about all arguments also applies particularly to the one we last dealt with—the argument regarding the body. The universal <note>German: *das Allgemeine*; referring to the broad principles of faith established in the preceding chapters.</note> remains more securely established through this than any individual point can be established by it; and many more questions could still be raised from its perspective <note>original: *Gesichtspuncte*</note> without being able to be answered from that same perspective in any way other than through vague possibilities or analogies, which are too isolated to offer certainty. An exact science of the connection between body and soul—I have named it **psychophysics** <term>psychophysics</term> <note>The author, Gustav Fechner, is credited with founding psychophysics, which explores the functional relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensations.</note>—will first have to become more firmly established and reach a greater stage of development through a more careful and precise determination of the facts <note>original: *Thatsachen*</note> and laws of this con- <note>The text breaks off mid-word: *Zusammenhanges* (connection or relationship).</note>

<summary>This page introduces the concept of "psychophysics" as a scientific framework for understanding the relationship between the physical body and the soul, particularly in how it relates to religious faith.</summary>

# Position of an Exact Doctrine of Body and Soul

-nection <note>Completing the word "connection" from the previous page.</note> in our small body and life than is currently the case, in order to be able to offer more definite points of reference and developmental factors for the argument of the physical body regarding this connection in a larger and transcendent body and life. But surely the time will come when one will know how to subordinate the small and the large—though they be most diverse in form—within this field to common viewpoints and laws. This would be just as has been the case in the purely physical realm <note>German: *Körpergebiete*; referring to the world of inanimate matter and physics.</note> with the fall of the apple and the movement of the earth around the sun, or the ray that shines and the ray that sounds; likewise, one will know how to find the one within the other again.

Now, however, it is strange that those who did not even have an inkling of this most general requirement, nor of the first principles of such a doctrine, attempt to prescribe its limits. Where they no longer perceive the nerves of the small human being—that quite limited form in which the vibrations of the body provide a foundation for a spirit—they believe there can also be no talk of God, the afterlife, or other souls.

How palpable the fallacy is which concludes the absence of the soul from the absence of nerves, I have already shown in more than one place*). But as one says of nature: **"You may drive it out with a pitchfork, yet it will always return,"** <note>original: *furca expellas, usque recurret*; a quote from the Roman poet Horace meaning that natural tendencies or truths cannot be suppressed by force.</note>

...so one can say of this conclusion. I let the pitchfork <note>Referring to the quote from Horace on the previous page regarding the futility of trying to suppress nature or truth.</note> rest. In earlier times, it was considered an **axiom** <note>A self-evident principle or truth that requires no proof.</note> that celestial bodies could only move in circles; the same role currently played by this axiom will one day be played by the axiom that movements of the soul can only occur on the basis of movements of the nerves.

And even if nerves were necessary, they are certainly not lacking in the shared foundation of the divine and the transcendent world. Rather, instead of a single system, we are presented with a system of nerve-systems, bound into a narrower system by vibrations that carry gaze and word from one to another—and by even more than these—bound into the greatest system by those vibrations that run between all suns. # Position of an Exact Doctrine of Body and Soul. 224

...despite all conflict in words, they so willingly encounter the most spiritually restrictive <note>original: *geistbeschränkendsten*; referring to a worldview that limits or "constricts" the presence of spirit or mind in the universe, typically associated with materialism.</note> worldview and, therefore, also its support.

Compare this with the so natural conception that Plato, in one of his dialogues (*Philebus*), puts into the mouth of Socrates\*).

**Socrates:** "That which belongs to the nature of the bodies of all living things—earth, fire, water, and also air—we find also in the composition of the Whole; of each of these, we find in ourselves only very little and of poor quality, and nowhere is anything pure or fully corresponding to the powers peculiar to its nature. For example, fire exists in us and in the Whole. But in us, there is only a little, weak, and poor fire; but that in the Whole is wonderful, abundant, and beautiful, and possesses the full power inherent in fire. The fire within us, however, is nourished, governed, and arises from the fire of the Whole. The same must be said of the earth in living beings and of the earth found in the Whole, as well as of water and air. All that has just been mentioned, when we see it joined into one in man, we call a **body** <note>original: *Leib*</note>. In exactly the same way, we can call the world, which is composed of these same components, a body. Now, will our body be nourished by this universal body, or will that universal body be nourished by ours?" — **Protarchus:** <note>A student of the sophists and a character in Plato's *Philebus* dialogue.</note> "This is not even worth the question." — **Socrates:** "But does our body not have a **soul** <note>original: *Seele*</note>? From where, then, should it have received it, if not also the [Whole...]"

Otherwise, **Materialism**, which rejects faith, would be correct. Against this, there are other views that attempt to support faith from the physical side, which are simultaneously psychophysically impossible and make it impossible to satisfy the historical and practical requirements of faith.

# The Position of an Exact Doctrine of Body and Soul

There is that other view, no less frequently championed, that an **ethereal body** composed of imponderable substances <note>In 19th-century science, "imponderable substances" referred to things like light, heat, and "ether"—phenomena that seemed to lack physical weight but filled space.</note> is enclosed within our current, coarser body as if in a casing, and that it frees itself at death while essentially preserving its organization. But it contradicts both <term>psychophysics</term> <note>The scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensations.</note> and physics to imagine an organic body composed of imponderable substances existing on its own; it is a demand even greater than expecting liquid water to keep its old shape after the glass is broken. And it is even more far-fetched to expect it to build a new body without the help of the old, **ponderable** <note>original: "wägbaren." Matter that has measurable weight, as opposed to the "imponderable" ether.</note> body. Where has anyone ever seen anything, except perhaps in dreams, that would justify such possibilities? This concept, too, only strips the spirit of the means to relate to the outer world, instead of expanding those means.

Once again, this is the opposite of our faith, according to which our future body will be a system of both the ponderable and imponderable—grown out of the whole of our current one—only larger. In this view, nothing prevents us from thinking of the **soul’s vibrations** <note>Fechner conceptualized mental activity as physical vibrations (Schwingungen) occurring within a unified system.</note> as being linked primarily to the vibrations of the imponderable, just as they are now; the only difference is that an organization of the imponderable is unthinkable and impossible to achieve, here or there, without the organization of the ponderable.

Broadly speaking, within our profound ignorance of the fundamental relationships between **body and soul**, there still lies hidden a vast treasure which the future must unearth—

## IX.

### Questions concerning how faith first came to humanity, and how the motives and reasons for belief in the existence of God are rooted in the existence of God.

Following this, a few more words on these questions, which we have set aside until now, although they have pressed upon us from the beginning. We set them aside with the advantage that we can now encounter them with greater clarity than at the start—with greater clarity, even if it is far from complete. For **opinion**, **faith**, and **knowledge** <note>In German philosophy, these represent a hierarchy of certainty: *Meinen* (opinion/supposition), *Glauben* (faith/belief), and *Wissen* (certain knowledge).</note> still often run into one another here, especially regarding the first question; let us only ensure that they do not run away from each other.

Regarding the first question, it is undoubtedly most natural to think that belief in God was generated from the beginning merely through the effect of the **theoretical** and **practical** motives <note>The "theoretical motive" refers to the intellectual need to explain the world's origins, while the "practical motive" refers to the human moral and emotional need for a higher power.</note>, and that once generated, it was then historically propagated. In this way, the **historical motive** would have origin—

# Questions on how faith first came to humanity, etc.

only the second place. From another perspective, if the **historical motive** <note>The "historical motive" refers to the way faith is passed down through generations and tradition.</note> is now the first thing that leads every human being to believe in God, should it not also have been the first from the very beginning? Just as parents today plant faith in God into their children before the **theoretical** and **practical** motives <note>The theoretical motive is the intellectual search for a First Cause; the practical motive is the human heart's longing for support and justice.</note> can find a point of attack in them, might God, as the Father of humanity, have also first communicated faith in Him to them in an immediate way? Thus, the historical principle would have taken its starting point from itself; the other principles, however, would have merely continued its achievement by working toward the preservation and development of the faith directly implanted in humanity by God.

But I believe: just as the three principles are not permitted to struggle for precedence now, they achieve—and can only achieve—what they do through their interconnection and their support of one another; so it must have been from the beginning. Rather, the beginning itself must have been the first and tightest knot of that entanglement in which we perceive them today. And if we are permitted to regard God as the Father of humanity—which we may indeed do, with the only difference being that God generates and gives birth to His children *within* Himself instead of *out of* Himself <note>This reflects Fechner’s panentheistic view: the idea that the world and humanity exist inside of God, rather than being separate creations.</note>—then we can only compare the way faith in God first came to humanity with the way faith in parents first comes to children from the parents themselves; not like the

# Questions on how faith first came to humanity, etc.

faith in God first comes from the parents to the children; and the production of faith in God by God Himself will also be much more an internal than an external process.

But I think of it this way:

Undisputedly, the nature surrounding human beings was, from the beginning, suited to make them recognize a power above their own. As long as humans did not yet think of their own spirit as separate from the body *in the abstract* <note>original: "*in abstracto*". This refers to the philosophical act of separating the mind or soul from the physical body, a concept Fechner believes early humans did not yet possess.</note>—since initially they did not distinguish between the two at all—there was also no occasion to abstract the spirit from nature. They could not have considered the sun, which moves across the sky, to be less alive than themselves, who walk upon the earth; they must have considered it only more powerful, more sublime, and more brilliant. After all, they could not brighten the day, open the flowers, or ripen the fruits as the sun did. Why should they have considered thunder to be something weaker and deader than their own voice? Or the storm something weaker and less alive than their own breath? Or the ebb and flow of the sea something more mechanical than their own pulse? What is so familiar to us now—once the abstraction of the spirit from nature and the powers of the spirit from the powers of nature has occurred—was entirely impossible before that happened. It was equally impossible for humanity to have begun with such an abstraction. Naturally, however, need must have driven humans to enter into a suitable understanding <note>The German *Vernehmen* suggests a mutual agreement or a sense of "hearing and responding" to these powers.</note> with the power or powers under whose influence they found and felt themselves—

<summary>Fechner argues that early humans naturally perceived nature as a living, divine entity because they lacked the modern "abstraction" that separates spirit from matter. To early humanity, natural phenomena like the sun and storm were not mechanical forces but grander versions of their own living breath and voice.</summary>

to establish, and the analogy of how he must behave toward Him <note>Referring to the relationship between the human being and the divine power recognized in nature.</note> in return, guided him. All natural religion <term>Naturreligion: a system of belief based on the observation of nature and reason rather than supernatural revelation</term> possesses this character. But now, if God truly dwells and lives and works in nature, and humans "live, move, and have their being" <note>original: "leben, weben, ſind". A direct reference to the biblical verse Acts 17:28, often used by Fechner to describe panentheism—the idea that the world exists within God.</note> in God; then this immediate way in which the existence of God and His highest essences first impressed itself upon the feeling or consciousness of humanity was also God’s first natural language to humans, the understanding of which is simply no longer familiar to us today. Thus, the historical motive, by seeking its beginning in a primal revelation <term>Uroffenbarung: the idea that God revealed Himself to humanity directly at the very beginning of history</term> of God, cannot initially be separated at all from the theoretical and practical motives. For God’s existence itself has its theoretical and practical sides, and in the primal effect upon both sides of the beings created within Him lay precisely the primal revelation of God, from which all historical propagation of faith originated, and which preceded all our theoretical and practical motives as a germ.

In the same way, a child first recognizes its parents through immediate observation of their behavior toward it, and through practical relationships that assert themselves automatically, for what they are to the child. <header>Questions regarding how faith first came to humanity,</header>

Smiles and threats: the infant understands everything correctly from its true parents; and at the start, they speak no other language with the child; only later does the spoken word take their place. God, through that by which He transcends His creatures, stood no less audibly and visibly before the first humans, and His power and practical relationships to them were no less palpable to them. In those days, there was no doctrine yet that placed God high above the heavens <note>The author is critiquing the idea of a "transcendent" God who is distant from the world, arguing instead that early humans felt God's presence as something immediate and close.</note> or allowed the world to fall away from God; humanity could still believe in the Living One, whom they saw before them and around them, and who acted upon them. Even without yet knowing the name of "God," they could react to Him, carrying relationships of consciousness <term>Bewußtseinsbeziehungen: the internal, psychological, and spiritual connections a person feels toward a higher power or another being</term> and feelings toward Him, just as it lies in the nature of the newborn child toward its parents. All of this could only develop further as humanity came of age.

For just as the newborn child’s feeling toward its parents is from the very beginning the most intimate, unified, and consistent for all children—yet simultaneously the most undeveloped—the feeling of human relations to God, of being and living in and with God, may have carried the same character from the start. Of course, who could truly empathize with it now, or describe how it was? However, as development began, so too did the decomposition, error, and confusion from which humanity is only gradually recovering.

...that is, one who had just surpassed the stage of the ape, but rather came to conscious development within humanity very slowly; even in the Negro <note>The author uses the term "Neger" (Negro), reflecting the racial hierarchies and Eurocentric anthropological views prevalent in the 19th century, which often erroneously placed non-European peoples on a "lower" developmental scale.</note>, it still stands at a low level. Finally, in a last exertion—we call it the last earth revolution <note>The "earth revolution" (Erdrevolution) refers to the theory of Catastrophism, popular before Darwinism, which suggested that Earth's history was defined by sudden, violent shifts that paved the way for new forms of life.</note>—it brought forth the creation of man from that same, only more powerfully swelling, universal fount <note>original: "Schöpfungsborne"</note> from which the first creation occurred. And just as man visibly stands in a relationship of purpose to the entire earth, so too the forces of the whole earthly realm would have joined together anew for his creation, rather than merely raising the ape to a human through gradual refinement.

Under this premise, the first human, by virtue of his different origin—the fresh origin from the great universal fount of creation—could also have possessed many superior physical and spiritual qualities than subsequent humans: closer and more intimate relationships of knowledge and feeling, particularly toward nature and God. For everything that is "first" has a more direct relationship to its source than anything that follows as an imitation; just as a son has closer knowledge of his father than a grandson does.

<header>and how the motives and reasons for faith, etc.</header>

From a strictly exact viewpoint, however, one view is as improbable—one might almost say as impossible—as the other. And yet, one must believe in one of these two impossibilities; it is an example of how faith can compel knowledge.

In **Darwin’s view**, a significance that exceeds all measure of experience must be attributed to the slowly transforming forces and the slow capacity of an organism to adapt; in the **opposing view**, an altogether unknown power must be assigned to the revolutions of the earth, and revolutions must be assumed that are entirely incomparable to current changes. The former view can connect to the present and the known from all sides; it can fill entire volumes with a web of inductive threads <note>The author uses "inductive threads" (inductiver Fäden) to describe the process of building a broad scientific theory by connecting many small, observed facts.</note>; yet, no threads reach as far as they are intended to reach, the weaving soon loses itself within its own patterns, and the enormous heap of evidence fails to establish even the meagerest proof.

The other view can connect to nothing in the present, nothing known; it can only rest, firstly, on the no-less-valuable certainty that the unknown forces it requires must have acted a *first time*, even according to that other view which denies their repeated action. Secondly, it rests on the probability that upheavals—revolutions of a nature not truly recognized in themselves—actually and repeatedly affected the whole earthly realm in connection or on a larger scale...

Thirdly, it rests on the consideration that the **teleological** <term>teleological: the idea that things in nature have a purpose or a specific end-goal, rather than happening by random chance</term> connection, the architectural structure, and the **periodicity** <note>The author refers to the way biological processes (like heartbeats or seasons) happen in regular cycles or "periods."</note> so characteristic of organisms makes the earthly realm truly comparable to an **organism**. Even every new conception and birth of a human being—indeed, even every emergence of a new tooth—occurs through forces that lie dormant in the ordinary course of things, awakening from time to time to stir the entire organism anew; it is not achieved by "repairing" an old child or an old tooth. These occur through unexplained forces; one is not supposed to believe in them, yet the facts force one to believe; however, they return only after long intervals. Thus, such forces may return to the Earth after even longer intervals than they do to its individual members; we, however, live in these "intervals." It is just that this heap of **analogies** can mean as little to an exact science as **Darwin's** heap of **inductions**, which in a certain sense let the "mouse give birth to the mountain," while our analogies, in another sense, let the "mountain give birth to the mouse." <note>The author is playing with the classical Latin proverb *parturient montes, nascetur ridiculus mus* (the mountains are in labor and a ridiculous mouse is born). The second question—how the three motives and reasons for the existence of God are rooted in God's own existence—is, if we are certain that we have our existence in God and not outside of Him, a question that subordinates itself to a more general one: how our entire mental life is rooted in the divine. Above all, the question arises: how can there even be a need for special mediations for faith in God within God Himself, and how is doubt about the existence of God possible within God Himself? Such doubt frequently exists in humans, and thus exists in God, if He includes humanity within Himself; does anyone doubt their own existence? Indeed, could that not even justify a doubt about the fact that God holds us within Himself?

Undisputably, no one doubts their own existence, and therefore neither does God; we, however, are not God just because we are in God. Unlike the way God includes us, we do not ourselves include whole beings with an **I** <term>the "I" (German: *Ich*): the conscious self or ego</term>—beings capable of the thought of a higher "I" and thus capable of doubting it. Rather, we are the first level of such beings, and for everything, there must be a first...

# X.

## The Orthodox and Free Point of View.

->I wish to make an open confession.<-

As free as the standpoint is that I represent in this work, and have represented in earlier writings, the orthodox point of view—wherever I have encountered it elsewhere—has nevertheless pleased me more than the "free" one, if not in every instance, then at least on average. This collectively firm adherence of faith to the word of the Bible—even if it meant including Noah’s Ark with the entire modern animal kingdom and the sun standing still on the day of the fall of Jericho—has pleased me more than the most rational, corrosive <term>The German word *zersetzende* (decomposing) refers to a type of biblical criticism that Fechner felt dissolved the spiritual essence of faith through over-analysis</term> criticism. To me, the "New Catholic" <note>The *Deutschkatholiken* were a 19th-century liberal movement that sought independence from the Pope and simplified church dogmas</note> and "free congregations" <note>Rationalist religious groups in Germany that moved away from traditional Christian doctrines in favor of humanistic values</note> have always seemed like herds that are glad to be rid of the sheepdog or even the shepherd, and thus fall prey to the wolf; at any rate, they remain a herd only so long as there is enough grass on the same meadow to—

...to hold together. But do I not thereby involuntarily pass judgment upon my own point of view?

It may seem so; however, when I consider how this comes to be, the matter is as follows.

Religion at the end of days, as I envision it, will generate the firmest faith that can possibly exist by providing the most perfect satisfaction of the three principles <note>Fechner refers to his three criteria for religious truth: the historical (tradition), the practical (moral and social utility), and the theoretical (reason and logic)</note>. The universality and unanimity with which it will be regarded as the best and truest—because it truly is so—will allow no doubt to arise within the individual from either a historical or practical perspective. By simultaneously satisfying reason in everything that reason demands—and in doing so, surpassing every individual reason, for no single mind could have produced this all-encompassing fulfillment of the three principles—it will naturally bring about the subordination of the reasoning mind (which recognizes this) to the historically established faith and the authority of its sources. A wonderful blessing will rest upon this firmness of faith, upon this subordination of individual reason to the final result of the workings of divine reason in history—a subordination that lifts one above all wavering in the highest and most ultimate matters. Now, when I see that some already enjoy this blessing to such an extent, and manifest it in their disposition and way of acting, as far as it is possible in the current time—

## The Orthodox and Free Point of View.

But if this is the case, one might certainly ask: is it not preferable to take things exactly as they are? To acknowledge both the goodness of the foundation and the deficiencies in the structural expansion of religion? To believe everything in its sources that is good and believable, and to emancipate oneself from belief in the rest? ...contradictions in the Bible <note>The word "Widersprüche" (contradictions) began on the previous page.</note>—instead of hiding or sugarcoating them—to look them clearly and openly in the eye and to explain them; for contradictions do indeed exist within it. In short, this approach seeks to use the best powers of **reason** to separate what is correct from what is incorrect, and what is beneficial from what is indifferent or deficient.

I myself stand entirely upon this point of view; yet, when I look around at what commonly results from such a treatment of the sources of faith, I cannot bring myself to say simply that it is preferable. What I see is the total destruction of that blessing of faith which lies in the shared community and firmness of belief—a firmness which is the primary condition for faith to provide any blessing at all. Furthermore, I see how little the common people have become better, more content, happier, or wiser because we have begun to feed them the "mother’s milk of reason" <note>Fechner uses this metaphor to describe the Enlightenment-era attempt to make rationalism the foundational "nourishment" of the public mind.</note> in these matters. Religion was intended to offer the individual’s reason the highest, surest, and firmest viewpoints; now, however, the individual’s reason is tasked with regulating, improving, judging, and sifting through religion. This is turning the matter upside down. Instead of the definitive unity we ought to demand from religion, we now add confusion and strife over religion itself to all our other existing causes of discord. In this process, the essence of religion is easily lost altogether.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s reflection on the conflict between traditional orthodoxy and the modern rationalist or "free" approach to religion. <page-num>246</page-num>
<header>The Orthodox and Free Point of View.</header>

And why, then, do I not adopt the position of unconditional belief in that which has been handed down through history? It is because I cannot, and hundreds of thousands of others cannot; the theoretical principle <note>Fechner refers here to the "theoretical principle" of scientific and logical inquiry which demands evidence and consistency.</note> also asserts its claims, and it is right that it should do so. While unconditional faith in established tradition offers irreplaceable advantages to those who possess it, it is impossible for everyone to share it if it requires reason to be sacrificed at every turn. 

Therefore, a new task emerges in history: the task of ensuring that those benefits—which traditionalists currently hold only in an exceptional and imperfect degree (since they mistake an incomplete religion for a finished one)—might one day become the common property of all and reach their full potential. This can only happen by actively guiding religion toward its ultimate completion. We must eventually reach a point where reason, instead of being asked to make impossible sacrifices for the sake of faith, finally finds full satisfaction and contributes to supporting the very faith it currently seeks to undermine. 

To achieve this, history certainly requires the introduction of "free" points of view—the efforts of a reason unbound by rigid dogmas—and its shaking of foundations that must eventually fall. This process demands immense diversity, mutual conflict, and even the failure of most of these efforts, so that after the exhaustion...

<summary>Fechner argues that while traditional faith is stable, modern reason must be allowed to critique religion to eventually move it toward a more "complete" state where faith and logic can coexist. He suggests that the current conflict and the failure of various rationalist movements are necessary steps in this historical evolution.</summary>
<keywords>reason vs. faith, religious evolution, dogma, theoretical principle, historical tradition, completion of religion</keywords>

<page-num>247</page-num>
<header>The Orthodox and Free Point of View.</header>

...and the removal of all false paths, the right one might finally remain firm and secure. All of this, however, should find no place in schools and churches; for the people — I trust I may be spared the explanation of what I mean by that term <note>Fechner likely refers to the general public or "the masses" who prioritize social and spiritual stability over the rigors of theological or scientific debate.</note> — are not there to participate in attempts to improve faith, but to enjoy its benefits insofar as they exist. Furthermore, the progress of faith is not meant to proceed from schools and churches, but only to eventually penetrate into them; and it will never do so without a revolution that questions and strikes at more than just faith alone. 

And how little will finally remain of all those rational efforts from free standpoints that run counter to existing faith and to one another; how little of that faith will ultimately have fallen. Rather, most of that which the reason <term>Vernunft: the faculty of logical and universal principles, often contrasted in this era with "Understanding" or simple "Unreason."</term> of the masses—acting out of unreason—has shaken will stand all the firmer; and this shaking itself will have contributed to finally establishing it securely.

Thus, a constant conflict exists between the demands and the benefits of both points of view. How is it to be resolved <note>original: "heben," which means to lift, remove, or resolve a conflict.</note> in these times? It cannot be resolved at all until the completion of religion has historically resolved it of its own accord; and the fact that it has not yet been resolved proves in itself that religion is not yet complete. Until then, it will be good that both the orthodox and 

<vocab>Faith, Reason, Point of View, Revolution, Religion, Conflict, Demands</vocab>

<summary>Fechner argues that while rational critiques of religion are necessary for progress, they belong in the realm of philosophy rather than in schools or churches, which serve to provide the practical benefits of faith to the public. He suggests that the ongoing conflict between tradition and reason is proof that religion has not yet reached its final, perfected historical form.</summary>
<keywords>faith vs. reason, religious evolution, institutional stability, social function of the church, historical progress of religion</keywords>

### The Orthodox and Free Point of View.

...that the free point of view exists, and one will have to accept with humility <note>original: "bescheiden," suggesting a modest recognition of reality or resignation to a fact</note> that its advantages cannot be had without its disadvantages; yet, according to the historical-practical principle <term>historisch-praktischen Princip: a philosophical method that evaluates the truth or value of an idea based on its historical development and its practical effects on human society</term>, these must ultimately lead to the perfection of faith.

---

**Vocabulary:** point of view, principle, of faith, orthodox, free

<summary>Fechner concludes that the challenges posed by rationalist critiques of religion are inseparable from their benefits, serving as a necessary historical process to refine and perfect faith. He argues that one must accept the friction of free inquiry as the only path toward a more mature religious understanding.</summary>

# XI.

## Retrospect, Overview, Prospect.

---

Looking back, one will find that in the establishment, discussion, and pursuit of the historical and practical principle <term>historical and practical principle: Fechner’s method of validating religious beliefs based on their historical persistence and their practical necessity for human morality and happiness</term> of faith, there has been no significant departure beyond the current standpoint of Christendom—so far as one can speak of a general standpoint of faith at all. For although some things here may be stated or arranged differently than usual, or the arguments of faith themselves reframed, one will essentially find nothing in the substance other than what anyone standing on the ground of today's faith—whether **Orthodox** <note>Those adhering to traditional, established church doctrines</note> or **Rationalist** <note>Those who believe religious claims should be held to the standards of human reason</note>, **Catholic** or **Protestant**—would wish to see stated as often as possible, arranged as adequately as possible, and justified from as many sides as possible, provided they desire a religious faith at all. I make an exception for certain things that might not appeal to specific individuals or particular factions <note>original: "Fractionen," referring to the various schools of thought or denominational sub-groups</note> of the current standpoint.

<summary>Fechner reflects on his philosophical defense of faith, arguing that his "historical-practical" approach aligns with the core interests of all Christians, whether they favor tradition or reason. He suggests his work provides a universal justification for religious belief that transcends denominational divides.</summary>

<keywords>philosophy of religion, Christendom, orthodoxy, rationalism, historical-practical principle, denominationalism, 19th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition in Chapter XI, where Fechner moves from discussing the "historical and practical principles" of faith to his more controversial "theoretical principle." He addresses how this work builds upon his previous philosophical publications regarding the justification of religious belief.</meta>

## Retrospect, Overview, Prospect.

The situation is quite different, however, regarding the third principle, the **theoretical principle of faith**, in whose establishment, discussion, and pursuit I have ventured so far beyond the previous standpoint <note>Standpoint (Standpunct) refers to the prevailing theological and philosophical views of the mid-19th century</note> that those who have grown old in that old way of thinking will not easily be able or willing to follow; and the schools always follow the lead of the masters. This work moves beyond the old view in the same spirit as my earlier writings, but it goes further than those previous works in that the hints I once gave regarding the position of this principle in relation to the other two have now been brought to fulfillment. This was achieved through their actual juxtaposition and the way they all provide mutual support for one another.

Does it seem as if the theoretical is being favored here? I have declared often enough: no primary weight is placed upon it; the primary weight lies in the support that faith finds collectively across all three. However, the theoretical principle has hitherto been the most misunderstood, rejected, and fragmented; it is from this side that faith required—and still requires—the most assistance. After all, this is what I intended to demonstrate here.

1) Faith is not capable of arising and persisting solely on the foundation of the <term>historical and practical principles: The validation of faith through tradition (historical) and its moral/psychological necessity (practical)</term>; rather, it requires the aid of the <term>theoretical principle: The rational or metaphysical framework that makes faith intellectually plausible</term>. Yet, the latter is just as insufficient on its own—

<summary>Fechner introduces his "theoretical principle" of faith, arguing that while it is not more important than historical or practical foundations, it is currently the most neglected and necessary for a complete religious worldview.</summary>
<keywords>theoretical principle, historical principle, practical principle, religious philosophy, Gustav Fechner, justification of faith</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 250, concluding the thought that the theoretical principle does not function in isolation but works alongside the historical and practical principles of faith.</meta>

## Retrospect, Overview, Prospect.

...in matters of faith, but only in cooperation and mutual completion with those two.

2) From time immemorial, it has acted within this connection as a **motive**; however, in order to act as a **ground** <note>Fechner distinguishes between a *motive* (a psychological drive that makes one want to believe) and a *ground* (a logical or objective justification that proves why one should believe)</note>, while avoiding the two opposite errors of its effect as a motive—which I have previously discussed—it must be positioned as has been shown. That is to say: one must proceed from the totality of what we know of existence, rather than immediately transferring what we know of finite, limited <term>circles of existence (Daseinskreise): Fechner’s signature concept of nested levels of reality, where smaller systems (like the soul) are contained within larger, higher systems (like the world-spirit)</term> to the infinite and eternal in which we are to believe. Instead, we must follow the same direction in which our knowledge already leads—from narrower and lower circles of existence to wider and higher ones—expanding and elevating ourselves beyond them in such a way that we arrive at conceptions of the most general, highest, and final circles and modes of existence. These conceptions then meet the conclusions and requirements of the other two principles in the most favorable manner. By this means, we compensate, on the one hand, for the uncertainty of overstepping the <term>circle of experience (Erfahrungskreis): The limits of what can be scientifically or empirically proven through the senses</term>, which remains an issue from the side of knowledge; and, on the other hand, we provide the historically and practically supported faith with the very foundation it still requires from the side of knowledge.

3) Along this path, the fundamental points of faith in the highest and last things emerge, which are represented by this writing, including those which the...

<summary>Fechner explains that his theoretical principle of faith works by expanding our existing knowledge of the physical world into higher, "infinite" circles of existence, thereby providing a logical foundation for historical and practical religious beliefs.</summary>

<keywords>theoretical principle, circles of existence, justification of faith, epistemology, motive vs ground, infinity, transcendence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous discussion regarding the principles of faith, focusing on how a new theoretical framework can reconcile historical and practical needs.</meta>

## Retrospect, Overview, Prospect.

...[points as] necessary [to] transcend the current standpoint. For there is no other way to satisfy the consequences and requirements of all three <term>principles (Principe): the theoretical, historical, and practical grounds for belief</term> in connection and harmony, except by means of these points.

4) However, the previous standpoint of faith is only surpassed in so far as it simultaneously establishes itself more firmly in its most essential aspects; and the most contradictory views that have asserted themselves historically are unified as well as their own contradictions allow. The most significant and mysterious words of the Bible are clarified by this and find a more literal interpretation than that offered by the literalists <note>original: "Wortgläubigen" — those who adhere strictly to the letter of the scripture</note> themselves.

But if this is so, then—according to the general principles of this work itself—those points which are the condition for reconciling all three principles of faith will, in the context in which they exist, finally break through historically. In doing so, they will not so much overthrow the previous standpoint as elevate <note>The original German "steigern" suggests an upward development or intensification, rather than a mere replacement</note> it.

They will do so; of this I am calm and certain.

And what does it matter that they do not do so all at once and suddenly! The one-sidedness and contradictions of existing views are also historically deep-rooted, and the efforts of a little human being brooding at a study desk are too weak a lever to uproot all at once a world-view <term>world-view (Weltansicht): a comprehensive philosophical outlook; Fechner often uses this to contrast his "Day-view" (living universe) against the "Night-view" (dead matter)</term> that has [prevailed for] almost a couple of cen— <note>The text breaks off mid-word "Jahr-", likely "Jahrhunderte" (centuries).</note>

<summary>Fechner argues that his philosophical framework does not destroy traditional faith but rather strengthens and elevates it by harmonizing conflicting historical and practical views. He acknowledges that such a significant shift in world-view is a slow historical process that cannot be forced by a single individual.</summary>

<keywords>reconciliation of faith, biblical interpretation, world-view, historical progress, principles of faith, literalism</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence begun on page 252, concluding the author’s reflection on the difficulty of shifting a deeply-rooted national worldview. It uses botanical and mechanical metaphors to describe the transition from old contradictions to a new spiritual "flight."</meta>

## Retrospect, Overview, Prospect.

...[that has] sent out its roots by the thousands into these instances of one-sidedness and contradictions, driving the firmest of them into the poetry and thought <note>original: "Dichten und Denken" — a classic German pairing referring to the creative and philosophical output of a culture; Germany is often called the "Land der Dichter und Denker" (Land of Poets and Thinkers)</note> of the poets and thinkers themselves, and permeating the consciousness of the entire people with its offshoots. Yet, these are the starting points for a lever, and quiet brooding precedes the flight and song of a new bird; one must not expect it to happen all at once.

## XII.
### C o n c l u s i o n.

---

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->Because God lives, I live;<-
->For He alone possesses life;<-
->I cannot stand apart from it;<-
->He cannot let go of me.<-

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->You say that it passes away;<-
->But I carry no such worries;<-
->For that which now exists in Him<-
->Is sheltered forevermore.<-

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->The entire course of my life<-
->Is preserved <term>aufgehoben</term> in Him;<- <note>original: "aufgehoben" — a complex German philosophical term meaning simultaneously to cancel, to preserve, and to lift up to a higher level. Here, it suggests the individual life is both finished and eternally saved within God.</note>
->And one day He shall lift<-
->The whole soul to the heights above.<-

---

<summary>This final page concludes the manuscript with a three-stanza poem reflecting on the immortality of the soul and its inseparable connection to the divine. It serves as a spiritual resolution to the philosophical tensions explored throughout the preceding chapters.</summary>
<keywords>God, soul, immortality, rest, preservation, divine union, conclusion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues and completes the poetic "Conclusion" (Schluß) initiated on the previous page. While the preceding verses focused on the soul's existence within God's eternal nature, these final five stanzas shift toward the soul’s experience of divine light, the angelic realm, the Christian virtues of faith, hope, and love, and the ultimate promise of salvation.</meta>

### Conclusion.

---

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->The soul sees Him not;<-
->There, to reveal God the Lord,<-
->The witnesses descend,<-
->With Christ leading as the light.<-

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->The entire host of angels<-
->In His pure heights<-
->I see walking, radiant with light,<-
->And one even carries me along.<-

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->He is the bond that joins all souls;<-
->For faith, love, and hope<-
->The soul first became open,<-
->Once it truly recognized Him.<-

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->He holds within Himself the counsel<-
->Of truth, beauty, and goodness,<-
->So that there may be unity in the spirit <note>original: "Gemüthe" — a term for the inner soul, heart, or emotional mind.</note><-
->And a goal for every deed.<-

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->What can the small part <note>A reference to the individual person as a "part" of the divine whole.</note> accomplish?<-
->How far away is that for which I long!<-
->Soul, do not be afraid;<-
->Salvation <term>Heil</term> <note>original: "Heil" — carries the dual meaning of "salvation" in a religious sense and "wholeness" or "well-being."</note> comes to meet you.<-

---

<summary>This final page of the manuscript concludes the work with a five-stanza poem emphasizing the soul's peace in God and the presence of Christ and angels. It resolves the text's philosophical inquiries by pointing toward a future of spiritual unity and divine salvation.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the poetic "Conclusion" (Schluß). While the previous stanzas focused on the soul's vision of angels and the light of Christ, these final four stanzas reflect on the relationship between human will and divine purpose, the nature of sin and redemption, and the ultimate peace found in God as an eternal harbor.</meta>

### Conclusion.

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->God brings the soul to fulfillment within Himself;<-
->His will is my duty <note>original: "Sollen" — a philosophical and theological term referring to moral obligation or what "ought" to be.</note>;<-
->I can will against it;<-
->Yet He carries it through to the end.<-

->In God my soul finds rest.<-
->He who does not sin Himself,<-
->Yet carries along with His child<-
->The child’s sin within Himself,<-
->Leading the soul at last to its duty.<-

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->O comfort in the greatest suffering!<-
->God cannot tolerate it <note>Referring to suffering or sin mentioned in the previous stanza.</note> within Himself,<-
->These are but debts of joy <note>original: "Freudenschulden" — a poetic compound suggesting that earthly trials are merely temporary "debts" that will be repaid with divine joy.</note>;<-
->I wait for my time.<-

->In God my soul finds rest;<-
->Let this be the final word;<-
->Though far from the earthly harbor,<-
->I can still sleep peacefully;<-
->He is my eternal port.<-
